a,\ \square_{a},\ b(a+a)+b,\ b(\square_{a}),\ b(a+\square_{b(a+a)+b})+\square_{b(b)}
.

The following are contexts in
\widetilde{V_{A}}
:

\square,\ a+\square,\ b(\square_{a(b)}+\square)+\square_{b(a+b(a))}
.
Note that there may be several default holes, but exactly one standard hole
\square
.

The following are
non-examples
, to illustrate the requirements:

•

\square_{a}(b)
–
non leaf
default hole is not allowed.

•

b(a+\square_{b(a+\square)})
– default hole with
context
value are not allowed.

•

b(a+\square_{b(a+\square_{a})})
–
nested
default holes are not allowed.

11.1
A Toolbox for Free Forest Algebras

In the remainder of this section we present several useful tools for reasoning about Free Forest Algebras (including our Augmented Algebra).
Note that
\widetilde{H_{A}}
and
\widetilde{V_{A}}
can be viewed as address domains, identically to
\langle H_{A},V_{A}\rangle
above. In particular, we can use
\mathsf{ForestDom}
and
\mathtt{sq\_pos}
in the same manner.

Our first tool is a property of the action
\mathtt{act}\colon\widetilde{V_{A}}\times\widetilde{H_{A}}\rightarrow\widetilde{H_{A}}
that provides a useful characterization for the equality of two contexts.

Lemma 35
.

Consider
C_{1},C_{2}\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
, then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

C_{1}=C_{2}
.

2.

There exist
h_{1}\neq h_{2}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}\cup\widetilde{V_{A}}
such that
C_{1}(h_{1})=C_{2}(h_{1})
and
C_{1}(h_{2})=C_{2}(h_{2})
.

3.

\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{1})=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{2})
and there exists
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
such that
C_{1}(h)=C_{2}(h)
.

Proof.

The implication
(1)\implies(2)
is trivial (indeed, if
C_{1}=C_{2}
then
C_{1}(h)=C_{2}(h)
for every
h
).

(2)\implies(3)
:

Consider
C_{1},C_{2},h_{1},h_{2}
such that

h_{1}\neq h_{2}
and
C_{1}(h_{1})=C_{2}(h_{1}),C_{1}(h_{2})=C_{2}(h_{2})
.
Denote
x_{1}=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{1}),x_{2}=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{2})
, we claim that
x_{1}=x_{2}
. Assume by way of contradiction that
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, we divide into cases according to the ordering between
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
(as elements in
\mathbb{N}^{*}
).
If
x_{1}<x_{2}
, then
C_{1}[x_{1}],C_{2}[x_{1}]\in\mathtt{Contexts}(A)
but
C_{1}[x]=\square\neq C_{2}[x_{1}]
. In particular,
\mathtt{Domain}(\square)=\{0\}\subsetneq\mathtt{Domain}(C_{2}[x_{1}])
.
In addition for every context
C
and
x\leq\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)
we have that
C(h)[x]=C[x](h)
.
Together we have by
\cref
def:context composition that

C_{1}(h_{1})[x_{1}]=C_{1}[x_{1}](h_{1})=h_{1}\neq C_{2}[x_{1}](h_{1})=C_{2}(h_{1})[x_{1}]

where the inequality is because the domain of
C_{2}[x_{1}](h_{1})
strictly contains the domain of
\square(h_{1})=h_{1}
.
This is a contradiction to the assumption
C_{1}(h_{1})=C_{2}(h_{1})
. The case
x_{2}<x_{1}
is dual.

Otherwise,
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are incomparable. Then, intuitively, plugging
h_{1}
at
x_{1}
does not affect the subforest rooted at
x_{2}
, and vice-versa. Formally, for every
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
we have

C_{2}[x_{2}\mapsto h][x_{1}]=C_{2}[x_{1}]

Together with the assumptions of
(2)
we get

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=C_{1}[x_{1}\mapsto h_{1}][x_{1}]=C_{2}[x_{2}\mapsto h_{1}][x_{1}]=C_{2}[x_{1}]

\displaystyle=C_{2}[x_{2}\mapsto h_{2}][x_{1}]=C_{1}[x_{1}\mapsto h_{2}][x_{1}]=h_{2}

which is a contradiction to the assumption
h_{1}\neq h_{2}
.
We conclude that
x_{1}=x_{2}
as required for
(3)
.

(3)\implies(1)
:

Denote
x=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{1})=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{2})
, and let
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
such that
C_{1}(h)=C_{2}(h)
. We show that
C_{1}=C_{2}
.
First, recall that for every
C,h
we have
C(h)=C[\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)\mapsto h]
.
Denote
\langle d,l\rangle=C_{1}(h)=C_{2}(h)
.
Notice that
\mathtt{Domain}(C_{1})=d\setminus x\mathbb{N}^{+}=\mathtt{Domain}(C_{2})
. Take
y\in d\setminus x\mathbb{N}^{+}
. If
y\in d\setminus x\mathbb{N}^{*}
then
\mathsf{lab}(C_{1})(y)=l(y)=\mathsf{lab}(C_{2})(y)
. Otherwise,
y=x
and
\mathsf{lab}(C_{1})(x)=\square=\mathsf{lab}(C_{2})(x)
.
So overall we get
C_{1}=C_{2}
which implies the required
C_{1}=C_{2}
, concluding
(1)
.
∎

Remark 36

(Two forests are needed to separate contexts)
.

The condition in Item 2 of
\cref
lem:equal contexts characterization may seem redundant, and raises the question of whether equality is already implied when a single
h
satisfies
C_{1}(h)=C_{2}(h)
.
This, however, is not the case. For example, consider:

C_{1}=a+\square,\quad C_{2}=\square+a,\quad h=a

Then
C_{1}(h)=C_{2}(h)
, but
C_{1}\neq C_{2}
as e.g.,
C_{1}(b)=a+b\neq b+a=C_{2}(b)
.
Thus, the condition that equality is maintained under two different
h_{1}\neq h_{2}
is necessary.

Another basic technical tool we require is an ability to manipulate addresses in a way that corresponds to the behavior of
\mathtt{sq\_pos}
under context composition. In the following we define such an operation
\circledast
and establish several properties of it.
Intuitively, one should think of
x\circledast y
as “if we take a context
C
with
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)=x
, and a node at address
y
in some forest
h
, then this node moves to address
x\circledast y
in
C\cdot h
”.

Definition 37
.

We define
\circledast\colon\mathbb{N}^{+}\times\mathbb{N}^{+}\to\mathbb{N}^{+}
as follows: for
x,y\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, write
x=x^{\prime}\cdot\sigma_{x},y=\sigma_{y}\cdot y^{\prime}
where
\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
x\circledast y=x^{\prime}\cdot(\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{y})\cdot y^{\prime}
.

\circledast
is essentially an extension of
\cdot
, in the following sense.

Proposition 38
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
y\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
then
x\cdot y=(x\cdot 0)\circledast y

Proof.

Denote
y=\sigma_{y}\cdot y^{\prime}
, then and we get
(x\cdot 0)\circledast y=x\cdot(0+\sigma_{y})\cdot y^{\prime}=x\cdot\sigma_{y}\cdot y^{\prime}=x\cdot y

∎

We now show that
\circledast
behaves well, algebraically. This also captures the intuition that
x\circledast y
is the address of
y
when plugged into a context at
x
(by essentially identifying
y
with
\mathtt{sq\_pos}
).

Lemma 39
.

\circledast
satisfies the following properties.

1.

\langle\mathbb{N}^{+},\circledast,0\rangle
is a monoid.

2.

\mathtt{sq\_pos}\colon\langle\widetilde{V_{A}},\cdot,\square\rangle\rightarrow\langle\mathbb{N}^{+},\circledast,0\rangle
is a monoid morphism.

Proof.

First we show that
\langle\mathbb{N}^{+},\circledast,0\rangle
is a monoid.

•

\circledast
is associative:
we compute
(\overbrace{x\cdot\sigma_{x}}^{x^{\prime}})\circledast(y\circledast(\overbrace{\sigma_{z}\cdot z}^{z^{\prime}}))
according to whether
|y|>1
:

\displaystyle x^{\prime}\circledast((y^{\prime}\cdot\sigma_{y})\circledast z^{\prime})=x^{\prime}\circledast(y^{\prime}\cdot(\sigma_{y}+\sigma_{z})\cdot z))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x\cdot(\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{y}+\sigma_{z})\cdot z=((x\cdot\sigma_{x})\circledast\overbrace{\sigma_{y}}^{y})\circledast z^{\prime}&y=\sigma_{y}\\
x\cdot(\sigma_{x}+\sigma^{\prime}_{y})\cdot y^{\prime\prime}\cdot(\sigma_{y}+\sigma_{z})\cdot z=((x\cdot\sigma_{z})\circledast(\overbrace{\sigma^{\prime}_{y}\cdot y^{\prime\prime}\cdot\sigma_{y}}^{y}))\circledast z^{\prime}&y=\sigma^{\prime}_{y}\cdot y^{\prime\prime}\cdot\sigma_{y}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}\circledast y)\circledast z^{\prime}

•

0
is unit of
\mathbb{N}^{+}
:
Let some
x\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
then denote
x=\sigma\cdot y
and we get

0\circledast x=(0+\sigma)\cdot y=\sigma\cdot y=x

In order to show that
\mathtt{sq\_pos}
is a monoid morphism, we need to show that
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime}\cdot C)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime})\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)
for every
C,C^{\prime}\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
. The formal proof is by induction below.
Intuitively, however, denote
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime})=x=x^{\prime}\sigma_{x}
and
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)=y=\sigma_{y}y^{\prime}
and consider
C^{\prime}\cdot C
. In
C^{\prime}
, the leaf
\square
at position
x^{\prime}\sigma_{X}
is replaced by all the roots of the forest
C
. Therefore, the branch leading to
\square
in
C^{\prime}\cdot C
, which in
C
is rooted at
\sigma_{y}
, now passes through
x^{\prime}(\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{y})
. Then, this branch proceeds along
y^{\prime}
to reach
\square
. Therefore
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime}\cdot C)=x^{\prime}\cdot(\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{y})\cdot y^{\prime}
, as required.

Before addressing this formally, we make the following basic observations:

•

\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\square)=0

•

\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\sigma(C))=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\sigma(\square))\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

•

\mathtt{sq\_pos}(h+C)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(h+\square)\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

•

\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C+h)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\square+h)\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

We can now show that
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime}\cdot C)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime})\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)
for every
C,C^{\prime}\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
. We show this by induction over the structure of
C^{\prime}
.

•

If
C^{\prime}=\square
then

\displaystyle\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\square\cdot C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)=0\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\square)\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

•

If
C^{\prime}=\sigma(C^{\prime\prime})
then

\displaystyle\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\sigma(C^{\prime\prime})\cdot C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\sigma(\square)\cdot C^{\prime\prime}\cdot C)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\sigma(\square))\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime\prime}\cdot C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\sigma(\square))\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime\prime})\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\sigma(C^{\prime\prime}))\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

•

If
C^{\prime}=h+C^{\prime\prime}
then

\displaystyle\mathtt{sq\_pos}((h+C^{\prime\prime})\cdot C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}((h+\square)\cdot C^{\prime\prime}\cdot C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(h+\square)\circledast(\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime\prime})\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C))

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(h+\square)\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime\prime})\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(h+C^{\prime\prime})\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

•

If
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}+h
then similarly to the above

\displaystyle\mathtt{sq\_pos}((C^{\prime\prime}+h)\cdot C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\square+h)\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime\prime})\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime\prime}+h)\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)

∎

Our next intuitive property is that if we fix
x
, then address shifts with respect to
x
are injective (i.e., address shifts do not cause any “collision” of addresses).

Proposition 40
.

For every
x\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
the functions
y\mapsto x\circledast y
and
y\mapsto y\circledast x
are injective.

Proof.

The proof is based on the following observation: for every
x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, if
x^{\prime}n_{1}y^{\prime}=x^{\prime}n_{2}z^{\prime}
then
n_{1}=n_{2}
and
y^{\prime}=z^{\prime}
.

Consider the function
y\mapsto x\circledast y
.
Denote
x=x^{\prime}\cdot\sigma_{x}
, and let
y=\sigma_{y}\cdot y^{\prime},z=\sigma_{z}\cdot z^{\prime}
such that
x\circledast y=x\circledast z
.
This means that
x^{\prime}(\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{y})y^{\prime}=x^{\prime}(\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{z})z^{\prime}
, yielding by the observation above that
\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{y}=\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{z}
(so
\sigma_{y}=\sigma_{z}
) and
y^{\prime}=z^{\prime}
. Thus,
y=z
, so the function is injective.

The case of
y\mapsto y\circledast x
is symmetric.
∎

Since
\circledast
extends
\cdot
as per
\cref
prop:spa and concat, it also corresponds to a partial order
\leq_{LCRS}
on
\mathbb{N}^{*}
that generalizes the prefix relation. Intuitively, in
\leq_{LCRS}
a node
x
is smaller than its left child
x0
, and
x\cdot i
is smaller than
x\cdot(i+1)
.

Definition 41

(Left-Child Right-Sibling Order)
.

The
Left Child Right Sibling order
(LCRS, for short) is a partial order
\leq_{LCRS}
on
\mathbb{N}^{*}
defined as the transitive closure of the relation
\{(x,x):x\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\}\cup\{(x,x0):x\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\}\cup\{(xi,x(i+1)):x\in\mathbb{N}^{*},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

For example:
1\leq_{LCRS}1\cdot 2
and
1\cdot 2\leq_{LCRS}1\cdot 3
, but
1\cdot 3\not\leq_{LCRS}2
.
Observe that an address domain is simply a
finite
subset
d\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{*}
which is closed under intervals by
\leq_{LCRS}
.

The connection between
\circledast
and
\leq_{LCRS}
is captured in the following.

Proposition 42
.

Consider
x,y\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
then
x\leq_{LCRS}y
if and only if exists
u\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
y=x\circledast u
. Moreover, this
u
is unique if it exists.

Proof.

The first direction follows by induction on the structure of
\leq_{LCRS}
(
\cref
def:lcrs order). Let
x,y
such that
x\leq_{LCRS}y
.

•

Base:

u=\begin{cases}0&y=x\\
00&y=x0\\
1&x=zi\wedge y=z(i+1)\end{cases}

•

Step: if
x\leq_{LCRS}z\leq_{LCRS}y
(
x\neq z\neq y
) then by the induction hypothesis we can write
z=x\circledast u_{z}
and
y=z\circledast u_{y}
, so by the definition of
\circledast
we have
y=x\circledast(u_{z}\circledast u_{y})

The uniqueness of
u
follows from the injectivity in
\cref
prop:spa injective.

For the second direction, assume
y=x\circledast u
. Denote
x=x^{\prime}\cdot\sigma_{x}
and
u=\sigma_{u}\cdot u^{\prime}
, then we get
y=x^{\prime}(\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{u})u^{\prime}
. We now notice that
x\leq_{LCRS}x^{\prime}(\sigma_{x}+\sigma_{u})\leq_{LCRS}y
.
∎

The tools introduced thus far apply to the augmented forest algebra as well as to the standard free forest algebra.
A natural operation unique to the augmented algebra is to replace a default hole
\square_{h}
with the concrete forest
h
. In general, we can take a subforest and replace all its default holes with their concrete values. We call this
unraveling
, as follows.

Definition 43

(Unraveling)
.

Consider
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}\cup\widetilde{V_{A}}
. The
unraveling
of
h
is

\mathtt{unravel}(h)=\begin{cases}h^{\prime}&h=\square_{h^{\prime}}\\
\square&h=\square\\
\sigma(\mathtt{unravel}(h^{\prime}))&h=\sigma(h^{\prime})\\
\mathtt{unravel}(h_{1})+\mathtt{unravel}(h_{2})&h=h_{1}+h_{2}\end{cases}

Note that by definition
\mathtt{unravel}
is a monoid morphism when viewed as either
\mathtt{unravel}\colon\widetilde{H_{A}}\to H_{A}
or
\mathtt{unravel}\colon\widetilde{V_{A}}\to V_{A}
.

We show that unraveling also respects the action of
\widetilde{V_{A}}
on
\widetilde{V_{A}}\cup\widetilde{H_{A}}
.

Lemma 44

(Unraveling Morphism)
.

For every
C\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
and
h\in\widetilde{V_{A}}\cup\widetilde{H_{A}}
we have

\mathtt{unravel}(C\cdot h)=\mathtt{unravel}(C)\cdot\mathtt{unravel}(h)

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
C
, using
\cref
def:unraveling.

•

C=\square
: trivial

•

C=\sigma(C^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\mathtt{unravel}(C\cdot h)

\displaystyle=\sigma(\mathtt{unravel}(C^{\prime}\cdot h))=\sigma(\mathtt{unravel}(C^{\prime})\cdot\mathtt{unravel}(h))

\displaystyle=\sigma(\mathtt{unravel}(C^{\prime}))\cdot\mathtt{unravel}(h)=\mathtt{unravel}(C)\cdot\mathtt{unravel}(h)

•

C=h^{\prime}+C^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\mathtt{unravel}(C\cdot h)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{unravel}(h^{\prime})+\mathtt{unravel}(C^{\prime}\cdot h)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{unravel}(h^{\prime})+\mathtt{unravel}(C)^{\prime}\cdot\mathtt{unravel}(h)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{unravel}(h^{\prime}+C^{\prime})\cdot\mathtt{unravel}(h)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{unravel}(C)\cdot\mathtt{unravel}(h)

•

C=C^{\prime}+h^{\prime}
: analogous to the previous case.

∎

It is often useful to equate a default hole
\square_{h}
with
h
, this can be lifted to an equivalence over forests and contexts, as follows (see
\cref
fig:equiv unravel).

Definition 45

(Unravel-Equivalence)
.

We say that
h_{1},h_{2}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}\cup\widetilde{V_{A}}
are
unravel equivalent
, denoted
h_{1}\approx h_{2}
if
\mathtt{unravel}(h_{1})=\mathtt{unravel}(h_{2})
.

\begin{forest}\approx\begin{forest}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{unravel}}\begin{forest}

(a)

Unravel-equivalent forests.

\begin{split}\begin{forest}&\approx\begin{forest}\\
&\downarrow\!{\mathtt{unravel}}\\
&\begin{forest}\end{split}

(b)

Unravel-equivalent contexts.

Figure 9
:
Unravel equivalent structures, and their corresponding unraveling.

Of particular importance in our decomposition are sets of contexts that are “stable” under unraveling (and also under replacing subforests with default holes). We capture this in the following.

Definition 46

(Stable Context Set)
.

A set of contexts
\widetilde{V}\subseteq\widetilde{V_{A}}
is
stable
if it is a sub-semigroup of
\widetilde{V_{A}}
, and for every
C_{1},C_{2}\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
, if
C_{1}\approx C_{2}
then
C_{1}\in\widetilde{V}\iff C_{2}\in\widetilde{V}
.

Recall that syntactically, we do not allow “nested default holes”, i.e.,
\square_{h}
when
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}\setminus H_{A}
. However, in the following it is convenient to refer to such elements, without changing the syntax. To achieve this, we lift the notion of
\square_{h}
to
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}\setminus H_{A}
by defining
\square_{h}=\square_{\mathtt{unravel}(h)}\in{H_{A}}
.

Remark 47

(
\mathtt{sq\_pos}
and
\mathtt{unravel}
)
.

Consider some
C\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
. The reader may suspect that
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)
is simply
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\mathtt{unravel}(C))
. This, however, is not always the case. For example,
take
C=\square_{a+a}+\square
and notice that
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)=1
while
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\mathtt{unravel}(C))=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(a+a+\square)=2
.
This address change is a nuisance that we encounter in certain proofs, and must account for.

We now lift the notion of morphism to handle
\langle\widetilde{H_{A}},\widetilde{V_{A}}\rangle
as follows.
For a forest morphism
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
where
\alpha\colon H_{A}\to H
and
\beta\colon V_{A}\to V
(for some forest algebra
\langle H,V\rangle
) we define
\langle\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta}\rangle=\langle\alpha\circ\mathtt{unravel},\beta\circ\mathtt{unravel}\rangle
. Since
\mathtt{unravel}
is a monoid morphism, this is a monoid morphism from
\langle\widetilde{H_{A}},\widetilde{V_{A}}\rangle
to
\langle H,V\rangle
.

The following now readily follows from the definition of morphism (since
\widetilde{\beta}
already unravels the given input).

Proposition 48
.

Consider a monoid morphism
\widetilde{\beta}\colon\widetilde{V_{A}}\to V
and a sub-semigroup
S\leq V
, then
\widetilde{\beta}^{-1}(S)
is a stable context set.

12
Binary Decomposition

We can now start describing the first phase of our decomposition scheme, whereby we take a forest and decompose it via a binary tree.
At a glance, this is a tree analogue of taking a word
w
and simply decomposing it recursively into subwords in some way. Crucially, however, in trees there are many more ways to decompose, and more importantly – in order to combine two children into a parent node we need to keep more information than just the two parts: we need to also know
how
they fit together (as discussed in
\cref
fig:problem-decomp).

We remark that this phase of the decomposition is purely syntactic: we take a forest
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
and produce a binary tree that corresponds to its decomposition, regardless of any morphisms involved in the setting. In
\cref
def:general decomposition we generalize the construction to achieve bounded depth, and there we use the morphism.

This section is organized as follows. In
\cref
sec:bin decomp definitions we define a binary decomposition. In
\cref
subsec:references and ancestor embedding we enrich the decomposition with additional structural information that is crucial for our reasoning. Then, in
\cref
subsec:stable and basis we parameterize the definition by sets of contexts and of leaves, which is used later to apply inductive arguments.

12.1
Binary Decomposition Definitions

Before presenting the precise definition, we illustrate a binary decomposition.

Example 49
.

Consider the forest
h=b(a+a)+a(b+a(a+b))
. We wish to decompose it as
b(a+a)+a(
b+a(a+b)
)
.
To this end, we write
h=C\cdot h^{\prime}
where
C=b(a+a)+a(\square)
and
h^{\prime}=b+a(a+b)
. Then, the corresponding binary decomposition is depicted in
\cref
fig:bin decomp example.
To distinguish the depictions of decomposition trees from forests in the algebra, we depict decompositions with horizontal/vertical edges, with
right-child
for the
factor
, and
down-child
for the
residue
.

The decomposition tree is constructed as follows: we start with
h
at the root of the decomposition, which is given address

\epsilon
.
We then “pluck” from
h
the sub-forest factor
h^{\prime}
and place it as the right-child, with address

1
. The node
1
is also assigned a
context
, which is the residue
C
(so that
C\cdot h^{\prime}=h
). Then, in the down-child (addressed

0
) we place the tree that remains to be factored, which is
C
only with
\square_{h^{\prime}}
instead of
\square
, to signify that this remaining hole is actually
h^{\prime}
, but
h^{\prime}
itself is already being factored elsewhere (namely from node
1
).
Thus, the forest at node
0
is
C\cdot\square_{h^{\prime}}
. We remark that for uniformity (i.e., so that each node is labeled with a forest and a context), we also assign a context to node
0
, but this is just
\square
and is immaterial.

Figure 10
:
A (partial) binary decomposition corresponding to
\cref
xmp:binary decomposition. The plucked factor
h^{\prime}
appears in Node
1
. The context
C
appears highlighted below the
\epsilon\to 1
edge. The contexts associated with nodes
\epsilon
and
2
are omitted (they are both
\square
, see
\cref
def:bin decomp).

We are now ready for the precise definition of binary decomposition.

Definition 50

(Binary Decomposition Tree)
.

A
binary decomposition tree
is an
\widetilde{H_{A}}\times\widetilde{V_{A}}
-labeled tree domain
\tau=\langle\tau.\mathtt{Nodes},\tau.l\rangle\in(\widetilde{H_{A}}\times\widetilde{V_{A}})\text{-{LTDS}}
equipped with three functions

•

\tau.\mathtt{forest}\colon\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\rightarrow\widetilde{H_{A}}

•

\tau.\mathtt{ctx}\colon\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\rightarrow\widetilde{V_{A}}

•

\tau.\mathtt{type}\colon\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\rightarrow\{\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{L}\}

that satisfy the following conditions.

1.

\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
is a
full, binary
tree domain. That is, every
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
is either a leaf, or it has
exactly
two children:
x0
and
x1
. In the latter case, we refer to these children as the
down
and the
right
child, respectively.

2.

For every
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
we have
\tau.l(x)=(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x),\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x))
.

3.

\tau.\mathtt{type}
identifies each node as a
leaf
(
\mathfrak{L}
) or a
binary node
(
\mathfrak{B}
). Thus, the set of leaves is
\tau.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
and the set of binary nodes is
\tau.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{B}]
.

4.

For every
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
and
i\in\{0,1\}
we have
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(xi)\neq\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
. Intuitively, this means that we pluck a nontrivial forest in each node.

5.

For every
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\setminus\mathbb{N}^{*}1
(i.e.,
x
a down-child or the root) we have
\tau.ctx(x)=\square
.

6.

For every binary node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{B}]
we have
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1))
and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)(\square_{\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)})
.

For brevity, in the remainder of the section we just write “decomposition” when referring to a binary decomposition. Also, since decompositions are in particular labeled tree domains, all the relevant definitions of
\cref
sec:labeling intuitive apply.
We say that a decomposition
\tau
is
of forest
h
if
h
is the forest of the root of
\tau
, i.e.,
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=h
.
For a node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
we sometimes refer to
x1
as the
factor
and to
x0
as the
residue
, corresponding to the intuition that we split
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
at some subforest, and factor out this subforest into
x1
, with the rest (the residue) going to
x0
. We often refer to a factor as being
plucked
from its parent node.

Example 51
.

Recall the decomposition in
\cref
xmp:binary decomposition,fig:bin decomp example. Formally, it has the following components:

•

\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}=\{\epsilon,0,1\}

•

\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=h=b(a+a)+a(b+a(a+b))

•

\tau.\mathtt{forest}(1)=b+a(a+b)

•

\tau.\mathtt{forest}(0)=h^{\prime\prime}=b(a+a)+a(\square_{b+a(a+b)})

•

\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(\epsilon)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(0)=\square

•

\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(1)=C=b(a+a)+a(\square)
(notice that
h=C\cdot h^{\prime}
)

Notice that
h=C\cdot h^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime\prime}=C\cdot\square_{h^{\prime}}
.

We remark that despite being part of the definition,
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}
can actually be fully inferred from
\tau.\mathtt{forest}
. Indeed, the requirement that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(xi)\neq\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
(Condition
4
) intuitively means that each decomposition step actually decomposes a nontrivial part of the forest, i.e., splits not just
\square
and not the whole forest. Specifically,
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0)\neq\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
, which means that the part of the tree decomposed into
x1
is not
\square_{h}
for some
h
. Therefore,

\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)\neq\square_{\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)}
for every
x
. Then, by
\cref
lem:equal contexts characterization we have that
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)
is fully determined by the two equations of Condition
6
.

In addition, since each node plucks a nontrivial sub-forest (Condition
4
), we show that every decomposition has bounded depth with respect to the forest at its root.

Lemma 52

(Maximal Decomposition Depth)
.

Consider a forest
t\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
and a binary decomposition
\tau
with
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=t
, then
0pt(\tau)\leq|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t)|-|\mathsf{lab}(t)^{-1}[\square_{H_{A}}]|
(i.e., the depth of
\tau
is at most the number of nodes in
t
that are not labeled by some
\square_{h}
).

Proof.

Denote
\Phi(t)=|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t)|-|\mathsf{lab}(t)^{-1}[\square_{H_{A}}]|
and observe that
\Phi(t)\geq 0
since
\mathsf{lab}(t)^{-1}[\square_{H_{A}}]\subseteq\mathsf{ForestDom}(t)
.
We prove the lemma by induction on
\Phi(t)
:

•

Base case:
\Phi(t)=0
.
In this case we have
t\in\square_{H_{A}}
, so the only possible decomposition of
\tau
is a single leaf at the root, whose forest is
t
. This has depth
0
, as required.

•

Step: assume that for every
t^{\prime}
such that
\Phi(t^{\prime})<\Phi(t)
the lemma holds. Consider a decomposition
\tau
such that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=t
, and assume
0,1\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
(otherwise
0pt(\tau)=0
and we are done).
Denote
t_{0}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(0)
and
t_{1}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(1)
.
Notice that
\Phi(t_{1})<\Phi(t)
. Indeed,
|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t_{1})|<|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t)|
, and any node in
t
that is not in
t_{1}
either contributes
1
to
\Phi(t)
(if it is not in
\square_{H_{A}}
) or
0
if it is in
\square_{H_{A}}
.

We claim that
\Phi(t_{0})<\Phi(t)
as well.
Observe that
|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t_{0})|\leq|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t)|
, but
t_{0}
has an new
\square_{h}
node, with
h=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(t_{1})
that is not in
t
. If
|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t_{0})|=|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t)|
, then since we introduce a new
\square_{h}
node in
t_{0}
, we have
\Phi(t_{0})=\Phi(t)-1
(notice that we cannot have
h\in\square_{H_{A}}
as it would violate Condition
4
, moreover
h\in A
).
Otherwise, we have
|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t_{0})|<|\mathsf{ForestDom}(t)|
, and the same reasoning as above applies: every node in
t
and not in
t_{0}
contributes either
1
or
0
to
\Phi(t)
, depending on whether it is or is not in
\square_{H_{A}}
.

By the induction hypothesis, we can now conclude that

0pt(\tau)\leq 1+\max\{0pt(\tau[0]),0pt(\tau[1])\}\leq 1+\max\{\Phi(t_{0}),\Phi(t_{1})\}\leq\Phi(t)

∎

12.2
References and Ancestor Embeddings

Our next step is to associate with a decomposition
\tau
a
reference
relation. Intuitively, this relation describes how different nodes in the decomposition are “linked” to each other as factors of the same forest. More precisely, consider some node
z\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
. When we factorize
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(z)
to
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(z)=C\cdot h
, then
C=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(z1)
, and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(z0)=C(\square_{h})
. However, there could other nodes in the decomposition tree whose forest is
\square_{h}
, stemming from other parts of the tree where another
h
subtree was plucked.
For certain algorithmic purposes, it is important that we not only remember
\square_{h}
, but also exactly which node in
\tau
generates this
specific

h
. We thus “link”
z0
with
z1
in this case.

However, things get complicated as the decomposition continues: this
\square_{h}
propagates through the tree in further decompositions. For example, when decomposing
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(z0)=C^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime}
, we either have that
\square_{h}
is part of
C^{\prime}
, or part of
h^{\prime}
. In the former case, we need to track the reference from
z0
, and in the latter from
z1
. We also need to carefully maintain the address of this link.

The formal definition is somewhat technical, since it requires tracking addresses. We therefore present an intuitive version, followed by the precise version and an example.

Definition 53

(Decomposition References – Intuitive Version)
.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
. The
reference relation
is a relation
\tau.\mathtt{ref}\subseteq\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\times\mathbb{N}^{+}\times\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, where
\tau.\mathtt{ref}(x,u,y)
is read as “node
x
at position
u
points to node
y
”, and we denote it is
\tau.x@u\to y
.
When
\tau
is clear from context we use the abbreviation
x@u\to y
. We write
x@*\to y
when the exact position
u
is not important.

Then,
x@u\to y
if
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
at address
u
is
\square_{h}
and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)=h
such that at some ancestor of
x
the decomposition plucks this particular subtree
h
.

Note that the only way to have a reference of the form
x1@u\to y
is if the plucked subforest at
x
contains the reference to a subforest that is already plucked above
x
. These represent a “bad” way of plucking (as it is “better” to first pluck the larger sub-forest). We refer to such references as
inherited
.

The precise version of the references relation is as follows.

Definition 54

(Decomposition References)
.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
. The
reference relation
is a relation
\tau.\mathtt{ref}\subseteq\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\times\mathbb{N}^{+}\times\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, where
\tau.\mathtt{ref}(x,u,y)
is read as “node
x
at position
u
points to node
y
”. We typically use the abbreviation
x@u\to y
, and write
x@*\to y
when the exact position
u
is not important.

We define
\tau.\mathtt{ref}
inductively on
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
as follows.
Denote
s=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
.

Base case:

we always add the reference

x0@s\mapsto x1

Step:

assume we already have
x@u\to y
, we define references of
x0
and
x1
that inductively stem from this reference.
Let
M=0pt(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1))
.

•

If
s\not\leq_{LCRS}u
: add reference
x0@u\to y
. This corresponds to the case where the reference from
x
is within the residue, and therefore carried to
x0
as is.

•

If
s\circledast(M+1)\leq_{LCRS}u
: denote
u=s\circledast(M+1)\circledast v
and add reference
x0@(s\circledast 1\circledast v)\to y
. This corresponds to the case where the reference from
x
is within the residue but its address require adjustment due to plucking out the factor.

•

Else (meaning
s\leq_{LCRS}u
and
s\circledast(M+1)\not\leq_{LCRS}u
): Denote
u=s\circledast v
, and add reference
x1@v\to y
. This corresponds to the case where the reference from
x
appears in the sub-forest which currently is plucked out.
We distinguish this latter type of references, and refer to them as
inherited references
.

Example 55

(References)
.

Consider the decomposition depicted in
\cref
fig:bin decomp example 2, extending
\cref
xmp:binary decomposition. The dashed arrows now represent references. Specifically, observe that the address of
\square_{h^{\prime}}
in the forest of Node
1
, namely
b(a+a)+a(\square_{h^{\prime}})
is
10
, and therefore we have the reference
0@10\to 1
.
Next, observe that the subforest plucked at Node
0
contains
\square_{h^{\prime}}
, and therefore we also have the reference
01@*\to 1
(specifically,
01@00\to 1
), and this reference is
inherited
.
We also have a reference
00@*\to 01
.

Figure 11
:
A binary decomposition with depicted references (dashed arrows).

Note that only
inherited
references are from right-children (i.e.,
x1
). Intuitively, an inherited references means that we pluck out a factor, but later decide to pluck a predecessor of that factor. This raises a red flag, as we could have started by plucking out the larger sub-forest. Inherited references help us keep track of this, and we often pay special attention to cases where they do not occur.

A consequence of
\cref
def:decomp references is that for every
x,y\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
there is at most one address
u
such that
x@u\to y
. Intuitively,
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)
is only plucked from
x
once using the node
y
. Identical forests may be plucked from
x
, but not to node
y
.
We can therefore represent the link between
x
and
y
by replacing the referenced
\square_{\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)}
in
x
by
\square
.

Definition 56

(Linking Context)
.

For every
x,y
such that
x@u\to y
we define the
linking context between
x
and
y
as
C_{link}(\tau,x,y)\coloneqq\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)[u\mapsto\square]
.

For example, in
\cref
fig:bin decomp example 2 the reference
01@*\to 1
induces the linking context
C_{link}(\tau,01,1)=a(\square)
. Note that this may seem independent of the node
1
, but in forests with several default holes, it is crucial to know which default hole is replaced with
\square
.

When constructing a decomposition tree of forest
h
, we repeatedly pluck a factor from the current forest of node
x
, and place the factor at
x\cdot 1
and the residue at
x\cdot 0
. Thus, the forests of the factor and the residue can be embedded into the forest of their ancestor nodes. This embedding, dubbed the
Ancestor Embedding

is useful for reasoning about decompositions. We demonstrate this in
\cref
fig:ancestor embedding and define it both intuitively and formally in the following.

Definition 57

(Ancestor Embedding – Intuitive Definition)
.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
and two nodes
x,y\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
such that
x\leq y
. Let
h_{x}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
and
h_{y}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)
. The
Ancestor Embedding
is a function
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)\colon\mathtt{Domain}(h_{y})\to\mathtt{Domain}(h_{x})
which assigns for each address
u
in
h_{y}
the corresponding address of
u
in
h_{x}
, when
h_{y}
is naturally embedded in
h_{x}
.

Definition 58

(Ancestor Embedding – Formal Definition)
.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
and two nodes
x,y\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
such that
x\leq y
. Let
h_{x}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
and
h_{y}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)
. Denote
y=z\cdot\sigma
and let
M_{z}=0pt(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(z1))
and
u_{z}=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(z1))
.
The
Ancestor Embedding

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)\colon\mathtt{Domain}(h_{y})\to\mathtt{Domain}(h_{x})
is defined inductively as follows.

\begin{cases}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)=\mathtt{id}_{\mathtt{Domain}(h_{x})}&y=x\\
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)(u)=u_{z}\circledast u&y=x\cdot 1\\
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)(u)=\begin{cases}u&u=u_{z}\vee u_{z}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
u_{z}\circledast M_{z}\circledast v&u\neq u_{z}\wedge u=u_{z}\circledast v\end{cases}&y=x\cdot 0\\
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,{xw},x)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,{xw})&y=x\cdot w\cdot\sigma\\
\end{cases}

Figure 12
:
A depiction of the Ancestor Embedding
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,01,0)
for the nodes
01
and
0
in
\cref
fig:bin decomp example 2. Specifically, the map is
0\mapsto 1
and
00\mapsto 10
.

In the following lemma we point out some natural properties satisfied by the ancestor embedding.
The first is that the root of
x\cdot 1
is mapped to the position of
\square
in the context of
x\cdot 1
.
The second is that in a reference of the form
y@u\to x1
, the position
u
is mapped to the position of
\square
in the context of
x1
.
The third is the converse of the second: if address
u
in
y
is mapped to the position of
\square
for some node
x1
, then there is a reference
y@u\to x1
.

Lemma 59
.

Consider a node
y
in a decomposition
\tau
. Then the following hold:

1.

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y1,y)(0)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y1))
.

2.

If
y@u\to x1
then
x<y
and
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
.

3.

For two nodes
x<y
such that
y\neq x1
, if
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
for some
u\in\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)
, then
y@u\to x1
.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be a decomposition.

1.

Consider some binary node
y
(so that
y1\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
). By
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal,def:spa we have that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y1,y)(0)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y1))\circledast 0=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y1))
.

2.

We prove this part by induction on the structure of the references, as per
\cref
def:decomp references.
The base case is
y=x0
. In this case we indeed we have
x<y
and by
\cref
def:decomp references we also have
u=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
. Therefore, by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we get
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,x0,x)(u)=u=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
.

For the induction step, assume
y@u\to x1
and that
y=z\sigma
for some
z
. In particular, by
\cref
def:decomp references it holds that
z@u_{z}\to x1
(since this is not a base-case reference).
By the induction hypothesis we then have
x<z<y
, so
x<y
.
We now split to cases:

•

If
y=z0
and
u=u_{z}
, then by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we have we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,z,x)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,z)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,z,x)(u_{z})
, and by the induction hypothesis this equals
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
.

•

If
y=z0
and
u\neq u_{z}
, then by
\cref
def:decomp references we have
u=s\circledast 1\circledast v
where
s=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(z1))
,
M=0pt(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(z1))
, and
u_{z}=s\circledast(M+1)\circledast v
. Again we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,z)(u)=u_{z}
by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal, so the same reasoning as the previous item proves the claim.

•

If
y=z1
, we have (by
\cref
def:decomp references) that
u_{z}=s\circledast u
. Then, by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we get
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,z)=u_{z}
and again the claim follows.

3.

We prove the claim by induction on
y
.

•

If
y=x0
, then by
\cref
def:decomp references we have
y@u\to x1
for
u=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
. Note that by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)(u)=u
(since
u=u_{z}
in the definition by cases). Thus, we have
u=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
, as needed.

•

If
y=xz\sigma
and
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
, by the inductive assumption we have
xz@\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xz\sigma,xz)(u)\to x1
.
Denote
s=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(xz1))
and
M=0pt(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(xz1))
.
We split to cases.

–

If
\sigma=1
: we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xz1,xz)(u)=s\circledast u
, so by
\cref
def:decomp references we get
xz1@u\to x1
.

–

If
\sigma=0
and
s\not\leq_{LCRS}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xz0,xz)(u)
then by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we have
u=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xz0,xz)(u)
. Together with
\cref
def:decomp references this implies
y@u\to x1
.

–

If
\sigma=0
and
s\leq_{LCRS}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xz0,xz)(u)
, notice that
u\neq s
. Indeed, otherwise we would have that
xz@s\to x1
, and therefore
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(xz0)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(xz)
which contradicts
\tau
to be a valid binary decomposition (specifically, Condition
4
of
\cref
def:bin decomp, stating that plucked forests are non-empty).

Then, by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we are at the case of
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xz0,xz)(u)=s\circledast M\circledast v
where
u=s\circledast v
(
1\leq_{LCRS}v
), and in particular
s\circledast(M_{1})\leq_{LCRS}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xz0,xz)(u)
. Thus, by
\cref
def:decomp references we have that
xz0@u\to x1
(as
u=s\circledast v
), so
y@u\to x1
as required.

∎

We conclude this section by formalizing the (intuitive) notion that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)
is determined only by the path in
\tau
from
x
to
y
, and not by anything outside it. We start by showing that
\mathtt{AncEmb}
is not affected by ancestors of
x
.

Lemma 60
.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
and nodes
x\leq y
. For every
z\leq x
denote
x=z\cdot x_{z}
and
y=z\cdot y_{z}
, then
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)\equiv\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau[z],y_{z},x_{z})
.

Proof.

Denote
y=x\cdot y^{\prime}
, we prove the claim by induction on
y^{\prime}
.

•

If
y^{\prime}=\epsilon
, i.e.
x=y
, then
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)
is the identity on
\mathtt{Domain}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y))
(by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal). We also have
x_{z}=y_{z}
and therefore
\mathtt{AncEmb}({\tau[z]},{y_{z}},ex_{z})
is the identity as well.

•

If
y^{\prime}=y^{\prime\prime}\cdot\sigma_{y}
then we have
y_{z}=x_{z}y^{\prime}
and the claim readily follows from the induction hypothesis and
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal:

\begin{split}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)=&\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xy^{\prime\prime},x)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,xy^{\prime\prime})=\\
&\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau[z],x_{z}y^{\prime\prime},x_{z})\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau[z],y_{z},x_{z}y^{\prime\prime})=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau[z],y_{z},x_{z})\end{split}

∎

We now show that
\mathtt{AncEmb}
is not affected by nodes outside the relevant subtree.

Lemma 61
.

Consider two decompositions
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
and node
z
such that
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus z}}\tau^{\prime}
and let
x,y
be nodes such that
z\not<x,y
and
x<y
. Then
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)=\mathtt{AncEmb}({\tau^{\prime}},y,x)
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus z}}\tau^{\prime}
(see notations in
\cref
sec:labeling intuitive), we have
x,y\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\iff x,y\in\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}
and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x)
and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y)
.

We now proceed by induction on the distance between
x
and
y
.

•

If
y
is an immediate child of
x
, notice that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)
and
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},y,x)
only depend on
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)
, respectively. However, we have
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)
(since
z\not<y
and therefore
z\not<x1
when
y
is an immediate child of
x
). We thus conclude the equality of
\mathtt{AncEmb}
.

•

If
y=x\cdot w\cdot\sigma
, then by the induction hypothesis we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xw,x)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},xw,x)
. As above,
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,xw)
and
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},y,xw)
are determined by
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(xw1)
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(xw1)
, respectively, and are
therefore equal (because
z\not<xw1
and
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus z}}\tau^{\prime}
).
We therefore conclude with

\begin{split}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,x)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,xw,x)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,xw)=\\
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},xw,x)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},y,xw)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},y,x)\end{split}

∎

12.3
Stable Decomposition, Basis, and Minimal Decomposition

Recall from
\cref
def:linking context that when
x@u\to y
, a
linking context
from
x
to
y
is induced.
In
\cref
sec:rotations we introduce certain manipulations of decompositions which cause these linking contexts to appear as contexts in the decomposition itself.
In order to guarantee a certain “stability” of the decompositions under such manipulations, we place emphasis on decompositions that allow a pre-specified stable set of contexts (as per
\cref
def:stable context set), as follows.

Definition 62

(Stable Decomposition)
.

Consider a stable context set
\widetilde{V}\subseteq\widetilde{V_{A}}
(as per
\cref
def:stable context set) and a decomposition
\tau
.
We say that
\tau
is
stable with respect to
\widetilde{V}
if for every
x,y
such that
x@u\to y
we have that
C_{link}(\tau,x,y)\in\widetilde{V}

Notice that in a stable decomposition
\tau
with respect to stable context set
\widetilde{V}
we also have that for every
x1\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
it holds that
\tau.x0@\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))\to x1
and thus, by stability we have
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=C_{link}(\tau,x0,x1)\in\widetilde{V}
.

A particular case where decompositions are stable “for free” is when they do not have inherited references, as we now show.

Lemma 63
.

Every decomposition
\tau
with no inherited reference is stable.

Proof.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
with respect to a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
with no inherited references (
\cref
def:decomp references). That is, every reference is of the form
\tau.x0@u\to y1
for some
x,y
. In order to prove that
\tau
is stable, we need to show that for every such reference it holds that
C_{link}(\tau,x0,y1)\in\widetilde{V}
(see
\cref
def:stable dec). Fix
y
, we prove this by induction on
x
.
The base is
x=y
, in which case
C_{link}(\tau,x0,y1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y1)\in\widetilde{V}
by
\cref
def:linking context.

We proceed with the inductive case. Since
x0@u\to y1
, then
x@u^{\prime}\to y1
for some
u^{\prime}
(by
\cref
def:decomp references). Denote
f=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x),f_{1}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1),f_{0}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0)
(see
\cref
fig:linking context stability).

Figure 13
:
The decomposition in the proof of
\cref
lem:no inherited decomposition is stable. The linking contexts are obtained by replacing
\square_{g}
with
\square
.

By
\cref
def:linking context,def:bin decomp we have:

\displaystyle C_{link}(\tau,x0,y1)=f_{0}[u\mapsto\square]

\displaystyle C_{link}(\tau,x,y1)=f[u^{\prime}\mapsto\square]

We claim that
C_{link}(\tau,x,y1)\approx C_{link}(\tau,x0,y1)
(see
\cref
def:equiv unravel). Indeed, the difference between the two contexts is that
C_{link}(\tau,x0,y1)
has
\square_{f_{1}}
where
C_{link}(\tau,x,y1)
has
f_{1}
itself, but this difference disappears under unraveling.

By the induction hypothesis, we have
C_{link}(\tau,x,y1)\in\widetilde{V}
. Since
\widetilde{V}
is a stable context set, it is closed under unraveling (
\cref
def:stable context set) and therefore
C_{link}(\tau,x0,y1)\in\widetilde{V}
, and we are done.
∎

Another restriction we place on decompositions is which forests can appear in their leaves. Technically, this restriction arises due to the inductive nature of our proof: we refine a decomposition by going through a sequence of quotients of algebraic structures. This means that at certain points, we decompose to a set of leaves, but then further decompose the leaves themselves. To this end, we draw the leaves from a specific
basis
set
B\subseteq\widetilde{H_{A}}
.

Furthermore, similarly to the case of words, in the following we construct decompositions by extending existing decompositions. Therefore, we need a notion for decompositions that are “prefixes” of full decompositions.

Definition 64

(Full and Partial Decompositions)
.

Consider a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
and a basis
B\subseteq\widetilde{H_{A}}
.

•

We say that a decomposition
\tau
is
full
with respect to
\widetilde{V}
and
B
if
\tau
is stable with respect to
\widetilde{V}
and for every leaf
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
we have
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)\in B
.

We denote the set of full decompositions by
\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
.
We say that a forest
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
is
decomposable by
\widetilde{V}
to the basis
B
if there is some
\tau\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
such that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=f
.

•

We call a decomposition
\tau

partial with respect to
\widetilde{V}
and
B
if there exists
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
such that
\tau\leq\tau^{\prime}

(meaning
\tau
is a substructure of
\tau^{\prime}
as per
\cref
def:dl order).
We denote the set of partial decompositions with respect to
\widetilde{V}
and
B
by
\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
.

Observe that while
\cref
def:partial decomposition does not explicitly require a partial decomposition to be stable, it is in fact implicitly required, since a partial decomposition must be extendable to a full decomposition (and in particular, a stable decomposition). Since all the linking contexts remain in the extension, we get in particular that the partial decomposition must also be stable.

In order to bootstrap our construction, we observe that there is always a trivial partial decomposition, which contains a single node, as follows.
We define for every forest
f\in\tilde{H}_{A}
the
singleton decomposition

\Upsilon(f)\coloneqq\langle\{\epsilon\},l_{f}\rangle
where
l_{f}(\epsilon)\coloneqq(f,\square)
.

We also use singleton decomposition for a more convenient notion of decomposition substitution:
Consider a decomposition
\tau
and some
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, and a forest
h\in\tilde{H}_{A}
.
Denote by
\tau[x\mapsto h]
the substitution
\tau[x\mapsto\Upsilon(h)]
.

13
Decomposition Rotations

A central tool in our proof, dubbed
rotations
, provides a method for changing the shape of a binary decomposition tree in a way that maintains certain aspects of the decomposition, and guarantees some desirable properties.
Fundamentally, rotations can be thought of as changing the order at which we pluck out factors. Crucially, the difficult part is maintaining the stability of the trees, and reasoning about their references structure.

We remark that this framework is a novelty of the setting of forests. Indeed, for the setting of words the order at which we pluck subwords is (to some extent) irrelevant, and can be easily changed (see
\cref
fig:rotation for words).

Figure 14
:
A tree rotation in the setting of words: two binary decomposition trees for
abcde
with the same leaves. To perform this rotation, we “violently” move the subtree that corresponds to the infix
bc
to the right child of the root instead of the down child. For decomposition (and their stability requirement) this is much more involved.

13.1
Sequential Rotation

A
sequential rotation
, depicted in
\cref
fig:factorizations for TB BT rotation lemma, refers to the following setting. We are decomposing a forest of the form
C_{1}C_{2}h
where
C_{1},C_{2}\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
and
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
. Intuitively, there are two approaches to such a decomposition: in the
Bottom-Top (BT)
approach, we first pluck the “bottom” factor
h
, and then pluck
C_{2}
from the residue. In the
Top-Bottom (TB)
approach, we first pluck the factor
C_{2}h
, and then pluck
h
from this factor.

BT and TB yield two different decomposition trees. However, there is a natural correspondence between them, which is captured by sequential rotations. More precisely, we show that the leaves of the two decompositions have a bijection between them that preserves the way they embed in the root, via
\mathtt{AncEmb}
.

(a)

BT factorization.

(b)

TB factorization

Figure 15
:
Two factorizations (BT and TB) for
C_{1}C_{2}h
. The translation between them is a
sequential rotation
.

Lemma 65

(Sequential Factorization Ancestor Embedding Equivalence)
.

Consider a binary decomposition
\tau
such that
\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}=\{\epsilon,0,1,00,01\}
.
Such that there exists contexts
C_{1},C_{2}\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
for which
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(1)=C_{1}\cdot C_{2}
and
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(01)=C_{1}
.
Denote
h=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(1)
.
Now consider a decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
which is defined by
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}=\{\epsilon,0,1,10,11\}
with
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(1)=C_{1},\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(11)=C_{2}
.
Then for the bijection
\pi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}00\mapsto 0\\
01\mapsto 10\\
1\mapsto 11\end{array}\right)
between the leaves of
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
it holds that for every leaf
y
of
\tau
we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,\epsilon)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},\pi(y),\epsilon)
.

Proof.

Denote
s_{1}=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{1}),\ s_{2}=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{2})
and let

M_{1}=0pt(h),\ M_{01}=0pt(C_{2}\square_{h}),\ M^{\prime}_{1}=0pt(C_{2}h)

We consider each leaf separately. Most of the equalities are immediate from
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal, but some require additional justification.

•

For the leaf
1\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, consider an address
u
. By
\cref
prop: address spos morphism we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,1,\epsilon)(u)=s_{1}\circledast s_{2}\circledast u
.
On the other hand, we have

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},11,\epsilon)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},1,\epsilon)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},11,1)(u)

and then

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},11,1)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(11))\circledast u=s_{2}\circledast u

so

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},1,\epsilon)(s_{2}\circledast u)=s_{1}\circledast s_{2}\circledast u

giving the equivalence
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,1,\epsilon)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},11,\epsilon)
.

•

For the leaf
01\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, again consider an address
u
. We have

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,01,\epsilon)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,01,0)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(s_{1}\circledast u)

By instantiating
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal to our setting, we have

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(s_{1}\circledast u)=\begin{cases}s_{1}\circledast u&s_{2}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{1}\circledast s_{2}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v&u=s_{2}\circledast v\end{cases}

We compare this to the leaf
10
of
\tau^{\prime}
, where the definition yields

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},10,1)(u)=\begin{cases}u&s_{2}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{2}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v&u=s_{2}\circledast v\end{cases}

and
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},1,\epsilon)(u^{\prime})=s_{1}\circledast u^{\prime}
for every
u^{\prime}
. Since

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},10,\epsilon)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},1,\epsilon)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},10,1)

we plug in the above and get

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},10,\epsilon)(u)=\begin{cases}s_{1}\circledast u&s_{2}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{1}\circledast s_{2}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v&u=s_{2}\circledast v\end{cases}

again yielding the desired equality
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},10,\epsilon)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,01,\epsilon)
.

•

Finally, for the leaf
00\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, consider an address
u
. We separate to cases depending on the relative position of
s_{1},s_{2}
and
u
.

–

If
s_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}u
, then in particular
s_{1}\circledast s_{2}\not\leq_{LCRS}u
. Then, we simply have

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,\epsilon)(u)=u=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0,\epsilon)(u)

and we are done.

–

Otherwise,
s_{1}\leq_{LCRS}u
, i.e.,
u
is a descendant of
s_{1}
in the left-child/right-sibling order. By
\cref
prop:lcrs spa we can write
u=s_{1}\circledast v
for some unique
v
.
Note that
u
cannot be a descendant of
s_{1}
itself, since
s_{1}
is a leaf in
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(00)
, and therefore has no descendants.
Thus, we can write
v=\sigma_{v}v^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{v}>0
(since if
\sigma_{v}=0
we would get
u=s_{1}\cdot v^{\prime}
, contradicting
s_{1}
being a leaf).

Write
s_{2}=\sigma_{2}s^{\prime}_{2}
and observe that
\sigma_{2}\leq M_{01}
by definition. We split to two further cases.

*

If
s_{2}\notin\mathbb{N}
(i.e., if
s^{\prime}_{2}\neq\epsilon
) then
C_{2}
does not have its
\square
as a root, and therefore
M_{01}=0pt(C_{2}\square_{h})=0pt(C_{2}h)=M^{\prime}_{1}
.
By
\cref
prop:spa and concat we have
s_{2}=(\sigma_{2}\cdot 0)\circledast s^{\prime}_{2}
. Now, since
\sigma_{2}\leq M_{01}
and
\sigma_{v}>0
we have

(\sigma_{2}\cdot 0)\circledast s^{\prime}_{2}\not\leq_{LCRS}((M_{01}+\sigma_{v})\cdot 0)\circledast v^{\prime}

Indeed, otherwise by
\cref
prop:lcrs spa we would have that
((M_{01}+\sigma_{v})\cdot 0)\circledast v^{\prime}=(\sigma_{2}\cdot 0)\circledast s^{\prime}_{2}\circledast v^{\prime\prime}
for some
v^{\prime\prime}
, but this is a contradiction since the right-hand side starts with
\sigma_{2}<M_{01}+\sigma_{v}
.
Then, by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we again have

\begin{split}&\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,\epsilon)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(s_{1}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v)=\\
&s_{1}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v=s_{1}\circledast M^{\prime}_{1}\circledast v=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0,\epsilon)\end{split}

*

If
s_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
then
s_{2}=\sigma_{2}
and
s^{\prime}_{2}=\epsilon
. Notice that in this case we have
M^{\prime}_{1}=M_{01}+M_{1}
. Since
s_{2}\leq M_{01}
, we have
M_{01}=s_{2}\circledast(M_{01}-s_{2})
. Plugging this into
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal and using the fact that for
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
we have
x\circledast y=x+y
, we have

\begin{split}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,\epsilon)=&\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(s_{1}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v)\\
=&\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(s_{1}\circledast s_{2}\circledast(M_{01}-s_{2})\circledast v)\\
=&s_{1}\circledast s_{2}\circledast M_{1}\circledast(M_{01}-s_{2})\circledast v\\
=&s_{1}\circledast(s_{2}+M_{1}+M_{01}-s_{2})\circledast v\\
=&s_{1}\circledast M^{\prime}_{1}\circledast v\\
=&\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0,\epsilon)(u)\end{split}

∎

Having established the equivalence in the leaves of
\mathtt{AncEmb}
between the a given BT and TB decomposition, we now show how to obtain a TB decomposition by “rotating” a BT decomposition.

Lemma 66

(BT to TB Rotation)
.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau
(over
\widetilde{V},B
) and nodes
x,x0,x1,x00,x01\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
such that
x01@u\to x1

(as depicted by the dashed red arrow in
\cref
fig:factorizations for TB BT rotation lemma).
Then
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)\cdot C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)
,
and there exist a partial decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau

such that:

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)=C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)

•

\tau^{\prime}[x0]=\tau[x00]
,
\tau^{\prime}[x10]=\tau[x01]
, and
\tau^{\prime}[x11]=\tau[x1]
.

Proof.

We start the proof by focusing on the first two items. To this end, we ignore the suffix of
\tau
below
x00,x01,x1
. Intuitively, we can think of these nodes as leaves, which they might be. Later on, we replace them with their actual subtrees in order to obtain the last requirement of the lemma.
Denote the following

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)

\displaystyle C_{01}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)

\displaystyle h_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0)

\displaystyle h_{01}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x01)

Since by
\cref
def:decomp references we have
x0@\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{1})\to x_{1}
and we assume
x01@u\to x1
, then by
\cref
def:decomp references we have
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{1})=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{01})\circledast u
.

We first show that
C_{1}=C_{01}\cdot C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)
, using the context-equality criteria of
\cref
lem:equal contexts characterization:

•

By the above and by
\cref
def:linking context,prop: address spos morphism we get

\begin{split}\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C1)&=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{01})\circledast u=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{01})\circledast\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1))\\
&=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{01}\cdot C_{link}(\tau,x01,x_{1}))\end{split}

•

We now plug
\square_{h_{1}}
in
C_{1}
(and recall
h_{1}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)
):

C_{1}(\square_{h_{1}})=h_{0}=C_{01}(h_{01})=C_{01}\cdot C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)(\square_{h_{1}})

By
\cref
lem:equal contexts characterization, the two criteria above imply
C_{1}=C_{01}\cdot C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)
.

We can now define
\tau^{\prime\prime}=\langle d^{\prime\prime}=\{\epsilon,0,1,10,11\},l^{\prime\prime}\rangle
such that:

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\langle\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x),\square\rangle

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=\langle\tau.\mathtt{forest}(00),\square\rangle

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(1)

\displaystyle=\langle C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)),C_{01}\rangle

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(10)

\displaystyle=\langle\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x01),\square\rangle

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(11)

\displaystyle=\langle\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1),C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)\rangle

Since
\tau
is a partial decomposition, then it is in particular stable (as per
\cref
def:stable dec). It then follows that
\tau^{\prime\prime}
is also a stable decomposition. Indeed, it has a single right child and
\widetilde{V}
is a stable context set.

Additionally, observe that
\tau[x],\tau^{\prime\prime}
satisfy the conditions of
\cref
lem:sequential factor map.
Define
\tau^{\prime}\coloneqq\tau[x\mapsto\tau^{\prime\prime}]
(i.e., we plug
\tau^{\prime\prime}
in
\tau
under
x
). We then have that
\tau^{\prime}
is a binary decomposition over
\widetilde{V}
,
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
and it holds that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)=\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(11)=C_{link}(\tau.x01,x1)
.

It remains to show that
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
, i.e., that it can be completed to a full decomposition. Intuitively, this follows by using roughly the same extension of
\tau
, correctly adapted to the rotation. We proceed with the details.

Since
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
, there is a full decomposition
\tau_{1}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
such that
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
. We assume without loss of generality that every leaf
y\in\tau.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
which is not below
x
(
x\not<y
) satisfies
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)\in B
(otherwise we can first complete
\tau
outside the
x
subtree).
Define
\tau_{2}\coloneqq\tau^{\prime}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}x0\mapsto\tau_{1}[x00]\\
x10\mapsto\tau_{1}[x01]\\
x11\mapsto\tau_{1}[x1]\end{array}\right]
. Since
\tau_{1}
is full, then
\tau_{2}
has all its leaves in
B
, and has contexts in
\widetilde{V}
. We now need to show that
\tau_{2}
is stable.
Since
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}
we also get
\tau_{1}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau_{2}
.
Consider
y,z\in\tau_{2}.\mathtt{Nodes}
and
u
such that

\tau_{2}.y@u\to z
, and denote denote
z=\hat{z}1
. We split to cases and prove that
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,z)\in\widetilde{V}
.

•

If
x\not\leq y,z
:
by
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references,lem:ancestor embedding not affected by incomparable we get that
\tau_{1}.y@u\to z
.
Since
\tau_{1}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau_{2}
we also have
\tau_{1}.\mathtt{forest}(y)=\tau_{2}.\mathtt{forest}(y)
.
We conclude that
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,z)=C_{link}(\tau_{1},y,z)\in\widetilde{V}
.

•

If there is some
w\in\{x0,x10,x11\}
(in
\tau^{\prime}
) such
w\leq y,z
,
recall (as in
\cref
lem:sequential factor map) that
\pi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}00\mapsto 0\\
01\mapsto 10\\
1\mapsto 11\end{array}\right)
maps between the leaves of
\tau[x]
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}
.
Denote
w=x\pi(v)
and
y=w\cdot y^{\prime},z=w\cdot z^{\prime}1
.
By
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding not affected by ancestor and because
\tau_{2}[w]=\tau_{1}[xv]
we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,\hat{z})=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2}[w],y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1}[xv],y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},xvy^{\prime},xvz^{\prime})
.
Hence (by
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references)
\tau_{1}.xvy^{\prime}@u\to xvz^{\prime}
,
and because
\tau_{1}.\mathtt{forest}(xvy^{\prime})=\tau_{1}[xv].\mathtt{forest}(y^{\prime})=\tau_{2}[w].\mathtt{forest}(y^{\prime})=\tau_{2}.\mathtt{forest}(y)
,
we conclude that
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,z)=C_{link}(\tau_{1},xvy^{\prime},xvz^{\prime}1)\in\widetilde{V}
.

•

If
x\not\leq z
and there is a leaf
w
of
\tau^{\prime}
such that
w\leq y
(denote
y=w\cdot y^{\prime}
), again recall
\pi
as per
\cref
lem:sequential factor map.
Notice that (by the assumptions and
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding not affected by ancestor,lem:ancestor embedding not affected by incomparable,def:ancestor embedding formal) we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},x,\hat{z})=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},x,\hat{z})
,
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},w,x)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},xv,x)
and
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,w)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},xvy^{\prime},xv)
.
Overall, together with
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references we conclude
\tau_{1}.xvy^{\prime}@u\to z
, and since
\tau_{1}.\mathtt{forest}(xvy^{\prime})=\tau_{2}.\mathtt{forest}(y)
we have
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,z)=C_{link}(\tau_{1},xvy^{\prime},z)\in\widetilde{V}
.

•

If
z=x11
and
x10\leq y
(denote
y=x10y^{\prime}
), we have

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x1)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},x10,x1)(\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x10)(u))=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{2}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11))

By
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we obtain
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x10)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{2}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11))
.
Denote
u^{\prime}=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{2}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11))=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{link}(\tau_{1},x01,x1))
.
By
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding not affected by incomparable,lem:ancestor embedding not affected by ancestor we have that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},x01y^{\prime},x01)(u)=u^{\prime}
.
By
\cref
def:linking context we get that

\tau_{1}.x01@u^{\prime}\to x1
, so by
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references, we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},x01,x)(u^{\prime})=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{1}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
.

Overall we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},x01y^{\prime},x)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{1}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
, so
\tau_{1}.x01y^{\prime}@u\to x1
.
Since
\tau_{2}.\mathtt{forest}(y)=\tau_{1}.\mathtt{forest}(x01y^{\prime})
we conclude
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,x11)=C_{link}(\tau_{1},x01y^{\prime},x1)\in\widetilde{V}
.

•

If
z=x1
and
x0\leq y
(denote
y=x0y^{\prime}
), then similarly to the previous case, we have

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},x0,x)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x0)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{2}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))

Thus,

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},x00y^{\prime},x00)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x0)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{2}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{1}.\mathtt{ctx}(x01))

Together with
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal,lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references we conclude
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},x00y^{\prime},x0)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau_{1}.\mathtt{ctx}(x01))
, so

\tau_{1}.x00y^{\prime}@u\to x01
.
Overall
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,z)=C_{link}(\tau_{1},x00y^{\prime},x01)\in\widetilde{V}
.

We conclude that
\tau_{2}
is stable, and therefore
\tau^{\prime}
is a partial decomposition.

Note, however, that we have yet to consider the third requirement of the lemma (namely the equivalence of the subforests rooted at the leaves
x00,x01
, and
x1
with their
\tau
analogues).
We now easily obtain it: recall that
\tau_{2}
is obtained by plugging in subtrees of
\tau_{1}
into the rotated
\tau^{\prime\prime}
, and that
\tau_{1}
extends
\tau
. We can therefore modify
\tau^{\prime}
so that instead of plugging singletons in the rotated tree, we plug the subtrees of
\tau
. Thus, we essentially “prune”
\tau_{2}
so that its subtrees come from
\tau
instead of
\tau_{1}
.
Then
\tau^{\prime}
remains a partial decomposition (since it is still a prefix of the stable
\tau_{2}
), and now it satisfies the last requirement, namely that

\tau^{\prime}[x0]=\tau[x00]
,
\tau^{\prime}[x10]=\tau[x01]
, and
\tau^{\prime}[x11]=\tau[x1]
.
∎

A dual argument to
\cref
lem:rotation BT to TB shows that we can obtain a BT decomposition by rotating a TB decomposition. We therefore have the following.

Lemma 67

(TB to BT Rotation)
.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau
(over
\widetilde{V},B
) and nodes
x,x0,x1,x11,x10\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
,
Then there exists a partial decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
such that:

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\cdot\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)

•

\tau^{\prime}[x00]=\tau[x0]
,
\tau^{\prime}[x01]=\tau[x10]
, and
\tau^{\prime}[x1]=\tau[x11]
.

13.2
Parallel Rotation

Our second type of rotation concerns the setting where we pluck two subforests that are not nested, i.e., they are “parallel”, as depicted in
\cref
fig:LR and RL decompositions.
We then have a choice of plucking the left sub-forest before the right one (LR), or vice-versa (RL), yielding two different, but highly-related, decompositions.

(a)

LR factorization.

(b)

RL factorization

Figure 16
:
Two factorizations (LR and RL). The translation between them is a
parallel rotation
.

Similarly to
\cref
sec:sequential rotation, we start by identifying a bijection between the nodes of LR and RL decompositions. Then, we proceed to show how we obtain an RL decomposition from an LR one, and vice versa.

Lemma 68

(Parallel Factorization Ancestor Embedding Equivalence)
.

Consider a binary decomposition
\tau
with
\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}=\{\epsilon,0,1,00,01\}

such that exist
u_{1},u_{01}
with

00@u_{1}\to 1
and
00@u_{01}\to 01
.
Let

\displaystyle C_{1}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(1)

\displaystyle C_{01}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(01)

\displaystyle h_{1}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(1)

\displaystyle h_{01}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(01)

\displaystyle h_{00}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(00)

We obtain
\tau^{\prime}
from
\tau
by setting:
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}=\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
,
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(1)=C_{01}[u_{1}\mapsto h_{1}]
(denoted
C^{\prime}_{01}
) and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(01)=C_{link}(\tau,00,1)
(denoted
C^{\prime}_{1}
). Then
\tau^{\prime}
is a decomposition which for the mapping
\pi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\mapsto 01\\
01\mapsto 1\\
00\mapsto 00\end{array}\right)
satisfies
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,y,\epsilon)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},\pi(y),\epsilon)
for every
y\in\{1,01,00\}
.

Proof.

Notice that
\tau^{\prime}
is a valid binary decomposition, with
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(1)=h_{01}
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(01)=h_{1}
, and since
\tau
is stable (over
\widetilde{V}
) we have
C^{\prime}_{1}=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(01)=C_{link}(\tau,00,1)\in\widetilde{V}
and
C^{\prime}_{01}=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(1)=C_{01}[u_{1}\mapsto h_{1}]\approx C_{01}\in\widetilde{V}
. Additionally,
C^{\prime}_{01}(h_{01})=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
,
C^{\prime}_{1}(h_{1})=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0)=C^{\prime}_{01}(\square_{h_{01}})
,
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(00)=C^{\prime}_{1}(\square_{h_{1}})=h_{00}
.
Notice that
\pi
matches between
\tau.\mathtt{forest}
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}
.

The remainder of the proof is painful accounting, complicated by the fact that
h_{1}
and
h_{01}
might have several roots (i.e., have width larger than
1
). Technically, we consider each possible configuration of
h_{1}
and
h_{01}
(i.e., whether they are nested or incomparable), and for each such configuration consider each possible address
u
in their domain. Moreover, this is done for each leaf, leading to a bunch of cases.

Denote by
s_{1},s_{01},s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{01}
the respective
\mathtt{sq\_pos}
of
C_{1},C_{01},C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{01}
, and let
M_{1}=0pt(h_{1})
and
M_{01}=0pt(h_{01})
.
Notice that
\tau.0@s_{1}\to 1
, thus by
\cref
def:decomp references if
s_{01}\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}\wedge s_{01}\circledast(M_{01}+1)\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}
then
\tau.01@*\to 1
. However, the latter is not the case, since we already have
\tau.00@u_{1}\to 1
by assumption. Therefore, we have that

s_{01}\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}\rightarrow s_{01}\circledast(M_{01}+1)\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}

Indeed, this intuitively means that if
s_{01}
is “left” of
s_{1}
, then it remains left of it also after adding the width of the plucked forest
h_{01}
(otherwise there is no reference back to
1
from
00
).
We proceed by expressing
s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{01}
in terms of
s_{1},s_{01}
:

•

For
s^{\prime}_{1}
:
Recall that
s^{\prime}_{1}=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{link}(\tau,00,1))=u_{1}
, and therefore
\tau.00@s^{\prime}_{1}\to 1
(by the assumption
\tau.00@u_{1}\to 1
).
Since
\tau.0@s_{1}\to 1
, then by
\cref
def:decomp references we have that

s^{\prime}_{1}=\begin{cases}s_{1}&s_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}\\
s_{01}\circledast w_{1}&s_{1}=s_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast w_{1}\end{cases}

•

For
s^{\prime}_{01}
:
again by
\cref
def:decomp references we have

u_{1}=\begin{cases}s_{1}&s_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}\\
s_{01}\circledast v_{1}&s_{1}=s_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v_{1}\end{cases}

Recall that
s^{\prime}_{01}=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{01}[u_{1}\mapsto h_{1}])
, and therefore (by direct computation) we have

s^{\prime}_{01}=\begin{cases}s_{01}&u_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{01}\\
u_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v_{01}&s_{01}=u_{1}\circledast v_{01}\end{cases}

Plugging in
u_{1}
in the latter we conclude that

s^{\prime}_{01}=\begin{cases}s_{01}&s_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{01}\\
s_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast w_{01}&s_{01}=s_{1}\circledast w_{01}\end{cases}

We now show the equality of
\mathtt{AncEmb}
for each leaf
y
, based on
s^{\prime}_{1}
and
s^{\prime}_{01}
above.

•

For
y=00
:

–

If
s_{1},s_{01}
are
\leq_{LCRS}
incomparable, then we have
s_{1}=s^{\prime}_{1},s_{01}=s^{\prime}_{01}
and we get the desired
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,\epsilon)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},00,\epsilon)
.

–

If
s_{1}\leq_{LCRS}s_{01}
(denote
s_{01}=s_{1}\circledast w_{01}
) then recall we have
s^{\prime}_{01}=s_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast w_{01}
, and
s^{\prime}_{1}=s_{1}
. Note that this case is not depicted in
\cref
fig:LR and RL decompositions – it reflects the case where
h_{01}
is nested within
h_{1}
.
We now compute
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,\epsilon)(u)
by first computing
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,0)(u)
, as per
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal.
We therefore have

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,0)(u)=\begin{cases}u&s_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{1}\circledast w_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v&u=s_{01}\circledast v=s_{1}\circledast w_{01}\circledast v\end{cases}

We can now split to cases according to whether
u
is below
s_{1}
and
s_{01}
or not. This yields

\begin{split}&\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,\epsilon)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,0)(u)=\\
&\begin{cases}u&s_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v&u=s_{1}\circledast v\wedge s_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast w_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v&u=s_{01}\circledast v\end{cases}\end{split}

A similar analysis for
\tau^{\prime}
yields the following.

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},00,0)(u)=\begin{cases}u&s_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v&u=s_{1}\circledast v\end{cases}

and again introducing a split on
s_{01}
and
u
yields

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},00,\epsilon)(u)=\begin{cases}u&s_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v&u=s_{1}\circledast v\wedge s_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s^{\prime}_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v&u=s_{01}\circledast v=s_{1}\circledast w_{01}\circledast v\end{cases}

and the latter is equal to
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,\epsilon)(u)
, so we are done.

–

If
s_{01}\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}
denote
s_{1}=s_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast w_{1}
. This is again a case not depicted in
\cref
fig:LR and RL decompositions.
Recall that
s^{\prime}_{01}=s_{01}
and
s^{\prime}_{1}=s_{01}\circledast w_{1}
.
We use
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal to compute

\begin{split}&\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,00,\epsilon)(u)=\begin{cases}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(s_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v)&u=s_{01}\circledast v\\
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(u)&s_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}u&s_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v&u=s_{01}\circledast v\wedge s^{\prime}_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast w_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v&u=s^{\prime}_{1}\circledast v=s_{01}\circledast w_{1}\circledast v\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0,\epsilon)(u)&s^{\prime}_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
s^{\prime}_{01}\circledast M_{01}\circledast v&u=s^{\prime}_{01}\circledast v\wedge s^{\prime}_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}u\\
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0,\epsilon)(s^{\prime}_{01}\circledast w_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast v)&u=s^{\prime}_{1}\circledast v\end{cases}\end{split}

and the latter is exactly
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},00,\epsilon)(u)
as required.

•

For
y=01
:

–

If
s_{1}\leq_{LCRS}s_{01}
denote
s_{01}=s_{1}\circledast w
.
Notice that in this case
s^{\prime}_{01}=s_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast w
, so

\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,01,\epsilon)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(s_{01}\circledast u)=s_{1}\circledast M_{1}\circledast w\circledast u=s^{\prime}_{01}\circledast u=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},1,\epsilon)(u)

–

If
s_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{01}
,
we show that
s_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{01}\circledast u
. Indeed, if
s_{01}\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}
, then recall the implication
s_{01}\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}\rightarrow s_{01}\circledast(M_{01}+1)\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}
, which precludes
s_{1}\leq_{LCRS}s_{01}\circledast u
(for every
u\in\mathtt{Domain}(h_{01})
), since
M_{01}=0pt(h_{01})
.
Otherwise, if
s_{01}\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{1}
, then clearly
s_{1}\not\leq_{LCRS}s_{01}\circledast u
, since no suffix appended to
s_{01}
can go “below”
s_{1}
.

Thus, we have
s^{\prime}_{01}=s_{01}
, and we conclude that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,01,\epsilon)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0,\epsilon)(s_{01}\circledast u)=s_{01}\circledast u=s^{\prime}_{01}\circledast u
.

•

For
y=1
, the setting is is symmetric to the case above, as
\pi(1)=01
.

∎

We can now show the parallel rotation lemma.

Lemma 69

(LR to RL Rotation)
.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau
(over
\widetilde{V},B
) and
x,x0,x1,x00,x01\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
such that

\tau.x00@u_{1}\to x1
.
Then there exist a partial decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
such that:

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)[u_{1}\mapsto\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)]\approx\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)=C_{link}(\tau,x00,x1)\approx\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)

•

\tau^{\prime}[x1]=\tau[x01]
,
\tau^{\prime}[x01]=\tau[x1]
, and
\tau^{\prime}[x00]=\tau[x00]
.

as depicted in
\cref
fig:LR and RL decompositions.

Proof.

Similarly to
\cref
lem:dec rot A, denote by
h_{1},h_{01},h_{00}
the forests
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)
,
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x01)
,
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x00)
, respectively, and by
C_{1},C_{01}
the contexts
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1),\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)
, respectively.
As per
\cref
lem:parallel factor map, denote
C^{\prime}_{01}\coloneqq C_{01}[u_{1}\mapsto h_{1}]
and
C^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq C_{link}(\tau,x00,x1)
,
and define
\tau^{\prime\prime}=\langle d^{\prime\prime}=\{\epsilon,0,1,00,01\},l^{\prime\prime}\rangle
such that:

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\langle\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x),\square\rangle

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=\langle C^{\prime}_{01}(\mathtt{to\_square}(h_{01}),\square\rangle

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(1)

\displaystyle=\langle h_{01},C^{\prime}_{01}\rangle

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(00)

\displaystyle=\langle h_{00},\square\rangle

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(01)

\displaystyle=\langle h_{1},C^{\prime}_{1}\rangle

By
\cref
lem:parallel factor map we have that
\pi=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\mapsto 01\\
01\mapsto 1\\
00\mapsto 00\end{array}\right)
preserves
\mathtt{AncEmb}
between
\tau^{\prime\prime}
and
\tau[x]
.
Define
\tau^{\prime}\coloneqq\tau[x\mapsto\tau^{\prime\prime}]
.
Notice that we already fulfill the requirements
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x1)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x01)
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x01)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)
.
We now show that
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
.
First consider
\tau_{1}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
which extends
\tau
, and assume without loss of generality that for every leaf of
y\in\tau.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
which is not below
x
(
x\not<y
) we have
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)\in B
.
Define
