\tau_{2}\coloneqq\tau^{\prime}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}x1\mapsto\tau_{1}[x01]\\
x01\mapsto\tau_{1}[x1]\\
x00\mapsto\tau_{1}[x00]\end{array}\right]
.
Notice that all the leaves of
\tau_{2}
are in
B
, so it remains to prove that
\tau_{2}
is a stable decomposition.
Similarly to
\cref
lem:dec rot A we rely on
\cref
lem:parallel factor map to prove that whenever
\tau_{2}.y@u\to z
it holds that
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,z)\in\widetilde{V}
.
The only two cases which that differ from the proof of
\cref
lem:rotation BT to TB are the following:

•

If
z=x1
: in this case we have
x00\leq y
(because
\tau_{2}.x00@u_{01}\to x1
).
By
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references we have that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},x00,x)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x00)(u)=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime}_{01})
.
Since
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},x00,x)(u_{01})=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C^{\prime}_{01})
we get
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},y,x00)(u)=u_{01}=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},y,x00)(u)
.
Recall that
\tau.x00@u_{01}\to x01
,
and therefore
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},x00,x0)(u_{01})=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{01})=u_{01}
.

We conclude (by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal) that
\tau_{1}.y@u\to x01
, yielding
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,x1)=C_{link}(\tau_{1},y,x01)\in\widetilde{V}
.

•

If
z=x01
: similarly to the previous case, we obtain
C_{link}(\tau_{2},y,x01)=C_{link}(\tau_{1},y,x1)\in\widetilde{V}
.

We therefore conclude that
\tau_{2}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
so
\tau^{\prime}
satisfies the first two requirements of the lemma.

We can now extends
\tau^{\prime}
by plugging in the respective subforests of
\tau
in its leaves. This decomposition is still partial, since
\tau_{2}
still extends it (as
\tau_{1}
extends
\tau
). We thus conclude the third requirement.
∎

14
Semantic Properties of Binary Decompositions

Equipped with the rotation toolbox, we can now show that, in a sense, the contexts along a decomposition get “smaller”.
As with many of our results – in the setting of words it is a trivial observation, but becomes intricate for forests.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau
and some
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
. There may be multiple ways to extend
\tau
and decompose
x
to achieve a
full
decomposition (see
\cref
def:partial decomposition,def:full decomposition).
In order to reason about such extensions, we define a “local” version of these possibilities, pertaining to which factors can immediately follow a node.

Definition 70

(Next Factors)
.

Consider a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
, a basis
B\subseteq\widetilde{H_{A}}
and a node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
.

•

The
factorization step
of
x
is
\mathtt{Factor}(\tau,x)\coloneqq(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1),\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1))\in\widetilde{V_{A}}\times\widetilde{H_{A}}
(if
\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{B}
, and undefined for leaves).

•

The
next factors
of
x
are:

\mathtt{NxtFctrs}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)\coloneqq\{\mathtt{Factor}(\tau^{\prime},x):\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B),\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{B}\}

When clear from context, denote
\mathtt{NxtFctrs}(\tau,x)
.

We often restrict attention only to the next context of a node, rather than the entire factor. We capture this as follows.

Definition 71

(Next Contexts)
.

Consider a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
, a basis
B\subseteq\widetilde{H_{A}}
, a binary decomposition tree
\tau
and some
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
.
We define

\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)=\{\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x\cdot 1):\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime},\ \tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B),\ \tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{B}\}

When clear from context, denote
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,x)
.

Note that
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,x)=\{C:(C,f)\in\exists f.\mathtt{NxtFctrs}(\tau,x)\}
.

An important observation is that
computing

\mathtt{NxtFctrs}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)
(and
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)
) might be difficult, as it relies on
every
full decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
. Since our main result is not algorithmic, this does not affect our results.

The following simple observation is that if a node in a partial decomposition has no next contexts, then its forest is already in
B
.

Lemma 72
.

Consider a binary decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
and
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
such that
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)=\emptyset
, then
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)\in B
.

Proof.

Since
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
, by
\cref
def:partial decomposition there exists
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
that extends
\tau
.
By
\cref
def:next contexts and since
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)=\emptyset
, we conclude that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{L}
(otherwise there would be a next context in
\tau^{\prime}
).
By
\cref
def:full decomposition we conclude that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x)\in B
.
∎

In the following we place further restrictions on the “allowed” set of next factors (or contexts), namely “minimality”. In order to allow enough flexibility in such restriction, we only consider restrictions that are closed under unraveling. Thus, we lift
\cref
def:stable context set to factors by defining a
stable factorization set
as
S\subseteq\widetilde{V_{A}}\times\widetilde{H_{A}}
such that for every
(C,f)\in S
, if
C\approx C^{\prime}
and
f\approx f^{\prime}
then
(C^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in S
.
In order to define minimality, we first equip
\widetilde{V_{A}}
with a natural ‘‘prefix’’ order
6
6

6

Readers familiar with Green’s relation
[
11
]
may notice that this is exactly Green’s
\leq_{R}
relation.
(and lift it to factors). We demonstrate this in
\cref
fig:order on contexts.

Definition 73

(The prefix order
\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}
.)
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
, then
C_{1}\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}C_{2}
if there exists
C\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
such that
C_{1}\cdot C=C_{2}
.

For factors
(C,f),(C^{\prime},f^{\prime})
we define
(C,f)\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}(C^{\prime},f^{\prime})
when
C\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}C^{\prime}
(ignoring the forest).

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c}C_{1}:&\begin{forest}&\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}\ \begin{forest}\ :C_{2}\end{array}

(a)

C_{1}\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}C_{2}
since
C_{1}\cdot(b(a+\square))=C_{2}

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c}C_{1}:&\begin{forest}&\not\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}\ \begin{forest}\ :C_{2}\end{array}

(b)

C_{1}\not\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}C_{2}
, since for every
C
we have
C_{1}\cdot C\neq C_{2}
.

Figure 17
:
Examples illustrating the order
\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}
between contexts.

We can now capture the notion of
minimal
decompositions, in the sense that from a certain node we pluck the “first” context available from the context set.
More precisely, a decomposition is minimal below a certain node
x
if the contexts plucked below
x
are
\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}
-minimal among the contexts in
\mathtt{NxtCtx}
(given
\widetilde{V}
, basis, and position). That is, we cannot leave a “smaller” residue and still extend to a full decomposition.

Definition 74

(Minimal Decomposition Below Node)
.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
, a stable factorization set
S\subseteq\widetilde{H_{A}}\times\widetilde{V_{A}}
(respectively, stable context set
S\subseteq\widetilde{V_{A}}
), and a node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
,

We say that
\tau

is minimal below
x
with respect to
\widetilde{V},B
and
S
, if for every
y\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\tau.\mathtt{type}(xy)=\mathfrak{B}
we have that
\tau.\mathtt{Factor}(xy)
(respectively,
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(xy1)
) is
\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}
-minimal among
\mathtt{NxtFctrs}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,xy)\cap S
(respectively,
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,xy)\cap S
).

When
S=\widetilde{H_{A}}\times\widetilde{V_{A}}
(respectively,
S=\widetilde{V_{A}}
) we omit it and say that that
\tau
is minimal with respect to
\widetilde{V},B
.
When
\widetilde{V},B
(and
S
) are clear, we simply say that
\tau

is minimal below
x
.

Notice that a minimal decomposition is not necessarily unique.

A minimal partial decomposition is not particularly restrictive. However, what we are actually after is a way of completing minimal partial decompositions to full ones, while maintaining minimality. The latter is stricter, since we need to maintain minimality while “striving” toward the basis
B
. In the following we show something even slightly stronger: if a partial decomposition is minimal below some node
x
, then we can complete the subtree below
x
to a full decomposition while retaining minimality.

Lemma 75

(Minimal Full Decomposition)
.

For every
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
and
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, there is
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
such that
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
is minimal below
x
(for both contexts and factors), and
\tau^{\prime}[x]\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
.

Proof.

We remark that proving the lemma for either contexts or factors implies the other, since a minimal decomposition with respect to the factorization set
\widetilde{H_{A}}\times\widetilde{V_{A}}
implies a minimal decomposition for the context set
\widetilde{V_{A}}
(by simple projection), and vice-versa, a minimal decomposition with respect to the context set
\widetilde{V_{A}}
induces a decomposition with respect to
\widetilde{V_{A}}\times\widetilde{H_{A}}
, by using the factors that actually occur in the tree. We therefore prove the lemma for contexts.

Observe that the decomposition
\tau^{\prime\prime}=\tau[x\mapsto\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)]
obtained by “folding” the subtree under
x
in
\tau
is trivially minimal below
x
, and moreover
\tau^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
. In particular, the set of minimal decompositions below
x
is nonempty.

Let
\tau^{\prime}
be a decomposition that is minimal below
x
, satisfies
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
, and is maximal in the sense that no leaf can be further expanded while maintaining these properties. Such
\tau^{\prime}
exists by
\cref
lem:max dec, which shows that the depth of all decompositions is bounded.

We claim that
\tau^{\prime}[x]
is a full decomposition. Indeed, assume by way of contradiction that this is not the case, then there exists some leaf
y\in\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
such that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y)\notin B
.
By
\cref
def:partial decomposition,def:next contexts we have that
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)\neq\emptyset
and it therefore has some minimal element
C
. We can thus extend
\tau^{\prime}
by plucking out a forest from
y
so that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(y1)=C
. This contradicts the maximality of
\tau^{\prime}
.
∎

The following result is our first important step toward semantic reasoning about decompositions. Intuitively, we show that the set of contexts that are available at every node is “decreasing”, in a sense that becomes precise in
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease.

Lemma 76

(Decompositions are Decreasing)
.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)

and a node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{B}]
, then the following hold.

1.

For every
C\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x0)
there exists
C^{\prime}\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)
such that
C^{\prime}\approx C
(i.e., they are
unravel equivalent
as per
\cref
def:equiv unravel).

2.

For every
C\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x1)
we have
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\cdot C\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)

Proof.

1.

Let
C\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x0)
. By
\cref
def:next contexts there exists a partial decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
with
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x0}}\tau^{\prime}
such that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)=C
(and in particular
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}
). There are now two possibilities: either the decomposition is BT or LR. More precisely, either
\tau^{\prime}.x01@u\to x1
(BT) or
\tau^{\prime}.x00@u\to x1
(LR). In the former, we can rotate to TB by
\cref
lem:rotation BT to TB to get
\tau^{\prime\prime}
with
\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=C
, and in the latter we can rotate to RL with
\cref
lem:rotation LR to RL to get
\tau^{\prime\prime}
with
\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=C[u\mapsto\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x1)]
, and notice that
\mathsf{lab}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x00))(u)=\square_{\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x1)}
, so
\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\approx C
. Either way, we obtain a partial decomposition
\tau^{\prime\prime}
such that
\tau^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
and for
C^{\prime}=\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)
we have
C^{\prime}\approx C
and
C^{\prime}\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)
.

2.

Assume
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x1)\neq\emptyset
. Let
C\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x1)
. By
\cref
def:next contexts there exists a partial decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
with
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x1}}\tau^{\prime}
such that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)=C
. We now have only one possible setting, namely TB. By
\cref
lem:rotation TB to BT there is a partial decomposition
\tau^{\prime\prime}
such that
\tau^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\cdot C=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\cdot C
. In particular,
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\cdot C\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)
.

∎

The decrease along the decomposition directly translates to the images under a morphism. Indeed, recall that for a morphism
\varphi
, if
C\approx C^{\prime}
then
\varphi(C)=\varphi(C^{\prime})
(as defined in
\cref
sec:toolbox for free algebras). We thus have the following.

Corollary 77
.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
, a node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{B}]
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\to V
, then the following hold.

1.

\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x0)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]

2.

\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x1)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]

A particularly useful application of
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease is when
V
has a zero, as follows.

Corollary 78
.

Consider a semigroup
V
with a zero
\mathbb{0}\in V
, a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow V
,
Then for every
x\leq y\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
we have

\mathbb{0}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,y)]\implies\mathbb{0}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]

Intuitively, we use this in
\cref
sec:decomposition theorems,sec:main theorem bound to “decompose away” all the
\mathbb{0}
contexts from a forest, and then we are guaranteed that
\mathbb{0}
does not occur in the next available contexts.

Recall from
\cref
lem:no inherited decomposition is stable that decompositions without inherited references are stable, which is a desirable property.
In the following we show that minimality in fact entails that there are no inherited references.
Intuitively, this corresponds to the intuition that inherited references correspond to “bad” factorization: plucking out a sub-forest, and later on plucking a parent of the sub-forest – this kind of factorization is not minimal.
Importantly, this proof relies on our framework of tree rotations (
\cref
sec:rotations).

Lemma 79

(No Inherited References in Factorization-Minimal Decomposition)
.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
that is minimal below some node
x
(with respect to
\widetilde{V},B
and a factorization set
S
, as per
\cref
def:min dec), then there are no inherited references below
x
, as per
\cref
def:decomp references.

Proof.

Notice that if
\tau
is minimal below
x
then it is also minimal below
z
for every
x\leq z
.
We proceed by way of contradiction: assume that
\tau
is minimal below
x
and that there is an inherited reference
y1@*\to z1
below
x
. In particular, we can assume without loss of generality that
y1@*\to x1
(since
\tau
is minimal below
z
as well, so we can assume
x=z
).

We now take
\tau,y,x
to be minimal with this property. That is, there is no smaller
y
for which this holds, where “smaller” is by the standard order on
\mathbb{N}^{*}
, in
any
decomposition
\tau
.

By the minimality of
y
, we can write
y=x0^{n}
for some
n>0
. Indeed, if
y
is obtained by a path with a right-child, then this right child also has a reference to
x1
, contradicting the minimality of
y
.

Denote
C_{x}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)
, and
C_{y}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y1)
, we now split to cases.

•

If
n=1
: this puts us under the conditions of
\cref
lem:rotation BT to TB (i.e., a BT decomposition). We therefore have
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=C_{x}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)\cdot C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)
(in particular
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}C_{x}
) and there exists
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
with
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)
and

\begin{split}&\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x1)=C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1))\\
&\approx C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)(\square_{\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)})=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x01)\end{split}

By the definition of a minimal decomposition (
\cref
def:min dec), we have

\mathtt{Factor}(\tau,x0)=(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01),\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x01))\in S
.
Overall we have that

\mathtt{Factor}(\tau^{\prime},x)=(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01),C_{link}(\tau,x01,x1)(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1)))\in\mathtt{NxtFctrs}(\tau,f)\cap S

This, however, contradicts the minimality of
\tau
: we could have replaced
C_{x}
with the smaller
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)
. So we are done.

•

If
n>1
: let
m=n-1
and consider
C^{\prime}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x0^{m}1)
(i.e., the context just above
y
, see
\cref
fig:LR to RL in MinLemma). We first claim that
x0^{n}0@*\to x0^{m}1
. Indeed, by
\cref
def:decomp references we have
x0^{n}@*\to x0^{m}1
, and there are now two options, either
x0^{n}1@*\to x0^{m}1
(again contradicting the minimality of
n
since we can use
x0^{m}
as
x
, reducing back to the
n=1
case), or we do not have such a reference, giving the desired
x0^{n}0@*\to x0^{m}1
.

This now puts us in the premise of
\cref
lem:rotation LR to RL, so we can apply an LR to RL rotation (see
\cref
fig:LR to RL in MinLemma). Intuitively, following the rotation we obtain an inherited reference
x0^{m}1@*\to x1
, contradicting the minimality of
n
(since
m<n
).

Figure 18
:
LR to RL rotation for
\cref
lem:min factorization dec no references. The dashed lines represent references. The rotation is at the subtree rooted at
x0^{m}
(the highlighted region).

Importantly, however, it is not clear that the rotated tree is minimal. We therefore need to dive into some more details. Denote
h_{00}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0^{n}0)
,
h_{1}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0^{m}1)
and
h_{01}=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0^{n}1)
. By
\cref
lem:rotation LR to RL there is a partial decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x0^{m}}}\tau
with
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x0^{m}1)=h_{01}
and
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x0^{m}1)\approx\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x0^{n}1)
, thus by the stability of
S
we get
\mathtt{Factor}(\tau^{\prime},x0^{m})\in\mathtt{NxtFctrs}(\tau,x0^{m})\cap S
.
In order to reason about the trees in their most “generic way” (in particular, so they can be expanded to a minimal decomposition), we “fold” everything below
h_{1},h_{00}
and
h_{01}
as follows. Denote

\tau_{1}=\tau[x0^{m}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\to\Upsilon(h_{1})\\
00\to\Upsilon(h_{00})\\
01\to\Upsilon(h_{01})\end{array}\right]\text{ and }\tau_{2}=\tau^{\prime}[x0^{m}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\to\Upsilon(h_{01})\\
00\to\Upsilon(h_{00})\\
01\to\Upsilon(h_{1})\end{array}\right]

The definition of
\tau^{\prime},\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
puts us in the premise of
\cref
lem:parallel factor map on
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
, so we get
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{1},01,\epsilon)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau_{2},1,\epsilon)
. Since
\mathtt{AncEmb}
is “well behaved” by
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding not affected by ancestor,lem:ancestor embedding not affected by incomparable,lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references and since
\tau.x0^{n}1@u\to x1
for some
u
, we have that
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C_{x})=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,x0^{n}1,x)(u)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},x0^{m}1,x)(u)
.

We are now ready to consider a minimal decomposition: let
\tau^{\prime\prime}
be a minimal decomposition such that
\tau^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x0^{m}}}\tau
and such that
\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x0^{m}1)\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x0^{m}1)
(which exists since
\tau^{\prime}
is a partial decomposition). By the ordering of the contexts, it follows that
\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x0^{m}1)
(which is plucked out at
x0^{m}
leaving
\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x0^{m}1)
) includes the sub-forest
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(x0^{m}1)
, as the latter leaves the larger
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x0^{m}1)
. In particular,
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(C_{x}))
is in the image of
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime\prime},x0^{m}1,x0^{m})
. Again by
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references this implies that
\tau^{\prime\prime}.x0^{m}1@*\to x1
, which is a contradiction to the minimality of
n
(since
\tau^{\prime\prime}
is a minimal decomposition, and
m<n
).

Since both cases reach a contradiction, we conclude that there are no inherited references below
x
.
∎

We now wish to obtain an analogue of
\cref
lem:min factorization dec no references to stable context sets. Fortunately, this follows immediately: if a decomposition is minimal below
x
with respect to a stable context set
S
, then it is also minimal below
x
with respect to the stable factorization set
S\times\widetilde{H_{A}}
, and therefore we can apply
\cref
lem:min factorization dec no references.

Corollary 80

(No Inherited References in Context-Minimal Decomposition)
.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
that is minimal below some node
x
(with respect to
\widetilde{V},B
and a stable context set
S
, as per
\cref
def:min dec), then there are no inherited references below
x
, as per
\cref
def:decomp references.

15
General Decomposition

We are now equipped with a robust notion of binary decomposition for forests, and have a versatile toolbox for manipulating such decompositions. For comparison, in the classical setting of words (rather than forests), everything we develop thus far are trivial observations about concatenation.

At the heart of Simon’s decomposition lie the
Idempotent nodes
(denoted
\mathfrak{I}
-nodes), which we are yet to define. These capture the plucking of several sub-forests simultaneously, where each is mapped to the same idempotent element.
In the setting of trees, defining such decomposition is (unsurprisingly) involved. Moreover, we introduce another type of nodes:
Centipede nodes
(denoted
\mathfrak{C}
-node). The latter are a convenient technicality, and we elaborate on them in the following.

Recall that in the setting of words (with respect to a morphism
\varphi
), an idempotent node labeled
x
with children labeled
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
means that
\varphi(x)=\varphi(x_{1})=\ldots=\varphi(x_{k})=e
for some idempotent element
e
. Intuitively, we can think of this node as “hiding” a binary decomposition of
x
to the leaves
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
, such that at each node in the binary decomposition we have the image
e
under
\varphi
. The latter is the intuition for our
\mathfrak{I}
-nodes: they hide a binary decomposition whose contexts are all mapped to an idempotent element.
Crucially, however, we also require that this decomposition is
stable
(
\cref
def:stable dec). This guarantees that all the linking contexts within the tree are also mapped to the same idempotent. In turn, this allows us to use developed for decompositions in
\cref
sec:dec lemmas as well as tree rotations.

The Centipede nodes are a novel technicality, which can be adopted to words (see
\cref
fig:centipede node). They are prompted by the following scenario (demonstrated over words, for simplicity). Consider a node labeled with a word
x
, and suppose we aim to initially decompose
x=uvw
. Using binary decompositions, we need to decompose
x=u\cdot(vw)
, and then
vw=v\cdot w
, in two “layers”. It is natural to ask why we do not directly allow a ternary node, to save this split.
The reason is that in the extreme case, one would decompose
x
directly to letters in a depth-1 tree, voiding the point of the decomposition theorem. More importantly, it does not really make sense to allow such a node, since given a single child of a multi-child node, we do not directly know where it “fits” within its parent node.

However, if allow multiple children and record an “index” where each child fits in its parent, then the problem above disappears: we cannot gain too much depth by decomposing to many children, as it would require a large index. Technically, this “index” is simply another word with placeholders for its children, i.e., a context over
\widetilde{V_{A}}
, and so the index itself needs to be further decomposed.
We remark that
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes can be avoided entirely (at a certain cost, see
\cref
lem:elimination of C nodes), but they are convenient, as we show in later proofs.

Figure 19
:
A Centipede decomposition, and the corresponding
\mathfrak{C}
-node. Note the bijection between the leaves. Also note there are no inherited references in the centipede. Finally, the node
x0000
is the “index” – it has holes corresponding to all other leaves.

We proceed with the formal definition of general decompositions, as an extension of Binary decompositions (
\cref
def:bin decomp).

Definition 81

(General Decomposition Tree)
.

Consider a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
, a finite semigroup
V
, and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\to V
.
A
general decomposition tree
with respect to
\varphi
is an
\widetilde{H_{A}}\times(\widetilde{V}\cup\{\square\})
-labeled tree domain
\tau=\langle\tau.\mathtt{Nodes},\tau.\mathsf{lab}\rangle\in(\widetilde{H_{A}}\times(\widetilde{V}\cup\{\square\}))\text{-{LTDS}}
equipped with three functions

•

\tau.\mathtt{forest}\colon\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\rightarrow\widetilde{H_{A}}

•

\tau.\mathtt{ctx}\colon\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\rightarrow(\widetilde{V}\cup\{\square\})

•

\tau.\mathtt{type}\colon\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\rightarrow\{\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{I},\mathfrak{C}\}

that satisfies the following conditions for every node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
.

1.

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{L}
then
x
is a
\mathfrak{L}
eaf
.

2.

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{B}
, then
x
is a
\mathfrak{B}
inary node
satisfying the conditions of
\cref
def:bin decomp.

3.

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{I}
then
x
is an
\mathfrak{I}
dempotent node
. Its set of children is unbounded (but finite), denoted
S=\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\cap(x\cdot\mathbb{N})
, and there is a binary decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
such that the following hold.

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
.

•

There exists an idempotent element
v\in V
(i.e.,
v^{2}=v
) such that for the stable context set
7
7

7

by the properties of idempotent elements, monoid morphism, and the artificial inclusion of
\{\square\}
, we get that
\widetilde{V_{x}}
is indeed a sub-monoid of
\widetilde{V_{A}}
.

\widetilde{V_{x}}=\varphi^{-1}[v]\cup\{\square\}
and the basis
\tau.\mathtt{forest}[S]
(i.e., the set of forests in the children of
x
), we have
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V_{x}},\tau.\mathtt{forest}[S])
.

•

There is a bijection
g\colon S\rightarrow\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
such that for every
y\in S
we have
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(g(y))
.

For an
\mathfrak{I}
-node, we set
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y)=\square
for every
y\in S
(intuitively, the context of
y
is of no consequence).

We denote the idempotent element
v
by
\mathtt{val}(\tau,x,\varphi)
.

4.

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{C}
then
x
is an
\mathfrak{C}
entipede node
. Its set of children is unbounded (but finite), denoted
S=\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\cap(x\cdot\mathbb{N})
, and there is a binary decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
such that the following hold:

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
.

•

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}\subseteq 0^{*}(\epsilon+1)
(see
\cref
fig:centipede node).

•

\tau^{\prime}
does not have inherited references (as per
\cref
def:decomp references).

•

There is a bijection
g\colon S\to\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
such that for every
y\in S
we have
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(g(y))

For a
\mathfrak{C}
-node, we also set
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y)=\square
for every
y\in S
.

Remark 82

(Full vs Stable Decomposition for
\mathfrak{I}
-Nodes)
.

In
\cref
def:general decomposition we require that an
\mathfrak{I}
-node has a corresponding full decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
(
\cref
def:full decomposition). In fact, we only need
\tau^{\prime}
to be a stable decomposition (
\cref
def:stable dec). Indeed, since we match the leaves of the decomposition, then we can essentially choose the basis to be exactly the forests in the leaves, rendering a given stable decomposition full.

Recall that a minimal decomposition does not have inherited references (
\cref
lem:min dec no references contexts) and that a decomposition without inherited references is stable (
\cref
lem:no inherited decomposition is stable). Thus, in light of
\cref
rmk:I node full decomp vs stable, we have the following.

Corollary 83
.

Consider a semigroup morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\to V
and an idempotent element
e\in V
.
In every decomposition
\tau
that is minimal below
x
with respect to
\widetilde{V},B
and the set
S=\varphi^{-1}[e]
(
\cref
def:min dec) we can be collapse the subtree
\tau[x]
to obtain a decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
such that
\tau^{\prime}[x]
is a single
\mathfrak{I}
-node (and in particular a subtree of depth
1
).

We are finally ready to define our main object of study, namely Full General Decomposition trees.

Definition 84

(Full General Decomposition)
.

A
full general decomposition
of a forest
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
over a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
, basis
B
, and morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow V
is a general decomposition
\tau
such that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=f
, for every node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
we have
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x)\in\widetilde{V}\cup\{\square\}
, and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)\in B
if
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{L}
.

The set of all full general decompositions over
\widetilde{V},B,f
and
\varphi
is denoted
\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi)
.

Notice that the requirements of a general decomposition in
\cref
def:general decomposition,def:full general decomposition are
local
, in the sense that they place restrictions for each node only on itself and its children. In particular, full general decompositions are closed under subtrees and under subtree-substitution, as follows.

Corollary 85
.

Consider a general decomposition
\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi)
and
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, then the following hold.

1.

\tau[x]\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x),\varphi)
.

2.

For a decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x),\varphi)
we have
\tau[x\mapsto\tau^{\prime}]\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi)
.

In contrast to
\cref
cor:general decomp closed substitution, if we consider
stability
(
\cref
def:stable dec) as a property, then it is not local, and the corollary does not hold for it (specifically, the second item fails).

As mentioned above, the parameterization of the decomposition by a basis is to facilitate the inductive proof. However, there is a canonical basis that is “elementary”, in some sense.

Definition 86

(Standard Basis)
.

we define the standard basis
B_{A}\subseteq\widetilde{H_{A}}
to be:

B_{A}=A\cup\{\sigma\square_{h}:\sigma\in A,h\in{H_{A}}\}\cup\{\square_{h_{1}}+\square_{h_{2}}:h_{1},h_{2}\in{H_{A}}\}

Remark 87

(
B_{A}
does not contain
\square_{h}
)
.

The observant reader may notice that elements of the form
\square_{h}
are not present in
B_{A}
. While these elements are “more elementary” than those in
B_{A}
, we observe that they (almost) never occur as leaves in a decomposition, since obtaining
\square_{h}
as a leaf means that the plucked subforest is the entire forest, which is precluded by
\cref
def:bin decomp (Condition
4
).

The only exception to this is when the subforest at the root is already
\square_{h}
. Since this is a degenerate case (and never occurs in the induction), we can safely disregard
\square_{h}
.

As the intuition suggests, every forest can be decomposed to the standard basis, regardless of the morphism. Moreover, such a decomposition can be achieved using binary decompositions (i.e., no
\mathfrak{I}
or
\mathfrak{C}
nodes). Intuitively, this is simple: inductively pluck subtrees according to the construction of the forest, until reaching a basis element.

Lemma 88
.

For every
f\in{H_{A}}

8
8

8

We could show this for
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
, with the exception of
f=\square_{h}
, see
\cref
rmk:standard basis no squares
there exists a decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V_{A}},B_{A})
such that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=f
.

In particular, for every morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\to V
, we have
\tau\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B_{A},f,\varphi)
.

Proof.

By induction on the generating sequence of
f
. Note that since we only have an inductive assumption on
{H_{A}}
, we take care not to introduce subtrees in
\widetilde{H_{A}}
, unless they are already basis elements.

Base:

f\in A
. Then, the singleton decomposition
\Upsilon(f)
is such a decomposition.

Composition step:

assume
f=\sigma(f^{\prime})
.
Consider the decomposition
\tau
with
\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}=\{\epsilon,0,1\}
such that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=f
and
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(1)=\sigma(\square)
.
Notice that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(0)=\sigma(\square_{f^{\prime}})\in B_{A}
and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(1)=f^{\prime}
.
By the inductive assumption, there is a corresponding decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V_{A}},B_{A})
for
f^{\prime}
. Then, the decomposition
\tau^{\prime\prime}=\tau[1\mapsto\tau^{\prime}]\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V_{A}},B_{A})
satisfies the requirements for
f
.

Addition step:

assume
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
.
Consider the decomposition
\tau
with
\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}=\{\epsilon,0,1,00,01\}
such that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=f
and
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(1)=f_{1}+\square
and
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(01)=\square+\square_{f_{2}}
.
Notice that
\tau
has no inherited references, and that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(1)=f_{2}
and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(01)=f_{1}
and
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(00)=\square_{f_{1}}+\square_{f_{2}}\in B_{A}
.
By the inductive assumption on
f_{1},f_{2}\in{H_{A}}
, there are corresponding decompositions
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V_{A}},B_{A})
. Then, we construct
\tau^{\prime}=\tau\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\mapsto\tau_{2}\\
01\mapsto\tau_{1}\end{array}\right]\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V_{A}},B_{A})
such that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=f
, as required.

∎

Recall that Simon’s factorization theorem places a bound on the depth of decomposition of any word over a given monoid (and morphism). We now introduce the notion of a depth bound for forests. For technical reasons that become apparent in the proof, we define this notion first for semigroups, and then specialize it to monoids and to the standard basis.

Definition 89

(Semigroup Decomposition Bound)
.

Consider a finite semigroup
S
, a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
, a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
, and a basis
B
.
The
decomposition bound
of
\varphi
over
B
is

\left\lVert\varphi,B\right\rVert\coloneqq\sup_{f\text{ is decomposable by }\widetilde{V}\text{ to }B}\min_{\tau\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi)}0pt(\tau)

(note that
\varphi
implicitly determines
\widetilde{V}
and
S
).
We further define:

•

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert=\sup_{B}\left\lVert\varphi,B\right\rVert
.

•

\left\lVert S\right\rVert=\sup_{\varphi:\widetilde{V}\to S}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert
(where
\varphi
is taken over any context set
\widetilde{V}
).

Thus, the decomposition depth of
\varphi
over basis
B
is the maximal depth of a minimal decomposition on any forest, restricted to forests that are actually decomposable. Note that this maximum (rather, supremum) might be infinite.

Remark 90

(Decomposability)
.

Note that the requirement of
f
to be decomposable by
\widetilde{V}
to
B
is independent of the morphism. Indeed, a forest is decomposable if and only if there is a binary decomposition (by e.g., replacing
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes and
\mathfrak{I}
-nodes with their respective binary sub-decompositions). Finally, note that binary decompositions are independent of the morphism.

Also note that for every decomposable forest we have
\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi)\neq\emptyset
, since binary decompositions are in particular general.

Then, the decomposition depth of a semigroup
S
is defined by the worst-case morphism and the standard basis.

As discussed above, while
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes are a convenient technicality, they are (in a sense) not necessary. In the following result we formalize this, and make explicit the blowup incurred by removing them. Note that our main result (
\cref
thm:main) states that there is a constant bound on the depth of decompositions. This therefore also holds without
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes, only with a higher (constant) bound.

Lemma 91
.

For every
\tau\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B_{A},f,\varphi)
there exists
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B_{A},f,\varphi)
with no centipede nodes, and such
0pt(\tau^{\prime})\leq 2^{0pt(\tau)+1}-2
.

Proof.

We start with a general observation about decompositions: consider some decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
for some
\widetilde{V},B
, and let
f=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
. Suppose that
f
has a leaf
x
labeled
\square_{h}
for some
h\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
. We can modify
\tau
by replacing
\square_{h}
with
h
in all occurrences that stem from
x
throughout
\tau
, thus obtaining a new decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
with
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}=\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
and the property that for every node
w
it holds that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(w)\approx\tau.\mathtt{forest}(w)
, and similarly
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(w)\approx\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(w)
and the reference structure is maintained.
Thus,
\tau^{\prime}
is “equivalent” to
\tau
up to unraveling.
The main difference is that the leaves of
\tau^{\prime}
are not not necessarily in
B
, but may contain instances of
h
.

This is illustrated in
\cref
fig:remove C node – the node
x000
has leaves labeled
\square_{h_{0}},\square_{h_{1}},\square_{h_{2}}
. In turn, the relevant subtrees are decomposed in the remaining subtrees. We can therefore replace each
\square_{h_{i}}
with the respective
h_{i}
, and concatenate the decomposition (as in
\cref
fig:remove C node 2).

Next, we recall the structure of the decomposition corresponding to a
\mathfrak{C}
-node (see
\cref
fig:centipede node): its root is
x
, and the nodes are
x0^{*}
and
x0^{*}1
up to some
x0^{k-1}1
and
x0^{k}
. Specifically, the bottom-most node (i.e., the “index” node) is
x0^{k}
. By the reference structure, we observe that
\mathtt{forest}(x0^{k})
has among its leaves
\square_{h_{0}},\ldots,\square_{h_{k-1}}
where
h_{i}=\mathtt{forest}(x0^{i}1)
.
In particular, if we replace each
\square_{h_{i}}
with
h_{i}
in
x0^{k}
, as described above, we would obtain exactly
\mathtt{forest}(x)
.
We henceforth assume that in the corresponding
\mathfrak{C}
-node, the child corresponding to
x0^{k}
is
x0
(recall that this
\mathfrak{C}
-node has
k+1
children).

(a)

\mathfrak{C}
-node.

(b)

Centipede.

(c)

Decomposition

Figure 20
:
A
\mathfrak{C}
-node with index
x000
(
\cref
fig:remove C node 0), and its corresponding Centipede (
\cref
fig:remove C node 1). In
\cref
fig:remove C node 2 we compose the decompositions and eliminate the
\mathfrak{C}
-node.

Our approach for eliminating
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes is now the following: consider a
\mathfrak{C}
node
x
in
\tau
. We consider the decomposition subtree
\tau_{x}
of
x0
(i.e., of the index node).
We then obtain from
\tau_{x}
an equivalent tree as described above: we replace each
\square_{h_{i}}
with
h_{i}
, and follow the same decomposition as
\tau_{x}
(which we can do by
\cref
cor:general decomp closed substitution).
Note the the new tree
\tau^{\prime}_{x}
has one fewer
\mathfrak{C}
-node than
\tau[x]
, and depth
lower
by
1
(since we remove the
\mathfrak{C}
-node and replace it with the child
x0
).
However, this decomposition no longer ends in
B_{A}
, but rather has in its leaves the trees
h_{i}
. We therefore need to continue decomposing these trees to reach
B_{A}
.
Note that for each
h_{i}
we already have a decomposition of depth at most
0pt(\tau[x])-1
, from the corresponding child of the
\mathfrak{C}
-node. Moreover, this decomposition has fewer
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes than
\tau
, so we can inductively obtain for it a decomposition without
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes (with a corresponding depth blowup).

Therefore, we now consider each leaf
y
of
\tau^{\prime}_{x}
, and decompose it to
B_{A}
(according to the cases of
\cref
def:standard basis), while accounting for the added depth. We denote by
D(n)
the depth of a decomposition without
\mathfrak{C}
nodes equivalent to a given a decomposition of depth
n
.

•

If
y\in B_{A}
, then no change is necessary.

•

If
y=\sigma(h_{i})
(for
\sigma\in A
), this case corresponds to a leaf labeled
\sigma(\square_{h_{i}})
of
\tau
.
We then factor it using with the context
\sigma(\square)
obtaining a bottom-child labeled
\sigma(\square_{h_{i}})\in B_{A}
and a right-child labeled
h_{i}
. We then plug the decomposition of
h_{i}
without
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes, obtaining a full general decomposition. The depth of this decomposition adds at most
D(n-1)+1
.

•

If
y=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(h_{i})+\square_{h}
for some
h
, this case corresponds to a leaf labeled
\square_{h_{i}}+\square_{h}
of
\tau
.
We then factor it using the context
\square+\square_{h_{i}}
, yielding a bottom-child leaf labeled
\square_{h_{i}}+\square_{h}
and a right-child labeled
h_{i}
, and again we plug the decomposition of
h_{i}
, adding depth at most
D(n-1)+1
.

•

If
y=\square_{h}+\tau.\mathtt{forest}(h_{i})
, this is analogous to the previous case.

•

If
y=\square_{\tau.\mathtt{forest}(h_{i})}+\square_{\tau.\mathtt{forest}(h_{j})}
, this is again analogous to the above, only now we need two factors: first use
\square+\tau.\mathtt{forest}(h_{i})
to pluck out
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(h_{i})
and leave residue
\square_{h_{i}}+\tau.\mathtt{forest}(h_{j})
, and then use
\square_{h_{i}}+\square
to pluck out
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(h_{j})
.
Overall this adds depth at most
D(n-1)+2
, due to the two binary nodes.

We then replace the original decomposition
\tau
, now with one fewer
\mathfrak{C}
-node, by a decomposition of depth at most
D(n-1)
.
Overall we obtain that a decomposition of depth
n
can be transformed to a decomposition without
\mathfrak{C}
nodes of depth at most
D(n-1)+D(n-1)+2
. Thus,
D(n)\leq 2D(n-1)+2
, and together with
D(0)=0
we get
D(n)\leq 2^{n+1}-2
, concluding the proof.
∎

16
Preliminaries Part II – Green’s Relations, Ideals and Congruences

In the remainder of the proof we make heavy use of tools from semigroup theory, specifically – Green’s relations and related concepts. We bring here several definitions and results. We remark that the results presented in this section are well known, but have several presentations. We include proofs to make the work self contained. We refer to e.g.,
[
12
,
9
]
for a more structured introductions.

For the remainder of this section, fix a finite semigroup
S
. We denote by
S^{1}
the monoid obtained by adding a unit element to
S
, if there is not one already.

16.1
Green’s Relations

A standard approach to studying a semigroup is by characterizing its
Green’s relations
, which we now recap.

Definition 92

(Green’s Relations)
.

Consider two elements
u,v\in S
.

Green’s pre-order relations
are the following:

•

u\leq_{\mathcal{J}}v
if
u\in S^{1}vS^{1}
.

•

u\leq_{\mathcal{L}}v
if
u\in S^{1}v
.

•

u\leq_{\mathcal{R}}v
if
u\in vS^{1}
.

•

u\leq_{\mathcal{H}}v
if
u\leq_{\mathcal{L}}v
and
u\leq_{\mathcal{R}}v
.

Every Green relation
G\in\{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{H}\}
induces an equivalence relation
\sim_{G}
by
u\sim_{G}v\iff u\leq_{G}v\wedge v\leq_{G}u
, and we denote by
[u]_{G}
the equivalence class of
u
, dubbed the
G
-class of
u
.
We abbreviate and write
u\mathcal{J}v
for
u\sim_{\mathcal{J}}v
(and similarly for
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{H}
).

When the semigroup
S
is not clear from context (in particular when several semigroups are involved) we write e.g.,
\mathcal{J}_{S}
to emphasize which semigroup the relation is over.

The results we present are corollaries and formulations of
Green’s lemma, the location lemma and the egg-box lemma

[
9
,
12
,
7
]
.
We bring the proofs for completeness, and since we tailor the formulations for our needs.

Lemma 93
.

For every
u,v\in S
the following hold.

1.

u\mathcal{J}uv\implies u\mathcal{R}uv
(i.e. if
S^{1}uS^{1}=S^{1}uvS^{1}
then
uS^{1}=uvS^{1}
).

2.

v\mathcal{J}uv\implies uv\mathcal{L}v
(i.e. if
S^{1}uS^{1}=S^{1}vuS^{1}
then
S^{1}u=S^{1}vu
).

Proof.

We prove the first implication, the second is analogous.
Assume
S^{1}uS^{1}=S^{1}uvS^{1}
, then there exist
x,y\in S^{1}
such that
x(uv)y=u
.
By
\cref
lem:idm power there exists an idempotent power
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(vy)^{n}=(vy)^{2n}
.
We now replace
u
in the expression
x(uv)y
by
x(uv)y
, and repeat this
n
times. That is, we have

u=xuvy=x(xuvy)vy=\ldots=x^{n}u(vy)^{n}=x^{n}u(vy)^{2n}=x^{n}u(vy)^{n}(vy)^{n}=u(vy)^{n}

We therefore get that
u=u(vy)^{n}=uv(y(vy)^{n-1})\in uvS^{1}
, so
uS^{1}\subseteq uvS^{1}
(indeed, we now have
u=uvw
for some
w\in S^{1}
, so for every
uz\in uS^{1}
we have
uz=uvwz\in uvS^{1}
).

The direction
uvS^{1}\subseteq uS^{1}
is trivial, giving us
uS^{1}=uvS^{1}
, as required.
∎

Lemma 94
.

Every
\mathcal{H}
-class that is closed under multiplication is a group.

Proof.

Consider an
\mathcal{H}
-class
H
of
S
that is closed under multiplication, i.e.,
H
is a semigroup.
By
\cref
lem:idm power there is an idempotent element
e\in H
.
We start by claiming that it is unique. Indeed, assume
e_{1},e_{2}\in H
are idempotent. By the definition of
\mathcal{H}
equivalence, we have
e_{2}\leq_{\mathcal{R}}e_{1}
and
e_{1}\leq_{\mathcal{L}}e_{2}
.
Thus, there exist
r_{1},l_{2}\in S^{1}
such that
e_{2}=e_{1}r_{1}
and
e_{1}=l_{2}e_{2}
. We then have

e_{1}=l_{2}e_{2}=l_{2}e_{2}e_{2}=e_{1}e_{2}=e_{1}e_{1}r_{1}=e_{1}r_{1}=e_{2}

We can therefore denote this unique idempotent by
e
.

We now claim that
e
is the unit of
H
. Consider some
x\in H
, then
e\mathcal{H}x
, so there are
y,z
such that
ey=x=ze
. It follows that

ex=eey=ey=x=ze=zee=xe

Finally, we show that every element
x\in H
has an inverse. Since every element has an idempotent power (
\cref
lem:idm power), there exists
i>1
such that
x^{i}=e
. Denote
x^{-1}=x^{i-1}
, then
x^{-1}x=xx^{-1}=x^{i}=e
.

We conclude that
H
is a group.
∎

A common practice when reasoning about semigroups is to look at an “atomic” kind of semigroups
[
12
]
, as follows.

Definition 95

(Simple,
\mathbb{0}
-simple and null semigroups)
.

A semigroup
S
is:

•

null
if there is a zero element
\mathbb{0}\in S
such that for every
a,b\in S
we have
ab=\mathbb{0}
.

•

simple
9
9

9

Despite the same terminology, this notion is different from simple
groups
.
if it is a single
\mathcal{J}
-class (i.e.,
u\mathcal{J}v
for all
u,v\in S
).

•

\mathbb{0}
-simple if it is not null
10
10

10

Note that the non-null requirement merely excludes the degenerate case of the semigroup
\{\mathbb{0},a\}
with
a^{2}=\mathbb{0}
.
, and it has a zero element
\mathbb{0}\in S
such that
S\setminus\{\mathbb{0}\}
is a single
\mathcal{J}
-class (note that
\{\mathbb{0}\}
is also a
\mathcal{J}
-class).

The following result conceptually states that in a simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple semigroup
S
, the
\mathcal{H}
class of
s\cdot t
is characterized by the
\mathcal{R}
-class of
s
and the
\mathcal{L}
-class of
t
, for every
s,t\in S
.

Lemma 96
.

Consider simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple semigroup
S
and elements
s,t\in S
.

1.

If
st=\mathbb{0}
then
s^{\prime}t^{\prime}=\mathbb{0}
for every
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}\mathcal{L}s
and
t^{\prime}\mathcal{R}t
.

2.

If
st\neq\mathbb{0}
then
s\mathcal{R}st
and
st\mathcal{L}t
.

Proof.

1.

By
\cref
def:green relations there are
l_{s},r_{t}\in S
such that
s^{\prime}=l_{s}\cdot s
and
t^{\prime}=t\cdot r_{t}
. Therefore,

s^{\prime}t^{\prime}=(l_{s}s)(tr_{t})=l_{s}\mathbb{0}r_{t}=\mathbb{0}

2.

Since
st\neq\mathbb{0}
, then by simplicity we have
s\mathcal{J}st\mathcal{J}t
. By
\cref
lem:J equivalence to one sided equivalence we obtain

s\mathcal{R}st
and
st\mathcal{L}t
.

∎

Intuitively,
\cref
lem:green lemma states that if
st=\mathbb{0}
, then
[st]_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathbb{0}
. Otherwise, we have

[st]_{\mathcal{H}}=\{v\mid st\mathcal{R}v\wedge st\mathcal{L}v\}=\{v\mid s\mathcal{R}v\wedge t\mathcal{L}v\}=[s]_{\mathcal{R}}\cap[t]_{\mathcal{L}}

16.2
Congruences and Ideals

A
congruence
on
S
is an
equivalence
relation
\cong\subseteq S\times S
that preserves the semigroup action.
That is, if
u_{1}\cong u_{2}
and
v_{1}\cong v_{2}
then
u_{1}\cdot v_{1}\cong u_{2}\cdot v_{2}
.
For a congruence, the
quotient
set
S/{\cong}
is a semigroup, with the operation
[u]_{\cong}\cdot[v]_{\cong}=[u\cdot v]_{\cong}
.
Then, the quotient map
\texttt{quo}_{\cong}
defined by

\texttt{quo}_{\cong}(u)=[u]_{\cong}
is a surjective semigroup morphism.

An
ideal
of
S
is a subset
I\subseteq S
that satisfies

S^{1}IS^{1}=I
.
Notice that every ideal
I\subseteq S
is in particular a sub-semigroup of
S
.

We present several results and definitions pertaining to ideals and congruences, used throughout the proof.

Definition 97

(
\mathbb{0}
-minimal ideal)
.

An ideal
I
is
\mathbb{0}
-minimal
if for every ideal
I^{\prime}
with
I^{\prime}\subsetneq I
it holds that
I^{\prime}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
.

Lemma 98
.

Consider a semigroup
S
and a
\mathbb{0}
-minimal ideal
I\subseteq S
such that
I^{2}=I
.
Then
I
is a simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple semigroup
(see
[
12
, 3.1.3]
).

Proof.

We show that all nonzero elements in
I
are
\mathcal{J}_{I}
equivalent.
Let
a\in I\setminus\{\mathbb{0}\}
(if
S
has a
\mathbb{0}
).
Notice that
S^{1}aS^{1}
is a non-zero ideal of
S
and
S^{1}aS^{1}\subseteq I
(since
a\in I
and
I
is an ideal).
By the minimality of
I
we have
S^{1}aS^{1}=I
. In addition, since
I^{2}=I
we also have
I^{3}=I
, and note that
IS^{1}=S^{1}I=I
since
I
is an ideal.
We therefore have:

I^{1}aI^{1}\subseteq S^{1}aS^{1}=I=I^{3}=I(S^{1}aS^{1})I=(IS^{1})a(S^{1}I)\subseteq I^{1}aI^{1}

and therefore
IaI=I
. Since this holds for any non-zero
a\in I
, we conclude that for any non-zero
a,b\in I
we have
I^{1}aI^{1}=I=I^{1}bI^{1}
, so
a\mathcal{J}_{I}b
, meaning
I
is simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple.
∎

Lemma 99
.

Consider a semigroup
S
and an ideal
I\subseteq S
.
Then
S
has a zero
\mathbb{0}_{S}
if and only if
I
has a zero
\mathbb{0}_{I}
. If these exist, then
\mathbb{0}_{I}=\mathbb{0}_{S}
.

Proof.

Assume
S
has a zero
\mathbb{0}_{S}
, then in particular
\mathbb{0}_{S}\in S^{1}IS^{1}=I
. Then, for every
a\in I
we have
a\mathbb{0}_{S}=\mathbb{0}_{S}a=\mathbb{0}_{S}
, so
\mathbb{0}_{S}
is also a zero of
I
.

Conversely, assume
I
has a zero
\mathbb{0}_{I}
, and let some
a\in S
.
Since
I
is an ideal, we have
a\mathbb{0}_{I},\mathbb{0}_{I}a\in I
. Since
\mathbb{0}_{I}
is a zero of
I
we have
a\mathbb{0}_{I}=\mathbb{0}_{I}a=\mathbb{0}_{I}
. But this holds for every
a\in S
, so
\mathbb{0}_{I}
is also a zero of
S
.
∎

An ideal
I\subseteq S
naturally induces an equivalence relation
\sim_{I}
by collapsing
I
to a single element, as follows.

Definition 100
.

Consider an ideal
I\subseteq S
, the
Rees Congruence
induced by
I
is
\sim_{I}
where for every
u,v\in S
we have
u\sim_{I}v
if
u=v
or
u,v\in I
.

In particular,
S/\sim_{I}=\{I\}\cup\{\{v\}\mid v\in S\setminus I\}
(see
\cref
rmk:identify singletons with elements Rees).

The following lemma justifies calling this relation a congruence.

Lemma 101

(Rees Congruence (
[
14
,
12
]
))
.

Consider an ideal
I\subseteq S
, then
\sim_{I}
is a congruence.

Proof.

Clearly
\sim_{I}
is an equivalence relation, as it is induced by the partition
\{\{v\}:v\notin I\}\cup\{I\}
of
S
.
We turn to show that it is a congruence. Let
u_{1}\sim_{I}u_{2}
and
v_{1}\sim_{I}v_{2}
.
We split to cases.

•

If
u_{1},v_{1}\notin I
then
u_{2}=u_{1}
and
v_{2}=v_{1}
, so
u_{1}\cdot v_{1}=u_{2}\cdot v_{2}
and in particular
u_{1}\cdot v_{1}\sim_{I}u_{2}\cdot v_{2}
.

•

If
u_{1},v_{1}\in I
then
u_{2},v_{2}\in I
. Since
I
is an ideal, we get that
u_{1}\cdot v_{1},u_{2}\cdot v_{2}\in I
, so
u_{1}\cdot v_{1}\sim_{I}u_{2}\cdot v_{2}
.

•

Otherwise, without loss of generality
u_{1}\in I,v_{1}\notin I
then
u_{2}\in I
and
v_{2}=v_{1}
. Again since
I
is an ideal we get that
u_{1}\cdot v_{1},u_{2}\cdot v_{2}\in I
and
u_{1}\cdot v_{1}\sim_{I}u_{2}\cdot v_{2}
.

∎

Remark 102
.

When considering the quotient map
\texttt{quo}_{\sim I}
, we often identify the quotient set with
\{I\}\cup S\setminus I
. That is, we identify singletons
\{v\}
with
v
for convenience.
We abbreviate
S/\sim_{I}
to
S/I
and
\texttt{quo}_{\sim I}
to
\texttt{quo}_{I}
.

Lemma 103
.

Consider an ideal
I\subseteq S
and
a,b\in S\setminus I
, then (as per
\cref
rmk:identify singletons with elements Rees)

a\mathcal{J}_{S}b\iff a\mathcal{J}_{S/I}b
.

Proof.

We show that
a\leq_{\mathcal{J}_{S}}b\iff a\leq_{\mathcal{J}_{S/I}}b
and obtain the result by symmetry.

•

If
a\leq_{\mathcal{J}_{S}}b
, then there exist
x,y\in S
such that
a=xby
(as elements of
S
).
Since
I
is an ideal and
a\notin I
we have that
x,y\notin I
. Thus, we still have
a=xby
as elements of
S/I
, so
a\leq_{\mathcal{J}_{S/I}}b
.

•

If
a\leq_{\mathcal{J}_{S/I}}b
: There exists
x,y\in S/I
such that
a=xby\in S/I
.
Because
a\notin I
(and in particular
a\neq\mathbb{0}_{S/I}
) we get that
x,y\neq\mathbb{0}_{S/I}
.
Thus we have
x,y\in S\setminus I
, and we have
a=xby
as elements in
S
, so
a\leq_{\mathcal{J}_{S}}b
.

∎

17
Decomposition Theorems

This section focuses on constructing full general decompositions for certain building-block cases, that are used for the general setting in
\cref
sec:main theorem bound.
Specifically, in
\cref
sec:unambiguity we define our central restriction on morphisms, namely
\mathcal{R}
-alignment
.
We then turn to establish concrete bounds on decomposition depth for the cases of groups (
\cref
sec:group decomposition), simple and
\mathbb{0}
-simple semigroups (
\cref
sec:simple semigroups, which is our most elaborate result), and null groups (
\cref
sec:null semigroups).

17.1
\mathcal{R}
-Alignment

We start by presenting the technical restriction on morphisms that enables our main result. We follow the precise definition with some discussion on its intuitive meaning.

Definition 104

(
\mathcal{R}
-aligned Forests and Morphisms)
.

Consider a semigroup
S
, a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
.
A forest
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned
over
\varphi
if
for every
f_{1},f_{2}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
and
C_{1}\neq C_{2}\in\widetilde{V}
such that
f=C_{1}(f_{1})=C_{2}(f_{2})
and
\varphi(C_{1})\mathcal{J}\varphi(C_{2})
there exist
r\in S
such that
r\mathcal{J}\varphi(C_{1})
and
\varphi(C_{2})=\varphi(C_{1})\cdot r
.

A morphism
\varphi
is said to be
\mathcal{R}
-aligned
if all forests
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
are
\mathcal{R}
-aligned.

Note that since the requirement in the definition holds for every
C_{1},C_{2}
, it is in fact symmetric: there is also
r^{\prime}\mathcal{J}\varphi(C_{1})
such that
\varphi(C_{1})=\varphi(C_{2})\cdot r^{\prime}
.

Intuitively, the definition states the following: suppose we decompose
f
in two different ways (by plucking out different sub-forests), such that the remaining contexts are
\mathcal{J}
-equivalent, then not only are these contexts also
\mathcal{R}
-equivalent, but they are
\mathcal{R}
-equivalent using multiplicands that are within the same
\mathcal{J}
-class (hence the term
\mathcal{R}
-aligned).

The semigroup-savvy reader may treat this condition as
\varphi(C_{1})\mathcal{J}_{S}\varphi(C_{2})\implies\varphi(C_{1})\mathcal{R}_{S_{J}}\varphi(C_{2})
where
S_{J}
is the
principal factor

[
12
]
of
J
.

The applications of
\mathcal{R}
-alignment become clear in
\cref
sec:simple semigroups. However, in order to apply it we need to ensure that it survives through some manipulations of morphisms.
The first such result shows that composition with a quotient morphism retains
\mathcal{R}
-alignment.

Lemma 105
.

Consider an
\mathcal{R}
-aligned morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
and an ideal
I\subseteq S
with the quotient morphism
\texttt{quo}_{I}\colon S\rightarrow S/I
.
Then
\texttt{quo}_{I}\circ\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S/I
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned.

Proof.

Following
\cref
def:unamb property, let
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
and
C_{1},C_{2}\in\widetilde{V}
such that
f=C_{1}(f_{1})=C_{2}(f_{2})
for some
f_{1},f_{2}
.
Let
u_{1}=\texttt{quo}_{I}(\varphi(C_{1})),u_{2}=\texttt{quo}_{I}(\varphi(C_{2}))
be the respective images under
\texttt{quo}_{I}\circ\varphi
, and assume that
u_{1}\mathcal{J}_{S/I}u_{2}
.
We prove that the condition holds by cases.

•

If
u_{1}=u_{2}=\mathbb{0}_{S/I}
(i.e., the zero element of
S/I
), take
r=\mathbb{0}
then clearly
r\mathcal{J}_{S/I}
and we have
u_{2}=u_{1}\cdot r=\mathbb{0}_{S/I}
).

•

Otherwise,
u_{1},u_{2}\neq\mathbb{0}_{S/I}
(since
[\mathbb{0}]_{\mathcal{J}}=\{\mathbb{0}\}
for every semigroup). Recall by
\cref
def:rees that
\mathbb{0}_{S/I}=I
, and in light of
\cref
rmk:identify singletons with elements Rees we can therefore (abusively
11
11

11

Strictly speaking, we have
u_{1}=\{v_{1}\},u_{2}=\{v_{2}\}
with
v_{1},v_{2}\in S\setminus I
and
v_{1}\mathcal{J}_{S}v_{2}
.
) write
u_{1},u_{2}\in S\setminus I
and by
\cref
lem:rees J we have
u_{1}\mathcal{J}_{S}u_{2}
.
Since
\varphi
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned, there exists
x\mathcal{J}_{S}u_{1}
such that
u_{2}=u_{1}x
.
Since
I
is an ideal it holds that
x\notin I
(otherwise we would have
u_{2}\in I
). Thus, we can write
u_{2}=u_{1}x
as elements of
S/I
.
Similarly, since
x\notin I
then by
\cref
lem:rees J we have
x\mathcal{J}_{S/I}u_{1}
.

We conclude that
\texttt{quo}_{I}\circ\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S/I
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned.
∎

The second result shows that restriction to the inverse image of a (
\mathbb{0}
-minimal) ideal retains
\mathcal{R}
-alignment.

Lemma 106
.

Consider an
\mathcal{R}
-aligned morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
, and some
\mathbb{0}
-minimal ideal
I\subseteq S
(
\cref
def:0min ideal).
Denote
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}[I]
and
\varphi^{\prime}\coloneqq\varphi|_{\widetilde{V^{\prime}}}\colon\widetilde{V^{\prime}}\rightarrow I
, then
\varphi^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned.

Proof.

Let
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
and
C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2}\in\widetilde{V^{\prime}}
such that
f=C_{1}(f_{1})=C_{2}(f_{2})
for some
f_{1},f_{2}
.
Let
u_{1}=\varphi^{\prime}(C_{1}),u_{2}=\varphi^{\prime}(C_{2})\in I
and assume
u_{1}\mathcal{J}_{I}u_{2}
.
Since
I\subseteq S
then in particular
u_{1}\mathcal{J}_{S}u_{2}
, so we can use the
\mathcal{R}
-alignment of
\varphi
and obtain
r\in S
with
r\mathcal{J}_{S}u_{1}
such that
u_{2}=u_{1}r
.
Moreover, we have
r\in S^{1}u_{1}S^{1}
, but since
u_{1}\in I
and
I
is an ideal, it follows that
r\in I
.

It remains to show that
r\mathcal{J}_{I}u_{1}
. Recall that
[\mathbb{0}_{S}]_{\mathcal{J}}=\{\mathbb{0}_{S}\}
and therefore if
\mathbb{0}_{S}\in\{u_{1},u_{2},r\}
then
r=u_{1}=u_{2}=\mathbb{0}
and we are done. Thus, assume they are not
\mathbb{0}_{S}
, so by
\cref
lem:0I we have that
r,u_{1},u_{2}\in I\setminus\{\mathbb{0}_{I}\}
(if
I
has a zero).

Observe that
I
is not a null semigroup: indeed, if
I^{2}=\{\mathbb{0}_{I}\}
, then since
u_{2}=u_{1}r
it follows that
u_{2}=\mathbb{0}_{I}
, contradicting our assumption above.
It follows that
I^{2}\subseteq I
is a non-zero ideal, and by the
\mathbb{0}
-minimality of
I
we have
I^{2}=I
. Thus, by
\cref
lem:min ideal is simple we have that
I
is simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple. In either case, since
u_{1},u_{2},r\neq\mathbb{0}_{I}
we have that
u_{1}\mathcal{J}_{I}u_{2}\mathcal{J}_{I}r
(as
I\setminus\{\mathbb{0}_{I}\}
is a single
\mathcal{J}
-class, by
\cref
def:simple zsimple null semigroup).
We conclude that
\varphi^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned.
∎

17.2
Decomposition over a Group

Our first base case is when the semigroup under consideration is actually a group. The overall approach to obtain a bound on the decomposition depth is to consider a node
x
in a decomposition
\tau
, and reason about
|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]|
, i.e., the number of possible images of the next decomposition step.
Recall that in
\cref
lem:decomposition decreasing,cor:decomp semantically decrease we show that this set is, in a sense, decreasing. We now specialize the arguments of
\cref
lem:decomposition decreasing to groups to obtain a sharper result.

Lemma 107
.

Consider a group
V
, a
binary
decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
, and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\to V
.
For every
x\leq y\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
we have:

|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,y)]\leq|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]|

Proof.

We prove the claim for
y=x\sigma
(i.e.,
y
is a direct child of
x
), and the claim follows by induction.
Most of the hard work is already done in
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease. We use it and split to cases based on
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
.

•

If
y=x0
then by
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease we have
\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\tilde{V},B,y)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\tilde{V},B,x)]
. Thus,

|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\tilde{V},B,y)]|\leq|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\tilde{V},B,y^{\prime})]|
.

•

If
y=x1
then by
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease we have
\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y))\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,y)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]

Since
V
is a group, the function
v\mapsto\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y))\cdot v
is a bijection, and therefore

|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\tilde{V},B,y)]|=|\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(y))\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,y)]|\leq|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]|

In both cases we have
|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\tilde{V},B,y)]|\leq|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\tilde{V},B,x)]|
, and we conclude the lemma by induction.
∎

We proceed with the main technical part of group decomposition. Here, we bound the depth of a minimal decomposition starting from some node
x
of an existing binary partial decomposition. The bound is given in terms of
|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]|
.

Lemma 108

(Decomposition Over a Group)
.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow V
where
V
is a group.
Let
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
and denote
n=|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]|
, then there exists a full general decomposition
\tau_{x}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x),\varphi)
such that
0pt(\tau_{x})\leq 2n
.
Moreover, if
\mathbb{1}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
then
0pt(\tau_{x})\leq 2n-1
.

Proof.

We start with an intuitive overview of the proof.
If we can decompose
x
with a context mapped to the neutral group element
\mathbb{1}
, then we decompose using
\mathbb{1}
“as much as possible”, and fold this structure to a single
\mathfrak{I}
-node (i.e., of depth 1).
We show that in this case, the remaining trees have a smaller image under
\varphi
, and we can apply induction.

If we cannot decompose
x
with
\mathbb{1}
, we decompose it instead to a centipede of maximal depth, which folds to a single
\mathfrak{C}
-node. Using our framework of minimal decompositions (
\cref
def:min dec) and the group structure, we show that we can apply the induction hypothesis to the children of this
\mathfrak{C}
-node.
We now turn to the precise details.

We prove the claim by induction on
n=|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]|
.
The base case is
n=0
, so
\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]=\emptyset
. By
\cref
lem:no next contexts implies B this implies
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)\in B
. In particular, the singleton decomposition
\tau_{x}
satisfies
\tau_{x}=\Upsilon(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x),\varphi)
and
\delta(\tau_{x})=0\leq 2\cdot 0
, as required. Note that the important part here is that this decomposition indeed has its only leaf in
B
.

Assume correctness for all
m<n
, we prove for
n
. We split to two cases, depending on whether
\mathbb{1}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
, where
\mathbb{1}
is the neutral element of the group
V
.
Intuitively, in both cases we consider a decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
with two properties: first, that
\tau^{\prime}
is
minimal below
x
in the sense of
\cref
def:min dec.

Second, that
\tau^{\prime}
is maximal in the sense that none of its leaves can be further decomposed while maintaining the minimality requirements.
We then use the case assumptions to fold the node at
x
to either an
\mathfrak{I}
-node or a
\mathfrak{C}
-node, while using the properties of groups in order to apply the induction hypothesis below these new nodes.

First case:
\mathbb{1}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
.

Consider the context set
S=\varphi^{-1}[\mathbb{1}]
. Intuitively, we try to decompose
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
as much as possible using only contexts in
S
, i.e., that map to
\mathbb{1}
. We then “fold” this partial decomposition into a single
\mathfrak{I}
-node, and show that we can apply the induction hypothesis to its leaves.

Let
\tau^{\prime}
be a decomposition such that
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
is minimal below
x
with respect to
\widetilde{V},B
and
S
. Note that such a decomposition exists, since if we fold
x
to a singleton in
\tau
(namely take
\tau[x\mapsto\Upsilon(x)]
), then
\tau[x\mapsto\Upsilon(x)]\leq\tau
and thus it is also a partial decomposition, and it is trivially minimal below
x
and
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}
.

Consider some node
y\in\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}
below
x
(i.e.,
x<y
) and write
y=y^{\prime}\sigma
. By
\cref
lem:decomposition decreasing we have the following:

•

If
y=y^{\prime}0
then
\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y^{\prime})]
.

•

If
y=y^{\prime}1
then
\overbrace{\underbrace{\varphi(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(y^{\prime}1))}_{=\mathbb{1}}\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y^{\prime}1)]}^{\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]}\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y^{\prime})]
where
\varphi(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(y^{\prime}1))=\mathbb{1}
since all the decomposition below
x
is mapped to
\mathbb{1}
.

By induction over
y
this shows that
\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
for every
y
in
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}
below
x
.

Now consider the set
L=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]\cap(x\cdot\mathbb{N}^{*})
of leaves in
\tau^{\prime}
below
x
. Since
\tau^{\prime}
is assumed to be maximal to extension, for every leaf
z\in L
we have
\mathbb{1}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,z)]
(otherwise we could extend
\tau^{\prime}
).
Combining this with the containment showed above, we conclude that for every
z\in L
we have

|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,z)]|\leq|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x)]\setminus\{\mathbb{1}\}|=n-1
.
Again, we rely here on the fact that
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x)=\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)
since
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}
.

We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis to every leaf
z
and obtain a full general decomposition
\tau_{z}
of
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(z)
such that
0pt(\tau_{z})\leq 2(n-1)
.

By the definition of an
\mathfrak{I}
-node (
\cref
def:general decomposition) and since
\tau^{\prime}
is minimal by assumption (
\cref
def:min dec) we can apply
\cref
cor:min to I and “fold”
\tau^{\prime}[x]
to a single
\mathfrak{I}
-node (since
\mathbb{1}
is idempotent). We can then plug each decomposition
\tau_{z}
to each child of the
\mathfrak{I}
-node as per
\cref
cor:general decomp closed substitution and obtain a full general decomposition
\tau_{x}
of
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
of height at most
1+2(n-1)=2n-1
, as required.

Second case:
\mathbb{1}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
.

Intuitively, in this case we decompose
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
as much as possible using a centipede structure, that we can then “fold” into a single
\mathfrak{C}
-node. The challenge is then to show that we can apply the induction hypothesis to its leaves, which requires some case analysis.

Let
\tau^{\prime}
be a decomposition with the following properties:

•

\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}
,

•

\tau^{\prime}
is minimal below
x
,

•

\tau^{\prime}[x]
is a centipede (i.e.,
\tau^{\prime}[x].\mathtt{Nodes}\subseteq 0^{*}\cdot(\epsilon+1)
), and

•

\tau^{\prime}[x]
is maximal with these properties with respect to extension.

As in the previous case, such a decomposition exists, since
\tau[x\mapsto\Upsilon(x)]
satisfies the first three properties.

Consider the set
L=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]\cap(x\cdot\mathbb{N}^{*})
of leaves in
\tau^{\prime}
below
x
. By the definition of a
\mathfrak{C}
node (
\cref
def:general decomposition) we can fold
\tau^{\prime}[x]
so that its root is a
\mathfrak{C}
node whose children are bijectively matched with
L
.
We now claim that every leaf
y\in L
has a full general decomposition of depth at most
2n-1
.
To show this, we split to cases.

•

If
y\in x\cdot 0^{*}
(i.e.,
y
is the “down-most” leaf), then by the maximality of
\tau
we have that
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)=\emptyset
(otherwise we could extend
\tau^{\prime}[x]
). By
\cref
lem:no next contexts implies B we have
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y)\in B
, so we can use the depth
0\leq 2n-1
singleton decomposition
\Upsilon(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y))\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y),\varphi)
.

•

If
y=x\cdot 0^{k}1
for some
k
and
\mathbb{1}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]
, then by
\cref
lem:group next ctx cardinality decreasing we have
|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]|\leq n
. We can now apply the first case of the induction (which is already proved), and obtain a full general decomposition
\tau_{y}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y),\varphi)
with
0pt(\tau_{y})\leq 2n-1
, as required.

•

If
y=x\cdot 0^{k}1
for some
k
and
\mathbb{1}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]
, we use the centipede structure as follows. By
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease we have

\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x0^{k})]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x)]
. By the assumption that
\mathbb{1}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
and since
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x)=\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)
we have
\mathbb{1}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x)]
and by the containment above we get
\mathbb{1}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x0^{k})]
. Denote
c_{y}=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(y)
, then we have
c_{y}\neq\mathbb{1}
.

Again by
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease we have
c_{y}\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x0^{k})]
. We claim that
c_{y}\notin c_{y}\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]
. Indeed, this follows from the fact that the action of a non-unit group element is a
derangement
.
More precisely, if (by way of contradiction) we have
c_{y}\in c_{y}\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]
, then since
V
is a group, by multiplying with
c_{y}^{-1}
from the left, we have
\mathbb{1}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]
, contradicting the assumption of this subcase. On the other hand, group action is bijective, so

|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]|=|c_{y}\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]|
.
Combining the results above, we have:

\begin{split}&|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]|=|c_{y}\cdot\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,y)]|\leq|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x0^{k})]\setminus\{c_{y}\}|\\
&=|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x0^{k})]|-1\leq|\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},\widetilde{V},B,x)]|-1=n-1\end{split}

Thus, by the induction hypothesis, there is a full general decomposition
\tau_{y}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y),\varphi)
with
0pt(\tau_{y})\leq 2(n-1)\leq 2n-1
, as required.

We can then plug each decomposition
\tau_{y}
to each child of the
\mathfrak{C}
-node as per
\cref
cor:general decomp closed substitution and obtain a full general decomposition
\tau_{x}
of
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
of height at most
1+2n-1=2n
, as required.
∎

We can now conclude our decomposition bound for groups.

Lemma 109
.

For every finite group
V
it holds that
\left\lVert V\right\rVert\leq 2|V|-1
.

Proof.

Fix
\widetilde{V}
,
B
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\to V
.
We show that every forest
f
that is decomposable by
\widetilde{V}
to
B
over
\varphi
has a decomposition tree
\tau
of depth at most
2|V|-1
.

Since
f
is decomposable, in particular its singleton decomposition satisfies
\Upsilon(f)\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
. We now split to cases.

•

If
\mathbb{1}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\Upsilon(f),\widetilde{V},B,\epsilon)]
, then by
\cref
lem:group decomp technical there is a decomposition tree
\tau
of
f
with

0pt(\tau)\leq 2|\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\Upsilon(f),\widetilde{V},B,\epsilon)|-1\leq 2|V|-1
, where the last inequality is because
\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\Upsilon(f),\widetilde{V},B,\epsilon)\subseteq V
.

•

If
\mathbb{1}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\Upsilon(f),\widetilde{V},B,\epsilon)]
then again by
\cref
lem:group decomp technical there is a decomposition tree
\tau
of
f
with

0pt(\tau)\leq 2|\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\Upsilon(f),\widetilde{V},B,\epsilon)|\leq 2(|V|-1)\leq 2|V|-1
, where the last inequality is because

\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\Upsilon(f),\widetilde{V},B,\epsilon)\subseteq V\setminus\{\mathbb{1}\}
.

∎

17.3
Decomposition over Simple Semigroups

In the proof of Simon’s factorization theorem for words, a central part of the proof proceeds by splitting a word
w=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n}
into sections where each section starts in some fixed
\mathcal{R}
-class and ends in a fixed
\mathcal{L}
-class. Intuitively, the way to obtain such a decomposition is to fix a pair
(L,R)
of an
\mathcal{R}
-class and an
\mathcal{L}
-class, respectively, and find all occurrences in the word of the immediate consecutive pair
LR
,

For forests, this approach becomes much more complicated. In fact, it generally breaks down (as we show in
\cref
sec:lower bound). This is the technical reason for the introduction of
\mathcal{R}
-aligned morphisms (
\cref
def:unamb property).

The analogue of the
LR
pairs for forests is to consider two consecutive plucked subforests. This is a natural generalization of
\mathtt{NxtCtx}
(
\cref
def:next contexts), where we consider the available
two
contexts to be plucked from a node
x
(i.e., to
x1
and
x11
).

Definition 110
.

Consider a decomposition tree
\tau
over
\widetilde{V}
and
B
, and let
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
. We define

\begin{split}\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)=\{(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1),\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)):&\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B),\\
&x11\in\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}\}\end{split}

For a morphism
\varphi
, we denote

\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]=\{(\varphi(C_{1}),\varphi(C_{2})):(C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)\}

When
\widetilde{V}
and
B
are clear for context, we write e.g.,
\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,x)
.

Using our framework of rotations (
\cref
sec:rotations), we can show that
\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}
is well-behaved (“decreasing”, in a sense), similarly to
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease.

Lemma 111
.

Consider a decomposition
\tau
, a binary node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{B}]
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow V
, then the following hold.

1.

\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x0)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]

2.

If
(u,v)\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x1)]
then
(\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))\cdot u,v)\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]

Proof.

We prove each part of the lemma separately.

Item 1.

Let
(C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,x0)
, we show that
(\varphi(C_{1}),\varphi(C_{2}))\in\varphi(\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,x))
.
We assume without loss of generality that
\tau
attains these contexts, i.e.,
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)=C_{1}
and
\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x011)=C_{2}
(otherwise replace
\tau
with an extension below
x
that does attain them).
Intuitively, in this case we want to “raise” the branch
x01\to x011
so that it starts from
x
instead of
x0
. We do this by applying either an LR to RL rotation or a BT to TB rotation, depending on the references to
x1
(similarly to
\cref
lem:decomposition decreasing).

•

If
\tau.x00@*\to x1
, then we are under the reference structure of LR to RL rotation (
\cref
lem:rotation LR to RL). Specifically, there is a decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
with the following properties:

1.

\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}

2.

\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)\approx\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)
, and in particular
\varphi(C_{1})=\varphi(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))
.

3.

\tau^{\prime}[x1]=\tau[x01]
, and in particular
C_{2}\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime}[x01])
(since
\tau^{\prime}[x1]
is decomposed identically to
\tau[x01]
, i.e., with
C_{2}
):

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)=\tau^{\prime}[x1].\mathtt{ctx}(1)=\tau[x01].\mathtt{ctx}(1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x011)=C_{2}

We readily obtain
(\varphi(C_{1}),\varphi(C_{2}))\in\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau^{\prime},x)
, and since
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau
it follows that
(\varphi(C_{1}),\varphi(C_{2}))\in\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,x)
, as required.

•

If
\tau.x01@*\to x1
, then we are under the reference structure of BT to TB rotation. We follow the same logic as above.
By
