\cref
lem:rotation BT to TB there is a decomposition
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
with the following properties:

1.

\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}

2.

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x01)=C_{1}

6

3.

\tau^{\prime}[x10]=\tau[x01]
.

We therefore have that
C_{2}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x011)\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},x10)
, so
\varphi(C_{2})\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},x10)]
.
Recall that by
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease that
\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},x10)]\subseteq\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,x)]
, so
\varphi(C_{2})\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau^{\prime},x10)]
.
Combining this with the second property above gives the desired

(\varphi(C_{1}),\varphi(C_{2}))\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
.

Item 2.

Let
(C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,x1)
, we show that
(\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))\cdot C_{1},C_{2})\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,x)]
.
We assume without loss of generality that
x111\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
(otherwise we can extend
\tau
by factoring
x1
by
C_{1}
and then
x11
by
C_{2}
).
We therefore have
C_{1}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)
and
C_{2}=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x111)
.

We now apply a TB to BT rotation. By
\cref
lem:rotation TB to BT there is
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
with the following properties:

1.

\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}

2.

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\cdot\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\cdot C_{1}
.

3.

\tau^{\prime}[x1]=\tau[x11]
.

By Property 3, we have that
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(x11)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x111)=C_{2}
.
We therefore have
(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1)\cdot C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau^{\prime},x)
, and we have the desired
(\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))\cdot\varphi(C_{1}),\varphi(C_{2}))\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,x)]
(again, since
\tau\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus x}}\tau^{\prime}
).
∎

Recall that our main interest in two consecutive plucked subforests is when these form a specific pair of
\mathcal{R}
- and
\mathcal{L}
-classes under the morphism
\varphi
. We now define this notion, as a sort of projection of
\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}
.

Definition 112

(LR)
.

Consider a partial decomposition
\tau
over
\widetilde{V},B
, a node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\to S
.
We define
\mathtt{LR}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x,\varphi)\coloneqq\{([u]_{\mathcal{L}},[v]_{\mathcal{R}}):(u,v)\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]\}
.

We write
\mathtt{LR}(\tau,x)
or
\mathtt{LR}(x)
when the remaining elements are clear from context.

Remark 113
.

Notice that by
\cref
def:green relations for every
(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\cdot)

l\cap r
is an
\mathcal{H}
-class of
S
.
In addition, because the quotient sets
S/\mathcal{L},S/\mathcal{R}
are partitions, we get that for
(l_{1},r_{1}),\neq(l_{2},r_{2})\in\mathtt{LR}(\cdot)
we have that
l_{1}\cap r_{1}
and
l_{2}\cap r_{2}
are mutually exclusive.

We now show that
\mathtt{LR}
is “decreasing”, in the spirit of
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease, but specialized for the case of simple semigroups.

Lemma 114

(
\mathtt{LR}
is decreasing)
.

Consider a simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple semigroup
S
, a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
. Let
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
and consider two nodes
x\leq y
in
\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
.
If
\mathbb{0}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
, then
\mathtt{LR}(y)\subseteq\mathtt{LR}(x)
.

Proof.

Let
\tau,x
as in the statement of the lemma, with
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{B}
.
We show that the inclusion
\mathtt{LR}(y)\subseteq\mathtt{LR}(x)
holds for each of the two children of
x
(i.e.,
y\in\{x0,x1\}
). The result then follows by induction for any descendant
y
.

1.

\mathtt{LR}(x0)\subseteq\mathtt{LR}(x)
: follows directly from
\cref
lem:decomp two semantically decreasing.

2.

\mathtt{LR}(x1)\subseteq\mathtt{LR}(x)
:
Let
(u,v)\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x1)]
. By
\cref
lem:decomp two semantically decreasing we have
(\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))\cdot u,v)\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau.\widetilde{V},B,x)]
.
Due to the assumption that
\mathbb{0}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\widetilde{V},B,x)]
we know that
\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))\cdot u\neq\mathbb{0}
.
Now, by
\cref
lem:green lemma, we have that
\varphi(\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(x1))\cdot u\mathcal{L}u
, and we have that
([u]_{\mathcal{L}},[v]_{\mathcal{R}})\in\mathtt{LR}(x)
as required.

∎

Before proceeding with the decomposition bound, we present a technical lemma that essentially lifts the “egg-box” structure of the non-zero
\mathcal{J}
-class (
\cref
lem:green lemma) in a simple semigroup to the specific setting of
\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\cdot)
.

Lemma 115
.

Let
S
be simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple and consider a stable context set
\widetilde{V}
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
and
\tau\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
such that
\mathbb{0}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\epsilon)]
. For every
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
and
(u_{1},u_{2})\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,x)]
the following hold.

1.

u_{1}\neq\mathbb{0}
,
u_{2}\neq\mathbb{0}
, and
u_{1}u_{2}\neq\mathbb{0}
.

2.

u_{1}u_{2}\in[u_{1}]_{\mathcal{R}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{L}}

3.

([u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}})\cdot([u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}})=([u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}})
(note the difference in
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
and
u_{1},u_{2}
from Item 2).

Proof.

1.

By the assumption that
\mathbb{0}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,\epsilon)]
and
\cref
cor:top zeros we immediately get
u_{1}\neq\mathbb{0}
and
u_{2}\neq\mathbb{0}
.
By
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease we have
u_{1}\cdot u_{2}\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau,x)]
, thus also
u_{1}\cdot u_{2}\neq\mathbb{0}
.

2.

By
\cref
lem:green lemma we now have
u_{1}u_{2}\mathcal{R}u_{1}
and
u_{1}u_{2}\mathcal{L}u_{2}
, so
u_{1}u_{2}\in[u_{1}]_{\mathcal{R}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{L}}
.

3.

Let some
a,b\in[u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}}
then by
\cref
lem:green lemma and because
u_{1}u_{2}\neq\mathbb{0}
we have
ab\neq\mathbb{0}
. Identically to Item 2 above, this implies (by
\cref
lem:green lemma) that
ab\in[b]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[a]_{\mathcal{R}}
, but
[a]_{\mathcal{R}}=[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}}
and
[b]_{\mathcal{L}}=[u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}
, so
ab\in[u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}}
.
We therefore have that
[u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-class that is closed under multiplication. In particular, we have the inclusion
([u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}})\cdot([u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}})\subseteq([u_{1}]_{\mathcal{L}}\cap[u_{2}]_{\mathcal{R}})
.

By
\cref
lem:H class is a group it follows that this class is a group, and therefore has an identity element, giving us the converse inclusion and concluding equality.

∎

We now proceed to prove the existence (and construct) a bounded general decomposition over simple (or
0
-simple) semigroup. Technically, this is one of the most involved proofs in this paper.

Lemma 116
.

Consider a simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple semigroup
S

and an
\mathcal{R}
-aligned morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
, Then
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert\leq 2|S|+1
.

Moreover, every decomposable forest
f
admits a full general decomposition
\tau
(with
0pt(\tau)\leq 2|S|+1
) that has at most one
\mathbb{0}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node and no other
\mathbb{0}
contexts.

Proof.

Various elements of the proof are depicted in
\cref
fig:simple semigroup proof.

Figure 21
:
Illustration of the decomposition constructed in
\cref
lem:simple decomp.

Consider a forest
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
that is decomposable by
\widetilde{V}
to
B
.
We start by getting rid of
\mathbb{0}
, in case
S
is
\mathbb{0}
-simple. To this end, we first decompose
f
as much as we can with contexts mapped to
\mathbb{0}
, so that the remaining leaves cannot be decomposed with
\mathbb{0}
. We then fold this decomposition to a single
\mathfrak{I}
-node.

Formally, let
\tau_{0}
be a maximal-depth binary decomposition that is minimal (below
\epsilon\in\tau_{0}.\mathtt{Nodes}
, as per
\cref
def:min dec) with respect to the stable context set
\varphi^{-1}[\mathbb{0}]
(see the top triangle
\tau_{0}
in
\cref
fig:simple semigroup proof).
Notice that
\Upsilon(f)
is such a minimal decomposition, so a maximal-depth one exists.
By
\cref
cor:top zeros we have that every leaf
x\in\tau_{0}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
cannot be decomposed further by zero contexts (that is,
\mathbb{0}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau_{0},x)]
).
Since
\tau_{0}
is minimal below
\epsilon
, then by
\cref
cor:min to I we can collapse
\tau_{0}
to a single
\mathfrak{I}
-node. We refer to this collapsed tree (of depth
1
) as
\tau^{\prime}_{0}
.

Now consider some leaf
x
of
\tau_{0}
, and recall that
\mathbb{0}\notin\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau_{0},x)]
, we construct a bounded general decomposition for
\tau_{0}.\mathtt{forest}(x)
by induction over
|\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y)|
(where
\tau
is some extension of
\tau_{0}
and
y\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
). In
\cref
fig:simple semigroup proof the node
x
is depicted as a node
y
, which conceptually can occur further below
\tau_{0}
, in an extension
\tau
.
We prove that for every such
\tau,y
there is a full general decomposition for
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y)
with no
\mathbb{0}_{S}
valued
\mathfrak{I}
-nodes, and of depth at most

1+\sum_{(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y))}2\cdot|l\cap r|

Base case:
\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y)=\emptyset
.

In this case, we have in particular that
\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,y)=\emptyset
, i.e.,
y
cannot be decomposed by plucking two consecutive subforests to obtain a
y11
node. By
\cref
lem:decomp two semantically decreasing it follows that
\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau,y0)=\emptyset
as well, and by induction this holds for any descendant
y0^{k}
. We can therefore take a maximal down-centipede
\tau_{y}
rooted at
y
, and we have that all its leaves are in
B
(as per
\cref
lem:no next contexts implies B, otherwise we could obtain a
11
child).
We can therefore fold
\tau_{y}
to a single
\mathfrak{C}
-node, and plug it into
\tau[y]
(as per
\cref
cor:general decomp closed substitution), thus obtaining a decomposition of depth
1
.

In
\cref
fig:simple semigroup proof the base case would end after the highlighted centipede rooted at
y
.

Induction Step:
\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y)\neq\emptyset
.

Fix some
(A_{L},A_{R})\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y)
.
Initially, we decompose
y
with a down-centipede
\tau_{L}
of maximal depth under several constraints (see the highlighted centipede in
\cref
fig:simple semigroup proof):

\tau_{L}
Constraint 1

Denote
\tau_{1}\coloneqq\tau[y\mapsto\tau_{L}]\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
, then
\tau_{1}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
,
That is, we must keep the decomposition partial even when plugged into
\tau
, so as not to eliminate the ability to extend it to a full decomposition.

\tau_{L}
Constraint 2

\tau_{1}
is minimal below
y
with respect to the set
\varphi^{-1}[A_{L}]
. That is, we only use elements in
A_{L}
for the decomposition. This is an analogue of marking the prefixes in a certain
\mathcal{L}
-class in Simon’s original setting of words.

\tau_{L}
Constraint 3

For every
z1\in\tau_{L}.\mathtt{Nodes}
we have
A_{R}\cap\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau_{1},yz1)]\neq\emptyset
. That is, every right child can be further decomposed by
A_{R}
. Again, this is analogous to the setting of words, where we look for consecutive
(A_{L},A_{R})
prefixes.

Notice that there exists such a decomposition, since the singleton decomposition
\Upsilon(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y))
satisfies the constraints.
In addition, observe that for the down-most leaf
y0^{k}
of
\tau_{L}
it holds that
(A_{L},A_{R})\notin\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{1},y0^{k})
, by the maximality of
\tau_{L}
.

We proceed to the
A_{R}
part of the decomposition, as follows.
For every node
z1\in\tau_{L}.\mathtt{Nodes}
, we obtain a maximal-depth decomposition
\tau_{z}
under the following constraints (see
\tau_{z}
rooted at
z1
in
\cref
fig:simple semigroup proof):

\tau_{z}
Constraint 1

Denote
\tau_{2}\coloneqq\tau_{1}[yz1\mapsto\tau_{z}]
, then
\tau_{2}\in\mathtt{PartialDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
. As above, we need to maintain decomposability.

\tau_{z}
Constraint 2

\tau_{2}
is minimal below
yz1
with respect to the following stable
factorizations set
(see
\cref
def:min dec):

\begin{split}F=\{(C,f_{1}):&\varphi(C)\in A_{L}\cap A_{R},\text{ and }\\
&\forall C^{\prime}\in\widetilde{V}\text{ s.t }\varphi(C^{\prime})\neq\mathbb{0},\text{ if }\mathtt{unravel}(f_{1})=C^{\prime}(f^{\prime\prime})\text{ for some $f^{\prime\prime}$, then }\varphi(C^{\prime})\in A_{R}\}\end{split}

Intuitively, the condition about
f_{1}
in
F
states that any decomposition of
f_{1}
(or rather, its unraveling) into
C^{\prime}(f^{\prime\prime})
must map
C^{\prime}
to
A_{R}
(unless it is
\mathbb{0}
).
Notice that by
\cref
lem:unr mor and the fact that
F
is defined with a condition on
\mathtt{unravel}(f_{1})
, it follows that
F
is indeed a
stable
factorizations set.

Again we have that
\Upsilon(\tau_{L}.\mathtt{forest}(z1))
is such a decomposition, and therefore a maximal-depth decomposition exists.

We now turn to reason about the leaves of
\tau_{z}
. Consider a leaf
w
of
\tau_{z}
and let
f=\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(w)
be its forest. We claim that the following holds:

\forall C^{\prime}\in\widetilde{V}\text{ s.t }\varphi(C^{\prime})\neq\mathbb{0},\text{ if }\mathtt{unravel}(f)=C^{\prime}(f^{\prime\prime})\text{ for some $f^{\prime\prime}$, then }\varphi(C^{\prime})\in A_{R}

(1)

That is, we claim that all the leaves satisfy the condition in Constraint 2 of
\tau_{z}
. We separate to cases.

•

If
w=w^{\prime}1
, i.e.,
w
is a right-child, the condition follows immediately by Constraint 2. Indeed, the factorization of
w^{\prime}
satisfies the constraint, and therefore
(\tau_{z}.\mathtt{ctx}(w^{\prime}1),\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(w^{\prime}1))\in F
, but
\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(w^{\prime}1)=f
.

•

If
w=w^{\prime}0
, i.e.,
w
is a down-child, we start by showing that there exists
some
context
C
with
\varphi(C)\in A_{R}
and
f^{\prime}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
such that
f=C(f^{\prime})
.
Since
\tau_{z}
is a binary decomposition, then (by
\cref
def:bin decomp)
f=\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(w^{\prime}0)=\tau_{z}.\mathtt{ctx}(w^{\prime}1)(\square_{\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(w^{\prime}1)})
. Denote
C=\tau_{z}.\mathtt{ctx}(w^{\prime}1)
, then by Constraint 2 we have
\varphi(C)\in A_{L}\cap A_{R}
, and thus
\varphi(C)\in A_{R}
.

Denote
f^{\prime}=\square_{\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(w^{\prime}1)}
, we conclude that
f=C(f^{\prime})
and
\varphi(C)\in A_{R}
(and
\varphi(C)\neq\mathbb{0}
).

We now proceed to show
\cref
eq:condition simple factorization, i.e., that the above holds for
every
context.
By
\cref
lem:unr mor we have
\mathtt{unravel}(f)=\mathtt{unravel}(C)(\mathtt{unravel}(f^{\prime}))
. Consider some context
C^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
such that
\varphi(C^{\prime})\neq\mathbb{0}
and
\mathtt{unravel}(f)=C^{\prime}(f^{\prime\prime})
.
Since
S
is simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple then by definition we have
\varphi(C^{\prime})\mathcal{J}\varphi(C)
. We now use the
\mathcal{R}
-alignment of
\varphi
(
\cref
def:unamb property) to conclude that
\varphi(C^{\prime})\mathcal{R}\varphi(C)
, so
\varphi(C^{\prime})\in A_{R}
. Since this is true for every such
C^{\prime}
, we conclude the property in
\cref
eq:condition simple factorization.

•

If
w=\epsilon
, i.e., is the root (and only node) of
\tau_{z}
, then observe that
f=\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(w)=\tau_{1}.\mathtt{forest}(yz1)
(recall that
\tau_{z}
is a decomposition of
\tau_{1}.\mathtt{forest}(yz1)
, in this case – a singleton decomposition).
The constraints on
\tau_{L}
give us that
A_{R}\cap\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau_{1},yz1)]\neq\emptyset
, and in particular there exists some context
C
with
\varphi(C)\in A_{R}
such that
f=C(f^{\prime})
for some forest
f
. By the same logic as the previous case, we have that
\varphi(C)\neq\mathbb{0}
. We can now repeat the same arguments as the previous case, and conclude the property in
\cref
eq:condition simple factorization.

Having established
\cref
eq:condition simple factorization for the leaf
w
, we now show that
(A_{L},A_{R})\notin\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{2},yz1w)
(recall that
\tau_{2}
is obtained by plugging in
\tau_{z}
in
\tau_{1}
at
yz1
).
Assume by way of contradiction that
(A_{L},A_{R})\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{2},yz1w)
, so there are
(C_{L},C_{R})\in\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau_{2},yz1w)
with
\varphi(C_{L})\in A_{L}
and
\varphi(C_{R})\in A_{R}
. By
\cref
lem:nts props we also have
\varphi(C_{L})\neq\mathbb{0}
and
\varphi(C_{R})\neq\mathbb{0}
(since
\mathbb{0}
was decomposed maximally already in
\tau_{1}
). In addition, by
\cref
lem:unr mor we have
\mathtt{unravel}(f)=\mathtt{unravel}(C_{L})(f^{\prime})
for some
f^{\prime}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
.
This places us under the conditions of
\cref
eq:condition simple factorization, and therefore
\varphi(C_{L})\in A_{R}
.
Thus,
\varphi(C_{L})\in A_{L}\cap A_{R}
.

We claim that we can now factor
w
by the context
C_{L}
while maintaining Constraint 2 of
\tau_{z}
, in contradiction to the maximality of
\tau_{z}
.
Indeed,
C_{L}\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau_{2},yz1w)
, and factoring with
C_{L}
yields
f=C_{L}(f^{\prime\prime})
for some forest
f^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
that is decomposable by
C_{R}
where
\varphi(C_{R})\in A_{R}
(due to the assumption that
(C_{L},C_{R})\in\mathtt{NxtTwoCtx}(\tau_{2},yz1w)
).
We can therefore write
\mathtt{unravel}(f^{\prime\prime})=\mathtt{unravel}(C_{R})(f^{\prime\prime\prime})
with
\varphi(C_{R})\in A_{R}
.
We now follow an identical argument as above: since
S
is simple, every context
C^{\prime}_{R}
that satisfies
\mathtt{unravel}(f^{\prime\prime})=\mathtt{unravel}(C^{\prime}_{R})(g)
satisfies
\varphi(C_{R})\mathcal{J}\varphi(C^{\prime}_{R})
. Since
\varphi
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned (
\cref
def:unamb property), we have
\varphi(C_{R})\mathcal{R}\varphi(C^{\prime}_{R})
, so
\varphi(C^{\prime}_{R})\in A_{R}
, and we conclude Constraint 2, leading to a contradiction.
Thus,
(A_{L},A_{R})\notin\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{2},w)
for every leaf
w
.

We now obtain a new decomposition
\tau_{3}
by plugging in
\tau_{z}
at
yz1
for every right-child
z1\in\tau_{L}.\mathtt{Nodes}
. That is,
\tau_{3}=\tau_{1}[yz1\mapsto\tau_{z}]
(for every
z1
). By the above, every leaf
w
of
\tau_{3}
below
y
(i.e.,
y\leq w
) satisfies that
(A_{L},A_{R})\notin\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{3},w)
.
Combining this with
\cref
lem:lr decreasing, we have that
|\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{3},w)|<|\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y)|
(recall that our induction is on
|\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y)|
). By the induction hypothesis we obtain for every leaf
w
a general decomposition
\tau^{\prime}_{w}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau_{3}.\mathtt{forest}(w),\varphi)
of depth at most
\sum_{(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{3},w))}2|l\cap r|+1
.

Intuitively, all the work thus far was in order to apply the induction hypothesis. In the remainder of the proof, we fold the prefix of the tree to a lower depth. This is done separately for
\tau_{z}
and for
\tau_{L}
, where the latter is simply folding
\tau_{L}
to a
\mathfrak{C}
-node. We start with the former.

Fix
z1\in\tau_{L}.\mathtt{Nodes}
and consider
\tau_{z}
. Recall that by Constraint 2 of
\tau_{z}
we have that all contexts in
\tau_{z}
map to
A_{L}\cap A_{R}
.
By
\cref
lem:nts props we have that
(A_{L}\cap A_{R})\cdot(A_{L}\cap A_{R})=(A_{L}\cap A_{R})
, so
A_{L}\cap A_{R}
is a sub-semigroup of
S
, with the following implications:

•

The set
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}[A_{L}\cap A_{R}]
is a sub-semigroup of
\widetilde{V_{A}}
, and therefore a stable context set.

•

By
\cref
rmk:lr
A_{L}\cap A_{R}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-class of
S
. Therefore, by
\cref
lem:H class is a group we have that
A_{L}\cap A_{R}
is a group.

We now replace
\tau_{z}
with a (possibly very different) decomposition
\tau^{\prime}_{z}
over the same
leaves
, using the fact that all the contexts in
\tau_{z}
come from a group, giving us the bound from
\cref
lem:group decomposition bound.

Denote by
B^{\prime}=\{\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(w):w\in\tau_{z}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]\}
the forests in the leaves of
\tau_{z}
. We claim that
\tau_{L}.\mathtt{forest}(z1)=\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
is decomposable by the (stable) context set
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}
to the basis
B^{\prime}
.
The nontrivial part required to establish decomposability is ensuring that all the contexts in
\tau_{z}
are in
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}
, but also all the
linking contexts
(
\cref
def:linking context). While the former holds by definition, the latter requires
\cref
lem:min factorization dec no references: since
\tau_{z}
is minimal with respect to the factorization set
F
, it follows that it does not have inherited references. Hence, all the linking contexts are in
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}
by the fact that
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}
is stable, so we conclude decomposability.

We restrict
\varphi
to this context set by defining
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi|_{\widetilde{V^{\prime}}}\colon\widetilde{V^{\prime}}\rightarrow A_{L}\cap A_{R}
. Then, by
\cref
lem:group decomposition bound there exists a general decomposition
\tau^{\prime}_{z}
of
\tau_{z}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
with leaves in
B^{\prime}
, contexts in
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}
, according to
\varphi^{\prime}
, and with depth at most
2|A_{L}\cap A_{R}|-1
.

Notice that by the definitions of
\varphi^{\prime},\widetilde{V^{\prime}}
as restrictions of
\varphi
and
\widetilde{V}
we have that
\tau^{\prime}_{z}
is also a general decomposition with respect to
\widetilde{V}
and
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
.
Moreover, since
\mathbb{0}_{S}\notin A_{L}\cap A_{R}
, then
\tau^{\prime}_{z}
has no
\mathbb{0}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-nodes.

We are finally ready to complete the induction step, namely constructing a full general decomposition for
\tau,y
:

•

For every leaf
w
of
\tau_{z}
, let
\tau^{\prime}_{w}
be the decomposition for
w
obtained by the induction hypothesis.
Notice that
\tau^{\prime}_{w}
has no
\mathbb{0}
valued
\mathfrak{I}
node (by induction).

•

For every leaf
z1
of
\tau_{L}
, let
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{z}
be the corresponding decomposition above. To each leaf of
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{z}
with forest
f
we plug a corresponding
\tau^{\prime}_{w}
that decomposes
f
(note that each leaf of
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{z}
indeed has such
\tau^{\prime}_{w}
, but there may be leaves in
\tau^{\prime}_{w}
that do not appear in
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{z}
, since these two decompositions may differ, but share the basis
B^{\prime}
).

•

We fold
\tau_{L}
to a single
\mathfrak{C}
-node
\tau_{C}
, and plug each
\tau_{z}
to its corresponding leaf in
\tau_{C}
(again, there is a bijection between the leaves of
\tau_{L}
and
\tau_{C}
).

The resulting resulting decomposition has no
\mathbb{0}
valued
\mathfrak{I}
nodes, and has depth bounded by:

\begin{split}&\underbrace{1+\sum_{(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y)\setminus\{(A_{L},A_{R})\}}(2\cdot|l\cap r|)}_{\text{inductive $\tau^{\prime}_{w}$}}+\underbrace{2\cdot|l\cap r|)-1}_{\tau^{\prime\prime}_{z}}+\underbrace{1}_{\tau_{C}}\\
&=1+\sum_{(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau,y)}(2\cdot|l\cap r|)\end{split}

as required to conclude the induction.

We can now complete the proof of the lemma by bounding the overall depth of the decomposition.
Observe that by
\cref
rmk:lr we have that
\{l\cap r:(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{0},x)\}
are mutually exclusive, and thus

1+\sum_{(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{0},x)}2|l\cap r|=1+2\sum_{(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{0},x)}|l\cap r|=1+2\left|\bigcup_{(l,r)\in\mathtt{LR}(\tau_{0},x)}l\cap r\right|\leq 2|S\setminus\{\mathbb{0}\}|+1

We therefore have the following:

•

If
S
is
\mathbb{0}
-simple, the above is bounded by
2(|S|-1)+1=2|S|-1
. Then, the full decomposition includes the initial single
\mathfrak{I}
-node
\tau^{\prime}_{0}
, so the depth is at most
2|S|
.

•

If
S
is simple, the above is bounded by
2|S|+1
(and there is no initial
\mathfrak{I}
-node).

We conclude that for
\mathcal{R}
-aligned
\varphi
and simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple
S
we have
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert\leq 2|S|+1
. Moreover, in both cases we have at most a single
\mathbb{0}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node as the initial node (namely
\tau^{\prime}_{0}
).
∎

17.4
Decomposition over null semigroups

The final “atomic” base case in our decomposition is that of
null semigroups
(
\cref
def:simple zsimple null semigroup), i.e., those where
st=\mathbb{0}
for every
s,t\in S
. We show that such semigroups always allow decompositions of depth at most 2.

Lemma 117
.

Consider a null semigroup
S
, then
\left\lVert S\right\rVert\leq 2
.

Moreover, every decomposable forest
f
admits a full general decomposition
\tau
(with
0pt(\tau)\leq 2
) that has at most one
\mathbb{0}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node and no other
\mathbb{0}
contexts.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
be a morphism and consider a forest
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
that is decomposable by
\widetilde{V}
to
B
.
Let
\tau_{0}
a maximal-depth decomposition rooted with
f
, which is minimal (as per
\cref
def:min dec) with respect to the context set
\varphi^{-1}[\mathbb{0}]
, and notice that this is a stable context set (
\cref
def:stable context set).
By
\cref
cor:min to I we can fold
\tau_{0}
to a single
\mathfrak{I}
node
\tau^{\prime}_{0}
.
Moreover, by
\cref
cor:top zeros and the maximality of
\tau_{0}
, we have that
\mathbb{0}\notin\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau_{0},y)
for every leaf
y\in\tau_{0}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
.

We now show that each leaf
y
can be extended to a full decomposition of depth 1 using a single
\mathfrak{C}
-node. Consider a leaf
y
.
By
\cref
lem:min full dec there is
\tau_{0,y}\in\mathtt{FullDec}(\widetilde{V},B)
that is minimal below
y
and
\tau_{0,y}\mathrel{\equiv_{\!\setminus y}}\tau_{0}
.
Let
\tau_{1,y}=\tau_{0,y}[y]
by the subtree of
\tau_{0,y}
rooted at
y
; we claim that
\tau_{1,y}
is a down centipede.
Indeed, assume by way of contradiction that some node
z\in\tau_{1,y}.\mathtt{Nodes}
has
z11\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
, then
\tau_{1,y}.\mathtt{ctx}(z11)\in\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau_{1,y},z1)
.
Since
S
is a null semigroup, then by
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease we have that

\mathbb{0}=\varphi(\tau_{1,y}.\mathtt{ctx}(z1))\cdot\varphi(\tau_{1,y}.\mathtt{ctx}(z11))\in\varphi[\mathtt{NxtCtx}(\tau_{1,y},z)]
, but (again by
\cref
cor:decomp semantically decrease) this contradicts the maximality of
\tau_{0}
(which guaranteed that
\mathbb{0}
is not in
\mathtt{NxtCtx}
of any leaf).

We can therefore fold each
\tau_{1,y}
to a single
\mathfrak{C}
-node, yielding an overall decomposition that consists of a singe
\mathfrak{I}
-nodes whose leaves are single
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes, hence depth at most
2
, and indeed has at most single
\mathbb{0}
valued
\mathfrak{I}
node.
∎

18
A Bound on Decomposition Depth

We are now ready to prove our main result, namely that every
\mathcal{R}
-aligned morphism admits decompositions of bounded depth.

Theorem 118
.

Consider a finite semigroup
S
and an
\mathcal{R}
-aligned morphism
\varphi\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow S
, then
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert\leq 4|S|-3
.

Proof.

Let
f
be a decomposable forest.
The proof is by induction on the semigroup size
|S|
. More precisely, our induction hypothesis is that every decomposable forest has a full general decomposition over
\varphi
of depth at most
4|S|-3
such that the decomposition has at most one
\mathbb{0}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node and no other
\mathbb{0}
contexts. The proof has several base cases, corresponding to the decompositions of
\cref
sec:decomposition theorems.

Base case:
|S|=1
.

In this case,
S=\{\mathbb{1}\}
where
\mathbb{1}
is idempotent and is also the zero element of
S
. Therefore, every full decomposition of
f
that is minimal below
\epsilon
can be transformed to a single
\mathfrak{I}
-node. Since
f
is decomposable, then such a minimal decomposition exists, so we have
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert S\right\rVert\leq 1=4|S|-3
, and the single
\mathbb{0}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node requirement holds.

Base case:
S
is a null semigroup and
|S|>1
.

By
\cref
lem:null semigroup decomposable depth 2 we immediately have
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert S\right\rVert\leq 2\leq 4|S|-3
(as
|S|\geq 2
), and that the single
\mathbb{0}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node requirement holds.

Base case:
S
is simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple and
|S|>1
.

By
\cref
lem:simple decomp we immediately have
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert\leq 2|S|+1\leq 4|S|-3
(as
|S|\geq 2
), and that the single
\mathbb{0}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node requirement holds.

Induction Step: general
S
with
|S|>1
.

In the following, we use subscripts to denote the zero element of different semigroups (e.g.,
\mathbb{0}_{S}
for the zero of
S
).

Since
|S|>1
, there exists some
a\in S
with
12
12

12

S
might not have a zero at all.

a\neq\mathbb{0}_{S}
such that
a
is minimal with respect to
\leq_{\mathcal{J}}
(among the non-zero elements).

Conceptually, we follow here a similar scheme to the proof of Simon’s factorization for words: we inductively “climb up” the hierarchy of
\mathcal{J}
-classes, each time considering the ideal generated by a
\mathcal{J}
-minimal element, and taking the quotient over it.

Consider the ideal
M=S^{1}aS^{1}
. We show that
M
is a minimal non-zero ideal of
S
(with respect to containment). To this end, notice that
a\in M
, so
M\neq\{\mathbb{0}_{S}\}
(if such a zero even exists), and that by definition
M
is an ideal. It remains to show that it is minimal.
Consider some non-zero ideal
I
such that
I\subseteq M
, we show that
M=I
. Let
b\in I\setminus\{\mathbb{0}_{S}\}
, then
b\in S^{1}a^{S}
, so
b\leq_{\mathcal{J}}a
.
By the minimality of
a
with respect to
\leq_{\mathcal{J}}
and since
b\neq\mathbb{0}
, we have
a\mathcal{J}b
. We can therefore write
a=xby
, but since
I
is an ideal and
b\in I
we have

M=S^{1}aS^{1}=S^{1}xbyS^{1}\subseteq S^{1}bS^{1}\subseteq S^{1}IS^{1}=I

as required.

Next, we notice that
|M|>1
. Indeed, if
S
has a zero
\mathbb{0}_{S}
, then
\mathbb{0}_{S}\in M
and since
M
is non-zero it follows that
|M|>1
. Otherwise,
S
does not have a zero. If
M=\{a\}
(by way of contradiction) then for every
b\in S
we have
S^{1}abS^{1}\subseteq M
and similarly
S^{1}baS^{1}\subseteq M
(since
M
is an ideal). This implies
ab=ba=a
, so
a
is a zero of
S
, which is a contradiction.

We conclude that
M
is a minimal non-zero ideal of
S
and
|M|>1
.
In particular, by
\cref
lem:rees cong we can consider the quotient morphism
\texttt{quo}_{M}\colon S\to S/M
.
Furthermore, we can apply
\cref
lem:rees unambiguous and get that
\texttt{quo}_{M}\circ\varphi
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned.

Now, since
|M|>1
, then
|S/M|=|S|-|M|+1<|S|
(by the definition of Rees Congruence in
\cref
def:rees).
We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis, and obtain a decomposition
\tau
of
f
with respect to
\texttt{quo}_{M}\circ\varphi
such that
0pt(\tau)\leq\left\lVert\texttt{quo}_{M}\circ\varphi\right\rVert\leq 4|S/M|-3
and
\tau
has at most one
\mathbb{0}_{S/M}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node.

Our goal is now to convert
\tau
to a decomposition with respect to
\varphi
. To achieve this, we first notice that there is very little to change: in the spirit of
\cref
rmk:identify singletons with elements Rees, the set
S/M
can be viewed as
\{M\}\cup S\setminus M
. In particular, for every
a\in S\setminus M
we have
\texttt{quo}_{M}(a)=a
, so the decomposition
\tau
is already essentially with respect to
\varphi
, with one important exception: for all
C\in\widetilde{V}
such that
\varphi(C)\in M
we have
\texttt{quo}_{M}\circ\varphi(C)=M\in S/M
.

Thus, the parts of
\tau
that might need to be adapted are those whose contexts map to
M
.
Fortunately, note that
M=\mathbb{0}_{S/M}
by
\cref
def:rees, and by the induction hypothesis there is at most one
\mathfrak{I}
-node in
\tau
that is mapped to
\mathbb{0}_{S/M}
and no other
\mathbb{0}_{S/M}
contexts.
Our task is therefore to convert the single
\mathbb{0}_{S/M}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node of
\tau
, denoted
x
, to a decomposition over the same leaves with respect to
\varphi
. Let
g=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x)
and let
B^{\prime}=\{\tau.\mathtt{forest}(xi)\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\wedge xi\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\}

be the set of forests in the children of the
\mathfrak{I}
-node
x
. By
\cref
def:full general decomposition, there is a decomposition of
g
to the basis
B^{\prime}
whose contexts are mapped by
\varphi
to
M=\mathbb{0}_{S/M}
. We wish to establish a bound on the depth of such a decomposition. We split to cases.

•

If
M
has a zero
\mathbb{0}_{M}
and
M^{2}=\{\mathbb{0}_{M}\}
, then
M
is a null semigroup (
\cref
def:simple zsimple null semigroup). We can then use
\cref
lem:null semigroup decomposable depth 2 to obtain a decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
of
g
to the basis
B^{\prime}
over
\varphi
of depth at most
2
.

Plugging
\tau^{\prime}
instead of the node
x
in
\tau
, and connecting each of its leaves to an appropriate subtree of
\tau
(as per
\cref
cor:general decomp closed substitution) we obtain a decomposition of
f
with respect to
\varphi
of depth at most

0pt(\tau)\underbrace{-1+2}_{\text{replace $x$ with $\tau^{\prime}$}}\leq 4|S/M|-3+1\leq 4(|S|-1)-3+1\leq 4|S|-3

•

If
M^{2}\neq\{\mathbb{0}_{M}\}
, or
M
does not have a zero, observe that
M^{2}
is a non-zero ideal of
S
(since
M
is an ideal) and moreover
M^{2}\subseteq M
.
Since
M
is minimal by assumption, we get
M^{2}=M
. Therefore,
M
satisfies the conditions of
\cref
lem:min ideal is simple, so it is simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple.

Recall that we are under the assumption that
S
is not simple or
\mathbb{0}
-simple, and therefore
M\neq S
, so
|M|<|S|
. Then, using
\cref
lem:simple decomp we obtain a decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
of
g
to the basis
B^{\prime}
over
\varphi
of depth at most
4|M|-3
.
Plugging
\tau^{\prime}
in as the case above gives us a decomposition of
f
with respect to
\varphi
of depth at most

0pt(\tau)-1+4|M|-3\leq 4|S/M|-3-1+4|M|-3=4(|S|-|M|+1)-4+4|M|-3\leq 4|S|-3

We therefore obtain a full general decomposition of
f
over
\varphi
of depth at most
4|S|-3
, as required.
In order to complete the induction, it remains to show that there is at most one
\mathbb{0}_{S}
-value
\mathfrak{I}
-node. To this end, observe that
\mathbb{0}_{S}
does not appear in
\tau
(before our change), since
\mathbb{0}_{S}\in M
(if it exists). Thus, the only possible place for
\mathbb{0}_{S}
to occur in the modified decomposition is within
\tau^{\prime}
.

Since
M
is an ideal of
S
, by
\cref
lem:0I we have
\mathbb{0}_{M}=\mathbb{0}_{S}
and therefore by either
\cref
lem:min ideal is simple or
\cref
lem:simple decomp (depending on how
\tau^{\prime}
is obtained) we have that there is at most one
\mathbb{0}_{M}
-valued
\mathfrak{I}
-node in
\tau^{\prime}
, concluding the induction and the proof.
∎

Corollary 119
.

Consider a forest language
L
over some alphabet
A
such that its syntactic forest morphism
\langle\alpha_{L},\beta_{L}\rangle\colon\langle{H_{A}},V_{A}\rangle\rightarrow\langle H_{L},V_{L}\rangle
satisfy that
\beta_{L}
is
\mathcal{R}
-aligned, then every forest
f\in{H_{A}}
admits a general decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{Dec}(V_{A},B_{A},f,\beta_{L})
of bounded depth
4|V_{L}|-3
.

This concludes the proof of our main result. In the following we present an additional tool for obtaining decomposition depth bounds that is independent from
\mathcal{R}
-alignment, but assumes that there are already-known bounds.

18.1
A Reduction Scheme

The inductive step in the proof of
\cref
thm:main constructs a decomposition over a composition of morphism by “expanding” an
\mathfrak{I}
-node with a decomposition over its leaves. This technique does not rely on
\mathcal{R}
-alignment, and is a way to obtain general bounds for (possibly not
\mathcal{R}
-aligned) morphisms. In this section we formalize this result.

Consider two finite semigroups
V_{1},V_{2}
and a semigroup morphism
\varphi\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
. The following lemma gives an upper bound on
\left\lVert V_{1}\right\rVert
by means of
\left\lVert V_{2}\right\rVert
and the bound on sub-semigroups of
V_{1}
. Specifically, observe that for every idempotent element
e\in V_{2}
, we have that
\varphi^{-1}(e)
is a sub-semigroup of
V_{1}
. We then have the following.

Lemma 120

(Reduction Lemma)
.

Consider two finite semigroups
V_{1},V_{2}
and a semigroup morphism
\varphi\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
, then
for every context set
\widetilde{V}
and a morphism
\beta\colon\widetilde{V}\to V_{1}
we have

\left\lVert\beta\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\varphi\circ\beta\right\rVert\cdot\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert

In particular,

\left\lVert V_{1}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert V_{2}\right\rVert\cdot\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert

Proof.

Assume

\left\lVert\varphi\circ\beta\right\rVert<\infty

(otherwise there is nothing to prove).
In order to prove the claim, we need to show that for every
\widetilde{V}
,
B
and morphism
\beta\colon\widetilde{V}\to V_{1}
, and for every forest
f
that is decomposable by
\widetilde{V}
to
B
, there exists a decomposition
\tau_{1}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\beta)
whose depth is at most

\left\lVert\varphi\circ\beta\right\rVert\cdot\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert
.

Thus, fix such a context set
\widetilde{V}
, a basis
B
, a morphism
\beta\colon\widetilde{V}\to V_{1}
, and a decomposable forest
f
.
Observe that
\varphi\circ\beta\colon\widetilde{V}\to V_{2}
is also a morphism, and recall from
\cref
rmk:decomposability that
f
is decomposable to
B
regardless of the morphism.
Therefore, there exists a decomposition
\tau_{2}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi\circ\beta)
with

0pt(\tau_{2})\leq\left\lVert\varphi\circ\beta\right\rVert
.
We now construct the desired
\tau_{1}
, starting with a high-level overview.

A naive approach is to ask whether
\tau_{2}
itself is in
\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi)
.
Indeed, this seems to almost work, since all the “syntactic requirements” of
\cref
def:general decomposition,def:full general decomposition hold: the contexts in
\tau_{2}
are from
\widetilde{V}
, its leaves are from
B
, and all
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes have the required underlying binary decomposition structure with no inherited references.

However, a problem arises when considering
\mathfrak{I}
-nodes. Specifically, there may be an
\mathfrak{I}
-node
x\in\tau_{2}.\mathtt{Nodes}
with idempotent value
e\in V_{2}
.
By
\cref
def:general decomposition this means that there exists a binary decomposition
\tau_{x}
whose contexts are from
(\varphi\circ\beta)^{-1}[e]=\beta^{-1}[\varphi^{-1}[e]]
.
However, in order for
x
to be an
\mathfrak{I}
node over
\beta
alone, we need the contexts to be from
\beta^{-1}[e^{\prime}]
for some idempotent
e^{\prime}\in V_{1}
). Unfortunately, it is not guaranteed that
\varphi^{-1}[e]
is a single idempotent element (it may contain several elements, and they are not necessarily idempotent). Moreover, it is not even guaranteed that there is another binary decomposition that corresponds to
x
with an idempotent value in
V_{1}
.

Intuitively, this already hints at the multiplicative factor in the statement of the lemma: we construct
\tau_{1}
by following the structure of
\tau_{2}
until encountering an
\mathfrak{I}
-node
x\in\tau_{2}.\mathtt{Nodes}
. By
\cref
def:general decomposition we have that
\tau_{2}.\mathtt{forest}(x)
is decomposable by the semigroup
\beta^{-1}[\varphi^{-1}[e]]
to
\{\tau_{2}.\mathtt{forest}(y):\text{$y$ is a child of $x$ in $\tau_{2}.\mathtt{Nodes}$}\}
.
Then, we replace the single node
x
in
\tau_{2}
with a general decomposition of depth at most
\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert
whose leaves are children of
x
in
\tau_{2}
. We then continue with the decomposition from these leaves.
Thus, each
\mathfrak{I}
-node causes a blowup of depth at most
\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert
for some idempotent
e\in V_{2}
, as required.

We proceed with the formal details.
Define a function
T_{2\to 1}
such that for every context set
\widetilde{V}
, a basis
B
and a morphism
\beta\colon\widetilde{V}\rightarrow V_{1}
transforms a decomposition
\tau\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi\circ\beta)
to a decomposition
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\beta)
. We define the function inductively on the depth of
\tau
, and prove that it satisfies the following:

•

If
\tau\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi\circ\beta)
then
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\beta)
.

•

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(\epsilon)\neq\mathfrak{I}
then
0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau))\leq 1+\max_{i}\{0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau[i]))\}
for
i\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\cap\mathbb{N}
.

•

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(\epsilon)=\mathfrak{I}
with idempotent value
v
then
0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau))\leq\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[v]\right\rVert+\max_{i}\{0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau[i]))\}
for
i\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\cap\mathbb{N}
.

Let
\tau\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,f,\varphi\circ\beta
) for some
f\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
. We define
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)
by induction on
|0pt(\tau)|
. The base case is
|0pt(\tau)|=0
, so
f\in B
and
\tau=\Upsilon(f)
. We define
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)\coloneqq\tau
and the requirements trivially hold.
The inductive case is
|0pt(\tau)|>0
.
In this case
\tau.\mathtt{type}(\epsilon)\neq\mathfrak{L}
, and we split to cases according to
\tau.\mathtt{type}(\epsilon)
.

•

\mathfrak{B}
: We define
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)\coloneqq\tau\left[\begin{array}[]{c}0\mapsto T_{2\to 1}(\tau[0])\\
1\mapsto T_{2\to 1}(\tau[1])\end{array}\right]
.
That is, we define
T_{2\to 1}(\tau).\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
and
T_{2\to 1}(\tau).\mathtt{ctx}(1)=\tau.\mathtt{ctx}(1)
, which results in
T_{2\to 1}(\tau).\mathtt{forest}(0)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(0)
and
T_{2\to 1}(\tau).\mathtt{forest}(1)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(1)
. We then inductively use the decompositions for the children. This results in a decomposition by
\cref
cor:general decomp closed substitution, so
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)\in\mathtt{Dec}(\tilde{V},B,\tau_{2}.\mathtt{forest}(x),\beta)
.
By the inductive hypothesis and that because
\varphi[V_{1}]
contains an idempotent i.e.
\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert\geq 1
, we obtain the bound

\begin{split}&0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau))\leq 1+\max\{0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau[0])),0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau[1]))\}\leq\\
&1+\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert\cdot(0pt(\tau)-1)\leq\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert\cdot 0pt(\tau)\end{split}

•

\mathfrak{C}
: This case is analogous to the previous case:
let
S\coloneqq\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\cap\mathbb{N}
. We now define
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)\coloneqq\tau[y\mapsto T_{2\to 1}(\tau[y])]
(for
y\in S
), so that
T_{2\to 1}(\tau).\mathtt{type}(\epsilon)=\mathfrak{C}
as well. The bound computation is identical to the above, with the maximum taken over
S
.

•

\mathfrak{I}
: This is the interesting case. Let
S\coloneqq\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}\cap\mathbb{N}
(i.e., the children of the
\mathfrak{I}
-node at the root) and let
v\in V_{2}
be the idempotent value of
\tau[\epsilon]
.
By the definition of
\mathfrak{I}
-node (
\cref
def:general decomposition), there exists a
binary
decomposition
\tau^{\prime}
such that:

–

\tau^{\prime}\in\mathtt{FullDec}((\varphi\circ\beta)^{-1}[v],\tau.\mathtt{forest}(S))
.

–

\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
.

Let
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}\coloneqq\beta^{-1}[\varphi^{-1}[v]]
, then

\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
is decomposable by
\widetilde{V^{\prime}}
to
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(S)
.
Denote
\beta^{\prime}=\beta|_{\widetilde{V^{\prime}}}\colon\widetilde{V^{\prime}}\rightarrow\varphi^{-1}[v]
.

By
\cref
def:semigroup decomp bound there exists some decomposition
\tau^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V^{\prime}},\tau.\mathtt{forest}(S),\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon),\beta^{\prime})
such that
0pt(\tau^{\prime\prime})\leq\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[v]\right\rVert
.

Since all the leaves of
\tau^{\prime\prime}
have labels in
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(S)
, we can define a mapping
g\colon\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]\to S
such that for every leaf
y
in
\tau^{\prime\prime}
it holds that
\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y)=\tau.\mathtt{forest}(g(y))
. Note that
g
is not necessarily bijective – it is possible that
\tau^{\prime\prime}
factors differently to
\tau^{\prime}
. The only requirement is that all the leaves have forests in
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(S)
, as they do in
\tau^{\prime}
.

We can now define
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)\coloneqq\tau^{\prime\prime}[y\mapsto T_{2\to 1}(\tau_{g(y)})]
(for
y\in\tau^{\prime\prime}.\mathtt{type}^{-1}[\mathfrak{L}]
).
By the inductive hypothesis (on each
T_{2\to 1}(\tau_{g(y)}))
), we have that
T_{2\to 1}(\tau)\in\mathtt{Dec}(\widetilde{V},B,\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon),\beta)
. Moreover, since
v
is idempotent, the depth bounds hold as follows:

\begin{split}&0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau))\leq 0pt(\tau^{\prime\prime})+\max\left\{0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau[y])):y\in S\right\}\\
&\leq\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[v]\right\rVert+\max\left\{0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau[y])):y\in S\right\}\\
&\leq\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert+(0pt(\tau)-1)\cdot\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert\\
&=0pt(\tau)\cdot\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert\end{split}

as required.

Finally, since we start with
\tau
with

0pt(\tau)\leq\left\lVert\varphi\circ\beta\right\rVert
,
we conclude that

0pt(T_{2\to 1}(\tau))\leq\left\lVert\varphi\circ\beta\right\rVert\cdot\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert
.
Since this holds for every basis and context set, we conclude the lemma with

\left\lVert\beta\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\varphi\circ\beta\right\rVert\cdot\max_{\overset{e\in V_{2}}{e^{2}=e}}\left\lVert\varphi^{-1}[e]\right\rVert

∎

19
Lower Bound without
\mathcal{R}
-Alignment

In this section we show that without the sufficient condition of
\mathcal{R}
-alignment, there is a morphism that does not have a bounded decomposition depth.
We briefly sketch the intuition for this counterexample, before giving the precise details.

We restrict attention to forests that are binary trees. We then tailor a specific morphism to a forest algebra, with the following property: each idempotent element in the algebra can only be used to factor only one “side” of a subtree (i.e., either left or right). Ensuring this property is the crux of the example.
Once this property is established, it follows that even
\mathfrak{I}
-nodes can only, at best, decompose one side of a subtree. Then, if we start with a complete tree of depth
d
, each node decreases its depth by at most
\frac{1}{2}
. This shows that any decomposition has depth at least
\log d
, which is unbounded.

We start by describing the forest language we use. The language can be simply described as a set of labeled trees, but unfortunately we must also formalize it as a morphism to a forest algebra, which is significantly more complicated.

We work over the alphabet
A=\{a,b,\ell_{a},\ell_{b},\ell\}
and consider binary trees whose leaves are labeled by
\ell_{a},\ell_{b},\ell
and internal nodes labeled
a,b
. We also assume leaves are the only children of their parent.
Our forest language includes forests
f
that satisfy the following (see
\cref
fig:counter example tree):

•

For all binary nodes
x
, the left child (
x[0]
) is labeled
a
and the right child (
x[1]
) is labeled
b
.

•

All the leaves below the left child of the root (i.e., below
f[0]
) have labels in
\{\ell_{a},\ell\}
.

•

All the leaves below the right child of the root (i.e., below
f[1]
) have labels in
\{\ell_{b},\ell\}
.

{forest}

Figure 22
:
An tree in the language of
\cref
sec:lower bound. Note that in the left subtree of the root, all leaves are labeled in
\{\ell_{a},\ell\}
, and in the right subtree in
\{\ell_{b},\ell\}
.

Intuitively, a subtree whose leaves are labeled only with
\ell
can be plugged anywhere in the tree. In contrast, subtrees that have a leaf labeled
\ell_{a}
(respectively
\ell_{b}
) can only be plugged in the left (respectively right) subtrees of the root. We call the latter trees
marked
.
This already suggests some inherent asymmetry in deciding how to decompose a node.

In order to proceed with reasoning about decompositions of this language, we must formulate it as a morphism to a forest algebra.
We start by presenting the monoid
H
, with intuitive explanations about its elements. For each element we also bring a “canonical” example of a tree that is mapped to it. To distinguish elements in the free forest algebra from elements in the finite forest algebra, we denote the latter with bold red symbols.

H=\underbrace{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell}},\end{array}\right\}}_{\text{Leaves}}\cup\underbrace{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c c}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}T}},&{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{a}T}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}F}},&{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{a}F}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{b}T}},&{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{b}T}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{b}F}},&{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{b}F}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}},&{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}},\end{array}\right\}}_{\text{One Root}}\cup\underbrace{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2T}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2F}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2T}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2F}},\end{array}\right\}}_{\text{Two Roots}}\cup\underbrace{\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T1}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T2}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}},\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\epsilon}},\\
\end{array}\right\}}_{\text{Special}}

The intuition behind the naming scheme and the types of elements (as is captured later by the morphism) is the following:

Leaves:

each leaf in
\ell_{a},\ell_{b},\ell
is mapped to its corresponding element in
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell}}
.

One Root:

these map from single-root subtrees where either
\ell_{a}
or
\ell_{b}
occur (or neither, but not both). The first letter is the root of the subtree, the second marks whether the corresponding leaf exists in the subtree, and the third whether the subtree is in the language (
T
) or not (
F
). Thus, for example:

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}T}}
captures trees whose root is labeled with
a
, their left-subtree contains at least one
\ell_{a}
, and their right subtree contains only
\ell
(and therefore they are in the language).

An example of such a tree is
a(a(\ell_{a})+b(\ell))
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{a}F}}
captures trees whose root is labeled with
b
, and their right-subtree contains at least one
\ell_{a}
, and their right subtree contains only
\ell
(and therefore they are not in the language).

An example of such a tree is
b(a(\ell)+b(\ell_{a}))
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}
captures subtree whose root is labeled with
a
, and all its leaves are labeled
\ell
(and therefore they are in the language, which is always the case if all leaves are labeled
\ell
).

An example of such a tree is
b(a(\ell)+b(\ell))
.

Two Roots:

these map from two-rooted subtrees where either
\ell_{a}
or
\ell_{b}
occur (but not both).
The first letter indicates a marking (either
\ell_{a}
or
\ell_{b}
),
2
indicates there are two roots, and
T
and
F
indicate whether the marking is on the correct side. Thus, for example:

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2T}}
is mapped from forests whose left root has a leaf labeled
\ell_{a}
, and the right only has
\ell
.

An example of such a tree is
a(\ell_{a})+b(\ell)
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2F}}
is mapped from forests whose left root has
\ell_{b}
.

Examples of such trees are
a(\ell_{b})+b(\ell_{b})
, or
a(\ell_{b})+b(\ell)
.

Special:

these map from some edge-cases:

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}
is mapped from sub-forests that cannot be part of a tree in the language due to ill-formation, or violating the condition, e.g.,
a(\ell_{b})+b(\ell_{a})
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\epsilon}}
is an ad-hoc unit element, mapped to by the empty forest.

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}}
is mapped from two-rooted forests whose leaves are all
\ell
, e.g.,
a(\ell)+b(\ell)
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T2}}
is mapped to from two-rooted forests that are fully marked, and therefore can become accepting only with a single root (but cannot serve as “deeper” subtrees), e.g.,
a(\ell_{a})+b(\ell_{b})
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T1}}
is mapped to from fully marked trees that are in the language, but cannot serve as “deeper” subtrees, e.g.,
a(a(\ell_{a})+b(\ell_{b}))
.

With these elements in mind, we now proceed to define
H
as a monoid, and define the semantic context set
V
and the morphism
\varphi
. While technically painful, the definitions follow immediately from the intuition of
H
.

The addition operation on
H
is given by the following (where
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}?}}
is a wildcard):

h_{1}+h_{2}=\begin{cases}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}}&h_{1}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}\wedge h_{2}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T2}}&h_{1}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}?}}\wedge h_{2}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{b}?}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2T}}&h_{1}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}\wedge h_{2}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{b}?}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2T}}&h_{1}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}?}}\wedge h_{2}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2F}}&h_{1}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}?}}\}\wedge h_{2}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{a}?}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2F}}&h_{1}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{b}?}}\wedge h_{2}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{b}?}}\}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}&h_{1}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{b}?}}\wedge h_{2}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{a}?}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}&else\end{cases}

We also define
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\epsilon}}
defined as a neutral element.
One can verify that the intuition of the elements of
H
is maintained through this addition. Note in particular that this already enforces trees that are at most binary, as all other additions immediately go to
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}
.

We proceed to define the monoid
V
of contexts. To this end, we identify
V\subseteq H^{H}
, i.e., each context is an action on
H
. Specifically, we have
V
to be the span of
V_{\text{letters}}\cup V_{\text{left}}\cup V_{\text{right}}\cup\{\textrm{id}\}
within the semigroup
H^{H}
, with the following components.

•

The “main” elements of
V
are
V_{\text{letters}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\ell_{a}}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\ell_{b}}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\ell}}}\}
, each corresponding intuitively to the composition of its respective letter above a subtree. They are defined as follows.

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}(h)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}T}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{b}F}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell}}\vee h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}F}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2F}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{a}T}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2T}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{b}F}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2F}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell_{b}T}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2T}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T1}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T2}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\qquad{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}(h)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{a}F}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{b}T}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell}}\vee h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{a}F}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2F}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{a}T}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}2T}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{b}F}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2F}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell_{b}T}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}2T}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T2}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}T2}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

and

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\ell_{a}}}}(h)=\begin{cases}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{a}}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\epsilon}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\ell_{b}}}}(h)=\begin{cases}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell_{b}}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\epsilon}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\ell}}}(h)=\begin{cases}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell}}&h={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\epsilon}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

•

The elements
V_{\text{left}}
and
V_{\text{right}}
capture the addition from left and right for every element in
H
(i.e., enable the functions
\mathtt{in_{l}}
and
\mathtt{in_{r}}
). For every
h\in H
define
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}r_{h}}}(u)=u+h
and
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}l_{h}}}(u)=h+u
, then
V_{\text{left}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}l_{h}}}\mid h\in H\}
and
V_{\text{right}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}r_{h}}}\mid h\in H\}
. We also introduce the convenient shorthands
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+a}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}r_{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}r_{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}l_{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b+}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}l_{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}}}}
.

•

Finally,
\textrm{id}\in H^{H}
is the identity function, and is the unit of
V
.
And we denote by
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}}\in V
such that for every
h\in H

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}}(h)={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\bot}}
. (which is serves as a
zero
of
V
, and it exists as for example
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}}=({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})^{3}\in V
)

We can finally define the morphism
\varphi\colon A^{\triangle}\to\langle H,V\rangle
as the morphism lifted uniquely
from the mapping
\sigma\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\sigma}}}
for
\sigma\in A=\{a,b,\ell_{a},\ell_{b},\ell\}
.

We now start analyzing the structure and idempotent elements of
V
, denoted
E(V)
.
Recall that a forest
f
is
unmarked
if all its leaves are
\ell
.
The following result studies prefix-contexts of unmarked forests.

Lemma 121
.

Let
f
be an unmarked forest and
C\neq\square
a context such that
f=C\cdot f^{\prime}
for some
f^{\prime}
. If
\varphi(C)
is idempotent, then
\varphi(C)
is one of the following:

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}),{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}}),{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})),{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}})

We denote this set (without
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}}
) by
E^{\prime}(V)\subseteq E(V)\subseteq V
, and note these are all idempotent.

In addition, for every
e\in E^{\prime}(V)
the following property holds (“absorbing from the left over
E^{\prime}(V)
”):

\forall e\in E^{\prime}(V),v\in V\ e\cdot v\in E^{\prime}(V)\rightarrow e\cdot v=e

Moreover, the following properties hold for every
v\in E^{\prime}(V)
and
v^{\prime}\in V
:

\begin{array}[]{l l c l}(P1)&v={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}}\cdot v^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}(V)&\implies&v={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}\\
(P2)&v={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})\cdot v^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}(V)&\implies&v={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}))\\
(P3)&v=({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})\cdot v^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}(V)&\implies&v={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}\\
(P4)&v={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})\cdot v^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}(V)&\implies&v={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}})\end{array}

Proof.

The proof of the lemma is by direct computation, following the definition of
\langle H,V\rangle
.

First, the set
E^{\prime}(V)
is computed as follows. Observe that
\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\ell}}}\}
is the set of all possible values for
\varphi(C)
, by the assumption on
f
and
C
.
Denote by
V_{l}
the subsemigroup spanned by this set (which is finite and can be directly computed). We then compute
E(V_{l})=\{e\in V_{l}:e^{2}=e\}
and we have
E^{\prime}(V)=E(V_{l})\setminus\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}}\}
.

To show the absorbing property, consider for example the element
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})
. We can compute the set
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})\cdot V=\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})\cdot v:v\in V\}
, and then intersected it with
V_{l}
to get
\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+a}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}),{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}})),{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})))\}
. Notice that the only non
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}}
idempotent in this set is
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})
, as required.

For
(P1)
, we present the computation for
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+a}}
. The computations for
(P2)-(P4)
are similar.
By direct computation, notice that

({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+a}}\cdot V)\cap V_{l}=\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}),{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})),{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}))\}

and the only non
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{\bot}}}
idempotent here is
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}})
as required.
∎

The latter property shown in
\cref
lem:ab idem calculation is at the heart of our reasoning. Intuitively, it shows that if
v\in E^{\prime}(V)
has a prefix from a certain list, then we know exactly what
v
is. This is used in the following to show that each context always decomposes a forest on a fixed “side”.

We can now establish that
\varphi
is indeed not
\mathcal{R}
-aligned (
\cref
def:unamb property). For example, the forest
f=a(\ell)+b(\ell)
can be factored by
C_{1}=a(\ell)+b(\square)
as well as by
C_{2}=a(\square)+b(\ell)
.
We have
\varphi(C_{1})={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}
and
\varphi(C_{2})={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}
, and we claim
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}\mathcal{J}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}
. Indeed, by
\cref
lem:ab idem calculation we have

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}=({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}})\cdot(a+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}})\qquad\text{ and }{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}=({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}})\cdot({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}})

However, again by
\cref
lem:ab idem calculation, there is no
r\mathcal{J}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}
such that
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}=({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}})\cdot r
, since
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}
is absorbing from the left.

We now turn to formalize the intuition that every idempotent
e\in E^{\prime}(V)
has to “pick a side” of forests to decompose. Notice that we do not claim this in complete generality, but rather only to unmarked forests. The counterexample we construct is ultimately unmarked, and therefore this is sufficient.

Lemma 122
.

For every
e\in E^{\prime}(V)
there is an address
u\in\mathbb{N}\cup\mathbb{N}^{2}
(i.e., of depth at most
2
) such that for every
f,f_{1},f_{0}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
with
f\in\varphi^{-1}[{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}}]
and
C\in\varphi^{-1}[e]
such that
f=C(f_{1})
and
f_{0}=C(\square_{f_{1}})
we have that
f_{0}[u]=f[u]
(i.e.,
f
and
f_{0}
have identical subtrees at address
u
).

In particular, factoring by
C
does not pluck out any subforest of
f
below
u
.

Proof.

We prove the claim by separating to cases according to
e\in E^{\prime}(V)\setminus\{\mathrm{id}\}
.

•

e={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}
: notice first that
f
has the structure
a(f_{1})+b(f_{2})\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
(otherwise it cannot be factored by
e
).
Moreover, since
\varphi(f)\in\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}}\}
) we actually have
\varphi(f)={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell 2}}
, as this is the only mapping of such a structure to this set.
Thus,
f
has all its leaves labeled
\ell
, and in particular
\varphi(b(f_{2}))={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}
.

We claim that in this case we can choose
u=0
, i.e., that
f_{0}[u]=f[u]
. Assume by way of contradiction that
f_{0}[0]\neq f[0]
. Therefore, we have
0\leq\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)
, i.e.,
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)
occurs below
0
.
Since
C
itself is nontrivial, then
C\neq\square
, so we can denote
C=(\square+b(f_{2}))\cdot C^{\prime}
for some
C^{\prime}
.
Note that
\varphi(\square+b(f_{2}))={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}
, and since
\varphi
is a morphism, then by
(P3)
of
\cref
lem:ab idem calculation we get

\varphi(C)=\varphi(\square+b(f_{2}))\cdot\varphi(C^{\prime})={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}\neq{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}

which is a contradiction.
Thus
f_{0}[0]=f[0]
as required, and we conclude that we can only use
e=a+f_{b}
to pluck out a subforest below the right root.

•

e={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}
: this case is symmetric to
e={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}
, but uses
(P1)
of
\cref
lem:ab idem calculation.

•

e={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})
: we follow a similar structure to the previous cases. Notice first that
f=a(a(f_{1})+b(f_{2}))
. We claim that for
u=01
it holds that
f_{0}[u]=f[u]
.
Indeed, otherwise we get either
C=a(\square)
(which is not possible as
\varphi(a(\square))\neq e
), or that
C=a(a+\square)\cdot C^{\prime}
for some
C^{\prime}
. Observe that
\varphi(a(a+\square))={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})
, and
by using
(P2)
of
\cref
lem:ab idem calculation we get

\varphi(C)={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}))\neq e
, which is a contradiction.

•

e={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})
: symmetric to the previous case, by using
(P4)
of
\cref
lem:ab idem calculation.

•

e={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}}))
: in this case we have
f=a(a(f_{1})+b(a(f_{3})+b(f_{4})))
, and we claim that for
u=00
we have
f_{0}[00]=f[00]
. Indeed, otherwise we get that
C=a(\square+b)\cdot C^{\prime}
for some
C^{\prime}
and because
\varphi(a(\square+b))\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})
we can use the absorption property in
\cref
lem:ab idem calculation and we get
\varphi(C)={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}+b}})\neq e
, which is a contradiction.

•

e={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{b}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}f_{a}}}({\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a+}})+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}})
: symmetric to the previous case.

We thus conclude the claim for every
e\in E^{\prime}(V)
.
∎

The counterexample we construct utilizes a large complete binary tree, and shows that at each node in a decomposition, there remains a deep-enough complete binary subtree. To capture this we denote, for a given forest
f
and an address
u
, the maximal depth of a complete binary subtree rooted at
u
by
\mathtt{V}(f,u)
, as well as
\mathtt{V}(\epsilon)
the maximal complete binary rooted at some root of
f
, and
\mathtt{V}(f,\infty)
as the maximal complete binary tree anywhere at
f
. Formally, we have the following.

Definition 123
.

Given a forest
f\in{H_{A}}
and an address
u\in\mathtt{Domain}(f)
define

\mathtt{V}(f,u)\coloneqq\min\{|v|-|u|+1:v\in\mathtt{Domain}(f)\wedge u\leq v\wedge v\text{ is maximal }\}

Define
\mathtt{V}(f,\epsilon)=\min\{\mathtt{V}(f,n):n\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
\mathtt{V}(f)=\mathtt{V}(f,\infty)=\max\{\mathtt{V}(f,u):u\in\mathtt{Domain}(f)\}
.

The following lemma relates the size of a maximal binary subtree in an unmarked forest
f
with that of its children’s in a node of a binary decomposition (
\cref
def:bin decomp).

Lemma 124
.

Consider
f,f_{0},f_{1}\in\widetilde{H_{A}}
with
f\in\varphi_{H}^{-1}[{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}}]
and
C\in\widetilde{V_{A}}
such that
f=C(f_{1})
and
f_{0}=C(\square_{f_{1}})
.
Then
\mathtt{V}(f_{0})+\mathtt{V}(f_{1})\geq\mathtt{V}(f)
.
Moreover, one of the following holds:

1.

\mathtt{V}(f_{0})=\mathtt{V}(f)
, or

2.

\mathtt{V}(f_{1})=\mathtt{V}(f)
, or

3.

\mathtt{V}(f_{0})+\mathtt{V}(f_{1},\epsilon)\geq\mathtt{V}(f)
.

Proof.

Consider
u\in\mathtt{Domain}(f)
such that
\mathtt{V}(f)=\mathtt{V}(f,u)
(i.e.,
u
witnesses the location of the maximal completely binary subtree), and denote
C^{\prime}=f[u\mapsto\square]
.
Let
f^{\prime}=\mathsf{ForestDom}^{-1}(\mathsf{ForestDom}(f)[u])
be the subforest of
f
below
u
, so that
f=C^{\prime}(f^{\prime})
.
By the choice of
u
, we have
\mathtt{V}(f^{\prime},\epsilon)=\mathtt{V}(f)
.
Now split to cases according to the order of
C
and
C^{\prime}
with respect to
\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}
:

1.

If
C
and
C^{\prime}
are incomparable, then
f^{\prime}
is a subtree of
f_{0}
, so
\mathtt{V}(f_{0})=\mathtt{V}(f)
.

2.

If
C\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}C^{\prime}
, then
f^{\prime}
is a subtree of
f_{1}
, hence
\mathtt{V}(f_{1})=\mathtt{V}(f)
.

3.

If
C^{\prime}\preceq_{\textnormal{pre}}C
, then by the definition of
C^{\prime}
and the fact that
f^{\prime}
is a tree (rather than a proper forest), we have that
\mathtt{sq\_pos}(C)=u\cdot v
(for
v\neq\epsilon
).
Notice that
\mathtt{V}(f_{0},u)=\min\{\mathtt{V}(f),|v|+1\}
and that
\mathtt{V}(f_{1},\epsilon)\geq\mathtt{V}(f)-|v|
(since at least the subtree starting from
u\cdot v
is a complete binary tree in
f_{1}
). Overall we get that
\mathtt{V}(f_{0})+\mathtt{V}(f_{1},\epsilon)\geq\mathtt{V}(f)

∎

We are now ready for our main argument, showing that every node in a general decomposition of an unmarked tree decreases the depth of a maximal complete binary tree by at most
\frac{1}{2}
.

Theorem 125
.

Consider a forest
f\in\varphi^{-1}[{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}]
and a general decomposition
\tau
such that
\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)=f
. For every inner node
x\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
there is a child
y
of
x
such that
\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(y))\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
.

Proof.

We prove the theorem by cases according to
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)
.

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{B}

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{B}
then by
\cref
lem:mc bin we readily have
\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0))+\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x1))\geq\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
, so at least one of the summands is
\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
.

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{C}
13
13

13

By
\cref
lem:elimination of C nodes we can actually first eliminate
\mathfrak{C}
-nodes, but we rely on this argument in the
\mathfrak{I}
-node case, and therefore prove it.
:

Denote by
\tau^{\prime}
the corresponding centipede binary decomposition (as per
\cref
def:general decomposition).
Assume that
x0\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
corresponds to the down-most leaf in
\tau^{\prime}
(i.e., the “index” node), and that
xi\in\tau.\mathtt{Nodes}
corresponds to
0^{i}1\in\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}
.
Intuitively, we now repeat a similar argument to the proof of
\cref
lem:mc bin to show that one of these nodes has a large maximal complete binary subtree.

Let
u\in\mathtt{Domain}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon))
such that
\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))=\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon),u)
.

•

If there exists some child
y=0^{i}1
such that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},y,\epsilon)(0)\leq u
, then the entire complete subtree rooted at
u
appears in
y
. Therefore,
\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(xi))=\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
, and we are done.

•

Otherwise, let
0^{k}
be the down-most leaf of
\tau^{\prime}
(the index node), and notice there is an address
u^{\prime}
such that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau,0^{k},\epsilon)(u^{\prime})=u
. Indeed, if none of the
0^{i}1
are mapped above
u
, then
u
remains in the residue
y
.
If
\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(0^{k}))=\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
then we are done. Otherwise, the subtree rooted at
u^{\prime}
in
0^{k}
is factored to some child
0^{i}1
, and we proceed.

Consider some leaf of
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{k})
at address
v^{\prime}
below
u^{\prime}
(i.e.,
u^{\prime}\leq v^{\prime}
) of minimal depth. Thus,
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{k}),u^{\prime})\geq|v^{\prime}|-|u^{\prime}|+1
.
Denote
v=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{k},\epsilon)(v^{\prime})
. Intuitively, we now consider the leaf
0^{i}1
where the subtree rooted at
v
is factored. Since a centipede has no inherited references, then the
entire
complete binary subtree rooted at
v^{\prime}
is factored at
0^{i}1
. Therefore
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1),\epsilon)\geq\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))-(|v^{\prime}|-|u^{\prime}|)
, from which we conclude the desired inequality

\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{k}),\epsilon)+\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1),\epsilon)\geq\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))+1

We proceed to give the formal argument.
Note that
v
cannot be a leaf of
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
, since we would then have
\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon),u)=|v|-|u|+1=|v^{\prime}|-|u^{\prime}|+1=\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{k}),v^{\prime})\leq\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0))
, which we already assume is not the case (otherwise we are done as above).

Thus, we have that
\tau^{\prime}.0^{k}@v\to 0^{i}1
for some
i<k
, and assume that
xi
is the corresponding node in
\tau
.
By the properties of
\mathtt{AncEmb}
(
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references and
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding not affected by ancestor) we have that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{i}1,\epsilon)(0)=v
.
More precisely, by
\cref
lem:ancestor embedding respects holes and references and
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we have
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{k},0^{i})(v^{\prime})=\mathtt{sq\_pos}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{ctx}(0^{i}1))=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{i}1,0^{i})(0)
and thus by
\cref
def:ancestor embedding formal we have
