\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{i}1,\epsilon)(0)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime}.0^{i},\epsilon)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{i}1,0^{i})(0)=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime}.0^{i},\epsilon)\circ\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{k},0^{i})(v^{\prime})=\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{k},\epsilon)(v^{\prime})=v
.

Now let
n,w\in\mathtt{Domain}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1))
such that
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1),\epsilon)=|w|-|n|+1=|w|
. Notice that
w
is a leaf of
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1)
, and that
\mathtt{AncEmb}(\tau^{\prime},0^{i}1,\epsilon)(w)=v\circledast w
.
Because
\tau^{\prime}
has no inherited references (and
w
is a leaf of
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1)
) we have that
v\circledast w
is leaf of
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon)
, and notice that
|v\circledast w|=|v|+|w|-1
.
Overall we conclude that
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon),u)\leq|v\circledast w|-|u|+1=|v|+|w|-|u|<(|v^{\prime}|-|u^{\prime}|+1)+|w|=\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{k}),u^{\prime})+\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1),\epsilon)
, and we conclude that
\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))+1\leq\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x0))+\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(xi),\epsilon)
.

If
\tau.\mathtt{type}(x)=\mathfrak{I}
:

let
\tau^{\prime}
a corresponding binary decomposition (as per
\cref
def:general decomposition).
We can further assume that
\tau^{\prime}
has no inherited references, by requiring that it is minimal (
\cref
lem:min dec no references contexts,lem:min full dec).

We now focus on the left-most “centipede” structure induced by the nodes
(\{0\}^{*}\cup\{0\}^{*}\cdot\{1\})\cap\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{Nodes}
(i.e, the left-most branch and it immediate right children).
Since there are no inherited references, this is a centipede, and we can apply the same reasoning as the case of a
\mathfrak{C}
-node.

This allows us to conclude that either the down-most leaf
y
in
\tau^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y))=\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(\epsilon))
(in which case we are done), or there is some
i
such that
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y))+\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1),\epsilon)\geq\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
.
If
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y))\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
, then we are done. We therefore assume that this is not the case, so
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1),\epsilon)>\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))+1
.
Notice that unlike
y
(which is a leaf, and therefore corresponds to a child of
\tau[x]
), the node
0^{i}1
in
\tau^{\prime}
does not directly correspond to a child of
\tau[x]
.

We focus on the case that
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y))<\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
and show that
\tau^{\prime}
has some leaf
z
(in fact, the down-most leaf below
0^{i}1
) such that
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(z))\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
.
Intuitively, this follows from
\cref
lem:ab idem preserving – the idempotent value of the
\mathfrak{I}
-node has to “pick a side” to factor by, which is maintained down the decomposition. Thus, when starting from a complete binary tree, at least half the tree survives all the way to the leaf (namely the side that is not picked). We make this intuition formal.

Recall that we assume that
f\in\varphi^{-1}[{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}]
. By the definition of
\langle V,H\rangle
, it is easy to verify that all its subforest are therefore mapped to
\{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}a\ell T}}+{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}b\ell T}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell}}\}
. Moreover, forests mapped to
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell}}
are not decomposable at all, as the only forest mapped to
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0}\ell}}
is the letter
\ell\in A
).
We can therefore apply
\cref
lem:ab idem preserving, so there exists an address
u\in\mathbb{N}\cup\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that for every internal node
w
in
\tau^{\prime}
we have
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(w0)[u]=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(w)[u]
. Applying this inductively from
0^{i}1
down to the down-most leaf
0^{i}10^{k}
yields
\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}10^{k})[u]=\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1)[u]
.

Nest, since
|u|\leq 2
(i.e., it is either at the root or at a child of the root) we have
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}10^{k}))\geq\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1,u))\geq\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1),\epsilon)-1
. That is, we lose at most depth
1
between
0^{i}1
and the leaf
0^{i}10^{k}
.

Recall that we are under the assumption that
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y))<\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
but we have

\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(y))+\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1))\geq\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))

Therefore, we have
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1))>\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))
, and since these are integers we have
\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1))\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))+1
.
Combining this with the observation above, we have

\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}10^{k}))\geq\mathtt{V}(\tau^{\prime}.\mathtt{forest}(0^{i}1,u))-1\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{V}(\tau.\mathtt{forest}(x))

as required.
∎

Equipped with
\cref
thm:counter example, we now have that the depth of a minimal general decomposition of an unmarked forest
f
is at least
\log(\mathtt{V}(f))
. Therefore, in order to complete our counterexample, we only need to show that there is a family of unmarked trees whose maximal complete binary subtree grows unboundedly.
The latter is easy to construct: for every
n
, take a complete binary tree of depth
n
where each left-child is labeled
a
and each right-child labeled
b
. Then, connect to every leaf a child labeled
\ell
.

Corollary 126
.

There is a morphism
\varphi
that is not
\mathcal{R}
-aligned such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is a decomposable forest
f_{n}
whose minimal decomposition depth is at least
n
.
